Linux & the Command Line Mastery

What is actually happening on the machine — the kernel boundary your code crosses on every read, the process table, the permission bits, the mount tree, the unit that supervises your service, and the namespaces that make a container a container. Plus the commands that show you each of those when one of them is lying to you.

How to use this course

  • This course is the machine your software runs on. The Backend & API Design course covers what a service promises; the Networking & HTTP course covers what crosses the wire; System Design covers how many of them fit together. This one covers the host: the layer every one of those assumes and none of them explains.
  • Everything here was run. Every transcript came out of a real terminal on a real machine, and the surprising results were kept rather than tidied — including two places where the recommended configuration turned out to do nothing at all, and the section that finds out why. Your numbers will differ; the shapes will not.
  • It assumes no Linux. It assumes you can read a little Python and are comfortable in a terminal. Section 1 starts at what a kernel is.
  • The authority is the kernel and the man pages, and where a claim is version-dependent it is named. The syllabus was derived from the LPIC-1 v5.0 objectives (exams 101 and 102), with the isolation material — which those objectives predate — taken from namespaces(7), cgroups(7), capabilities(7) and seccomp(2).
  • Each section is Concept → Mechanism → Practice → Exercises with solutions.
  • You need a Linux machine to work along: a VM, a cloud instance, or WSL2. macOS is close enough for §2–§5 and diverges completely from §8 onward.
uname -r                     # 5.x or newer; 6.x/7.x for everything in section 15
systemctl --version          # systemd 250+
lsns --version               # util-linux
bwrap --version              # bubblewrap, for the section 15 experiments
ss -V                        # iproute2

Versions every transcript in this course was produced against: Ubuntu 26.04 LTS · Linux 7.0.0-30 · systemd 259 · util-linux 2.41.3 · bubblewrap 0.11.1 · OpenSSH 10.2p1 · iproute2 6.19 · strace 6.19 · GNU findutils 4.10 · Docker 29.1.3.

One environment note that will save you a confusing hour: Ubuntu 25.10 and later ship the Rust uutils reimplementation of coreutils as the default ls, cp, date and friends, with GNU coreutils installed alongside. Behaviour is deliberately compatible and the edges are not always identical. §8.10 shows how to find out which one you are running, and it takes one command.

What you are actually learning

Not a list of commands. Four mechanisms, each of which explains a large class of behaviour, and the tools that make each one visible:

  1. The syscall boundary. Why a syscall costs, why buffering exists, why filtering syscalls is cheap security, and how strace turns "it doesn't work" into a filename and an errno.
  2. The file abstraction. Inodes against names, which explains hard links, why deleting an open file does not free space, why mv is instant within a filesystem, and why /proc can report a size of zero and still have content.
  3. The process. What it carries — identity, descriptors, environment, namespaces, cgroup, credentials — and the fact that a container is nothing more than a process whose last three were set unusually.
  4. Confinement, split three ways. What a process can see (namespaces), how much it can use (cgroups), and what it may do (capabilities, seccomp, LSMs). Three unrelated mechanisms that fail in three visibly different ways.

By the end you should be able to walk onto an unfamiliar Linux machine, work out what it is running and why it is unhappy, and leave a service on it that is supervised, logged, bounded and confined — without looking anything up.

SECTION 1

What a Linux System Is

Before any command is worth learning, four ideas have to be in place, because every tool in this course is an application of one of them. What the kernel is and where it stops. Why almost everything is presented as a file. What a process is and what it carries. And where things live on disk, which is a convention rather than a law but is followed closely enough to rely on.

Get those four right and the rest of the course is detail. Get them wrong and Linux looks like a pile of unrelated commands with inconsistent flags.

1.1 Kernel and User Space

A Linux system is two things wearing one name.

The kernel is one program, started by the bootloader, which never exits. It owns the hardware, the memory map, the process table, the filesystems and the network stack. It runs in a CPU mode that can do anything.
User space is everything else — your shell, systemd, a database, a browser. Each runs in a mode where the CPU physically forbids touching hardware, other processes' memory, or the page tables.

"Linux" strictly names only the first. The rest is the GNU tools, systemd, a package manager and a few thousand other projects, which is why uname reports the combination:

uname -a

Linux devbox 7.0.0-30-generic #30-Ubuntu SMP PREEMPT_DYNAMIC Fri Jul 31 18:22:54 UTC 2026 x86_64 GNU/Linux

Kernel name, hostname, kernel version, build details, architecture, and — at the end — the honest description of the whole: GNU/Linux.

The only way across the boundary is a system call: the program puts a number in a register and executes a syscall instruction, and the kernel takes over, validates every argument, does the work and returns. There are roughly 400 of them, and they are the entire interface. open, read, write, close, fork, execve, mmap, socket — everything a program can do to the outside world is one of these or a library function built on them.

Two consequences worth carrying from the first page:

The syscall list is the security boundary. It is why filtering it with seccomp (§15.10) is such a cheap defence, and why a kernel bug is categorically worse than an application bug — there is nothing above the kernel to contain it.

Crossing costs. A syscall is hundreds of times more expensive than a function call, which is why buffering exists, why printf does not write a byte at a time, and why a program doing a million tiny reads is slow in a way that no clever code inside the program can fix. §8.1 measures it.

1.2 Everything Is a File

The idea Unix is famous for is that wildly different things are presented through one interface: open it, read bytes, write bytes, close it.

ls -l /dev/null /proc/self/status

crw-rw-rw- 1 root root 1, 3 Aug 27 11:37 /dev/null
-r--r--r-- 1 dev  dev     0 Aug 27 11:41 /proc/self/status

Neither of those is a file in any ordinary sense. /dev/null is a device node — a connection to a driver that discards everything (§8.5). /proc/self/status reports a size of zero and yet has content, because it does not exist until you read it: the kernel generates it on demand from live data structures (§8.2).

The list of things wearing the file interface is longer than people expect:

Regular files
Bytes on a disk.
Directories
A file whose contents are a table of name → inode (§3.1).
Devices
Disks, terminals, random number generators — /dev.
Kernel state
/proc and /sys: process information, tunables, device attributes.
Pipes
A buffer between two processes (§4.4).
Sockets
Network and local connections.
Symlinks
A file whose contents are a path (§3.2).

The payoff is composability. grep does not know whether it is reading a disk file, a pipe, a terminal or a device, so the same program works with all four — which is the entire premise of §4. And the payoff for the administrator is that state you would otherwise need a special tool to inspect is cat: how much memory is free, which I/O scheduler a disk uses, what a process has open, which namespaces it is in.

The unit that makes this work is the file descriptor: a small integer, private to a process, indexing a table of open things. Descriptor 0, 1 and 2 are conventionally standard input, output and error (§4.1), and the fact that they are just numbers in a table is what lets the shell rewire them before a program starts.

The honest caveat, because it gets overstated: not everything really is a file. Signals are not, process creation is not, and the modern kernel interfaces — epoll, io_uring, bpf — are their own syscalls. "Everything is a file" is a design principle followed unusually consistently, not a law.

1.3 The Process

A process is a running program plus everything the kernel tracks about it:

Memory
Its own address space; it cannot see another process's memory.
File descriptors
The table of open things.
Identity
UID, GID and supplementary groups (§6.1) — what permission checks compare against.
Working directory
What a relative path resolves against.
Environment
A copy of the parent's variables (§2.4).
Namespaces
Which instance of each global resource it sees (§15.2).
cgroup
Which resource-accounting group it belongs to (§15.7).
Credentials
Capabilities, seccomp filter, LSM label (§15.9–15.11).

Every process except the first is created by another process, so they form a tree rooted at PID 1 — systemd on nearly every current distribution (§9.4). Nothing appears from nowhere; if a process is running, something started it, and ps -eo pid,ppid will say what.

Two properties of that tree are worth stating now because so much depends on them. A child inherits, and inheritance is one-way: environment, descriptors, working directory, identity and namespaces are copied down, and nothing a child does can change its parent. And the last four rows of that table are the whole of §15 — a container is a process whose namespaces, cgroup and credentials were set unusually at creation, and nothing more.

1.4 Distributions

The kernel is one project; a distribution is a curated assembly of it with everything else.

Debian family
Debian, Ubuntu, Linux Mint. .deb packages, apt. Ubuntu LTS releases every two years with five years of support.
Red Hat family
RHEL, Fedora, Rocky, Alma. .rpm packages, dnf. SELinux enforcing by default.
SUSE family
openSUSE, SLES. .rpm, zypper, and YaST for administration.
Arch
Rolling release, pacman, minimal defaults and excellent documentation.
Alpine
musl libc and busybox instead of glibc and GNU coreutils. A base image of about 5 MB, which is why it dominates containers — and why a binary built against glibc will not run on it.

What actually differs between them, in the order it will matter to you: the package manager and package names (§12), the init and service configuration (systemd everywhere except Alpine and a few holdouts), the default LSM (AppArmor on Ubuntu and SUSE, SELinux on Red Hat — §15.11), file locations, and the default firewall front end. What does not differ is anything in §1–§8 of this course.

grep -E '^(NAME|VERSION)=' /etc/os-release

NAME="Ubuntu"
VERSION="26.04 LTS (Resolute Raccoon)"

/etc/os-release is the machine-readable answer to "what am I on", standardised across distributions, and the right thing for a script to read rather than guessing from the presence of /etc/debian_version.

A note about this course's transcripts. Every command shown was run on Ubuntu 26.04 with kernel 7.0. Where a distribution difference matters, it is named. One deserves flagging immediately, because it can make output differ character by character: Ubuntu 25.10 and later ship the Rust uutils reimplementation of coreutils as the default ls, cp, date and friends, with GNU coreutils installed alongside. The behaviour is deliberately compatible, and edge cases in option handling and error text are not always identical. §8.10 shows how strace reveals which one you are actually running.

1.5 One Tree

There are no drive letters. Every filesystem is mounted into a single tree rooted at /, and where it appears is a choice, not a property of the device (§10.7).

ls /

bin   dev  home   lib32  libx32      media  opt   root  sbin  srv   sys  usr
boot  etc  lib    lib64  lost+found  mnt    proc  run   snap  swap.img  tmp  var

The Filesystem Hierarchy Standard gives each of those a job:

/bin /sbin
Essential commands; sbin for administrative ones. On modern systems both are symlinks into /usr.
/boot
Kernel, initramfs, bootloader. Often a separate small partition (§10.1).
/dev
Device nodes, created dynamically by udev (§8.6).
/etc
System configuration, and text files by convention. The directory to back up.
/home
User home directories.
/opt
Self-contained third-party software.
/proc /sys
Kernel interfaces, not disks (§8.2, §8.4).
/root
root's home. Not /.
/run
Runtime state — PID files, sockets. A tmpfs, empty at every boot.
/srv
Data served by this machine.
/tmp
Temporary, world-writable with the sticky bit (§6.6). Cleared periodically.
/usr
The bulk of the system: /usr/bin, /usr/lib, /usr/share. Read-only in principle.
/usr/local
Locally installed software. Never touched by the package manager (§12.6).
/var
Variable data: /var/log, /var/lib, spools, caches. The directory that fills up.

Two structural facts in that listing.

The /usr merge. /bin, /sbin and /lib are now symlinks:

ls -ld /bin /sbin /lib

lrwxrwxrwx 1 root root 7 Apr 20 08:46 /bin -> usr/bin
lrwxrwxrwx 1 root root 7 Apr 20 08:46 /lib -> usr/lib
lrwxrwxrwx 1 root root 8 Apr 20 08:46 /sbin -> usr/sbin

The original split existed because /usr might be on a separate disk mounted later in boot, so essential tools had to live outside it. The initramfs (§9.3) removed that constraint, and merging leaves one location per binary — which matters because it means /bin/sh and /usr/bin/sh are the same file, and a script's shebang can use either.

The /etc versus /var distinction is the one to internalise. /etc is configuration a human wrote and should be in version control; /var is data a program produced. Anything under /var/lib is a program's private state and is not safe to edit by hand. This distinction is what makes "back up /etc, /home and your databases" a complete answer for most machines.

Two more conventions the filesystem hierarchy relies on and that scripts should respect: a program's own data belongs under a directory named after it (/etc/myapp/, /var/lib/myapp/), and nothing outside /usr/local and /opt should be written by anything other than the package manager. Where the filesystem hierarchy is followed, a stranger can find a program's configuration, state and logs without being told; where it is not, every machine is a puzzle.

An executable text file declares its interpreter on the first line with a shebang — #!/usr/bin/env bash or #!/usr/bin/python3 — which the kernel reads at execve time and uses to launch the named interpreter with the script as its argument. The shebang is why a script needs no compilation step and why ./script.py works without typing python3; without one, the kernel refuses with Exec format error and the shell falls back to running it as sh.

Finally, ~ is not a directory — it is shell syntax for the value of HOME, expanded before any command sees it (§2.1). A program passed ~/file as a literal string will look for a directory named ~, which is why quoting it ("~/file") breaks paths in exactly one confusing way.

Exercises

Exercise 1.1 — Sort the operations. For each, say whether it crosses into the kernel (a syscall) or happens entirely in user space, and why it matters.

  1. x = y + 1 in a running program.
  2. printf("hello\n") to a terminal.
  3. printf("hello\n") with stdout redirected to a file, called in a loop 1000 times.
  4. Expanding *.txt into a list of filenames.
  5. Checking whether a file's permissions allow you to read it.
Solution

1 — User space. Arithmetic on registers. Nanoseconds, and no boundary crossing.

2 — One syscall per call, roughly. Standard output to a terminal is line-buffered, so each newline triggers a write. That is a deliberate trade: interactive output appears immediately at the cost of a syscall per line.

3 — Far fewer than 1000 syscalls. Redirected to a file, stdout becomes fully buffered (typically 4 KB), so the library accumulates output and issues one write per buffer. A thousand short lines might be two or three syscalls.

The reason this matters is the failure it causes: run a program in a terminal and it prints progress; redirect it to a file and the file stays empty for minutes. Nothing is wrong — it is buffering, and §4, Exercise 4.2 is that diagnosis in full.

4 — Entirely user space, in the shell. Globbing is done by the shell before the command runs (§3.4), using readdir syscalls to list the directory — so it touches the kernel to read the directory, but the pattern matching itself is shell code, and the command being run never sees the pattern.

5 — A syscall, and the answer is a trap. access() or stat() reports what the permissions say. But permissions can change between the check and the use, and the check uses the real UID while an actual open uses the effective one (§6.1) — so a setuid program that checks with access() and then opens is the classic time-of-check-to-time-of-use vulnerability.

The correct pattern is to attempt the operation and handle the failure, not to ask permission first. That is true at every level of this course: open and catch EACCES, rather than stat then open.

Exercise 1.2 — Where does it go? You are configuring a machine. For each item, give the directory and justify it.

  1. A configuration file for a service you wrote.
  2. The service's binary, built from source and not packaged.
  3. Its log files.
  4. Its database files.
  5. A 40 GB dataset it serves to clients.
  6. A scratch file it needs during one request.
  7. A PID file or a Unix socket it creates at startup.
Solution

1 — /etc/myservice/. Configuration a human wrote. It belongs in version control and in your backups, and /etc is the one directory where "back this up" is unambiguous.

2 — /usr/local/bin/. Locally installed software, explicitly reserved by the FHS so the package manager never touches it (§12.6). Installing into /usr/bin means the next package upgrade overwrites your binary or your binary shadows a packaged one.

3 — /var/log/myservice/ — or, better, nowhere: a service under systemd that writes to stdout gets journald's handling for free, with rotation, size limits and structured metadata already solved (§11.4). If it must be files, /var/log with a logrotate drop-in (§11.6).

4 — /var/lib/myservice/. Program-managed state that must persist. The rule is that nothing under /var/lib should be edited by hand.

5 — Its own filesystem, mounted at /srv/myservice/. /srv is for data this machine serves. A separate volume matters more than the path: 40 GB that can grow must not be able to fill / and take the whole machine down (§10, Exercise 10.4), and mounting it separately lets you size, snapshot and back it up independently.

6 — /tmp/, or $TMPDIR. Created with mktemp so the name is unpredictable — a fixed name in a world-writable directory is a symlink attack. Under systemd, PrivateTmp=yes gives the service its own /tmp in a mount namespace (§15.12), which removes the whole class of problem.

7 — /run/myservice/. Runtime state, on a tmpfs, gone at reboot — which is exactly right for a PID file, since a PID from the previous boot is meaningless. systemd's RuntimeDirectory=myservice creates and removes it with the correct ownership automatically.

The organising question behind all seven: who wrote it, and does it survive a reboot? Human-written and permanent → /etc. Program-written and permanent → /var/lib. Program-written and transient → /run or /tmp. Served to others → /srv. Executable code you installed → /usr/local.

Exercise 1.3 — What does a container inherit? Given the process model in §1.3, predict which of these a process running in a Docker container shares with the host, and which it has its own copy of: memory, file descriptors, UID numbers, the process table, the filesystem root, the kernel, environment variables, and the system clock.

Solution
Memory
Own

Every process has its own address space; nothing container-specific.

File descriptors

Own
Per-process, as always.

UID numbers

Shared
Unless a user namespace is used — and Docker does not use one by default. UID 0 in the container is UID 0 on the host.

Process table

Own
A PID namespace. The container sees its own processes numbered from 1 — but the host sees them all under their host PIDs (§15.3).

Filesystem root

Own
A mount namespace with an overlayfs root entered by pivot_root (§15.6).

Kernel

Shared
One kernel, always. This is the whole trade against a virtual machine.

Environment

Own
An ordinary per-process copy, set by the image and the run command.

System clock

Shared
A time namespace can offset the monotonic clocks, but the wall clock is deliberately excluded (§15.2), and Docker does not use one.

The three shared entries are the ones that matter, and each is a real operational consequence rather than trivia:

  • The kernel is shared, so a kernel vulnerability is reachable from inside every container, and a container cannot run a different kernel version from its host. It is also why a kernel patch requires restarting the host and does nothing for the containers until then (§12.9).
  • UID numbers are shared, so a file written as UID 1000 inside a container is owned by whoever is UID 1000 on the host, and root inside a container writing to a bind-mounted directory writes as real root. This is why bind mounts produce root-owned files in your project directory, and why rootless containers with a user namespace exist.
  • The clock is shared, so you cannot fix a container's time by setting it inside — date -s is either forbidden by CAP_SYS_TIME being dropped, or it changes the host's clock. Fix time on the host (§11.8).

The prediction method is the point, not the table. Everything a process carries in §1.3 is either per-process anyway, or namespaced when the runtime asks for it. Nothing else is isolated. Asking "which namespace covers this?" answers every question of this shape, including the ones about mechanisms that did not exist when this was written.

SECTION 2

The Shell as a Program

The shell is not a menu and not a wrapper around the kernel. It is an interpreter for a programming language whose primary data type is a list of strings and whose primary operation is starting processes. Every line you type is parsed, expanded through seven distinct stages, and only then executed — and almost every surprising thing a shell does is explained by knowing which stage did it.

That is the framing this section is built on. If you know the expansion order, quoting stops being a superstition, $(ls) in a loop stops being a mystery, and PATH problems become one command to diagnose rather than an afternoon.

2.1 What Happens When You Press Enter

1
Brace expansion
{a,b}, {1..5}. Pure text generation; the filesystem is not consulted.
2
Tilde expansion
~ → your home, ~alice → alice's home.
3
Parameter expansion
$var, ${var:-default}, and the whole family in §2.5.
4
Command substitution
$(cmd) — run it, use its output.
5
Arithmetic expansion
$(( 2 + 2 )).
6
Word splitting
The results of 3, 4 and 5 are split on IFS — space, tab and newline by default. Only unquoted results.
7
Pathname expansion
Globs are matched against the filesystem (§3.4).
8
Quote removal
The quotes that did all this work are stripped before the program sees anything.

That fixed sequence is the expansion order, and it is worth learning as a list rather than as a feeling. Every question of the form "why did my variable turn into three arguments" or "why did my quotes not stop the glob" is answered by locating the two stages involved in the expansion order and noticing which ran first.

Brace expansion runs before everything else and is purely textual: {a,b} and {1..5} generate words whether or not the resulting files exist, which is what makes cp file{,.bak} and mkdir -p src/{main,test} work. Tilde expansion is next, replacing a leading ~ with $HOME and ~alice with alice's home directory — and because tilde expansion happens before quote removal, "~/file" is a literal path containing a tilde and not your home directory at all.

Command substitution — $(cmd), or the older backticks — runs the command in a subshell and replaces itself with the output, minus trailing newlines. Command substitution is stage 4, which is precisely why its result is then subject to word splitting at stage 6 (§2.3). Arithmetic expansion, $(( )), is stage 5 and evaluates an integer expression; arithmetic expansion does not require $ on the variables inside it, so $(( count + 1 )) works.

Two properties of that list explain most shell behaviour.

The order is fixed, and stage 6 comes after stages 3 to 5. A variable's value is subject to word splitting and globbing after it has been substituted — the shell does not know or care that the text came from a variable.

The command never sees any of it. ls *.txt passes ls a list of filenames; echo $name passes echo however many words name split into. There is no way for a program to know what you typed.

2.2 Quoting Is Not Decoration

name="Ada Lovelace"
printf '[%s]\n' $name          <- unquoted, deliberately
printf '[%s]\n' "$name"

[Ada]
[Lovelace]
[Ada Lovelace]

printf repeats its format once per argument, so the output counts the arguments for us. Unquoted, one variable became two arguments; quoted, one. Nothing was quoted in the variable — the quotes at assignment time were consumed then and there. It is the quotes at use time that suppress word splitting.

The same stage-ordering point, with globbing:

files="*.txt"
printf '[%s]\n' $files         <- unquoted, deliberately
printf '[%s]\n' "$files"

[notes.txt]
[report.txt]
[*.txt]

The variable contains the four characters *.txt either way. Unquoted, stage 7 matched it against the directory and produced two filenames; quoted, it stayed literal. This is why command "$pattern" and command $pattern are genuinely different programs, and why a script that works until someone puts a space or a * in a filename is a quoting bug and not bad luck.

The asymmetry is worth stating outright: a double quote stops word splitting and globbing while still allowing substitution, and a single quote stops everything. Reach for a double quote by default, and for a single quote when the text contains $ or a backslash that something else must interpret.

Double quotes
Suppress word splitting and globbing. Allow $, backticks and \. What you want almost always.
Single quotes
Everything literal, with no exceptions — a single quote cannot even be escaped inside single quotes. For regexes, awk programs and passwords.
Backslash
Escapes one character.
$'...'
Interprets C escapes: $'\t' is a real tab, $'\n' a newline. The clean way to pass a tab to cut -d.

The rule that removes the whole problem: quote every variable expansion unless you have a specific reason not to. "$var", "$@", "${arr[@]}", "$(cmd)". The exceptions are rare and deliberate — deliberately splitting a list, or deliberately globbing.

"$@" deserves its own mention: it expands to the positional parameters as separate words, each quoted, which is the only form that passes arguments through a wrapper script correctly. "$*" joins them into one string, and bare $@ re-splits them. Wrapper scripts that break on filenames with spaces are almost always using one of the wrong two.

2.3 The Danger of Parsing Command Output

for f in $(ls q); do echo "got: [$f]"; done      <- the anti-pattern
for f in q/*;     do echo "glob: [$f]"; done     <- the correct form

got: [two]
got: [words.txt]
glob: [q/two words.txt]

The directory contains one file, two words.txt. The first loop ran twice with two broken half-names, because $(ls q) produced text that stage 6 then split on whitespace. The second ran once, correctly, because a glob produces a list of words directly and never passes through word splitting.

Never loop over $(ls). Use a glob when the files are in one directory, or find -print0 with while IFS= read -r -d '' when they are not (§3, Exercise 3.4). This is the concrete form of the general rule in §4.9: output formatted for humans is not a data structure.

2.4 Variables and the Environment

name=value          # a shell variable: this shell only
export name         # now also in the environment: inherited by children

A shell variable lives in the shell. An environment variable is copied into every process the shell starts. export moves a variable from the first category to the second, and the env command shows what is currently in the environment.

The env command has a second use worth knowing: env -i starts a program with an empty environment, which is the way to reproduce the cron problem of §9.11 on demand rather than waiting for 3 a.m. env -i /bin/bash --noprofile --norc gives you a shell with nothing inherited, and running your script there tells you in ten seconds whether it depends on something your login set up.

No spaces around =. name = value runs the command name with two arguments, and the error message will not mention the space.

The set builtin is the other half of the shell's own state. With no arguments it prints every variable and function; with options it changes the shell's behaviour — set -e, set -u and set -o pipefail are the ones §4.6 builds on, and set -x traces every command as it runs, which is the fastest way to see what a script is actually doing. set and unset are a matched pair: the set builtin turns options on and defines positional parameters, and unset removes a variable or a function entirely, which is different from setting it to the empty string (${v:-d} and ${v+alt} can tell those two apart).

Three consequences of "children get a copy" that come up constantly:

  • A child can never change its parent's environment. A script that sets a variable and exits has changed nothing in your shell. That is why activating a virtual environment or changing directory in a script requires source (§2.6), which runs it in the current shell rather than a child.
  • VAR=value command sets a variable for that one command only, without touching the shell's own. LC_ALL=C sort file is the idiomatic use (§11.9).
  • The environment is visible. /proc/PID/environ (§8.2) means secrets in environment variables are readable by the process owner and root, which is why they are better than command-line arguments (visible to everyone in ps) and worse than a file with mode 600.

The variables worth knowing:

PATH
Where to look for commands (§2.7).
HOME
Your home directory; what ~ and a bare cd use.
PWD, OLDPWD
Current and previous directory. cd - returns to the previous one.
SHELL
Your login shell from /etc/passwd — not necessarily the shell you are in.
EDITOR, VISUAL
The EDITOR variable is what git commit, crontab -e and sudoedit launch; VISUAL takes precedence where a full-screen editor is usable.
LANG, LC_*
Locale (§11.9).
TERM
Terminal type. A wrong value is why colours or arrow keys break over SSH.
PS1
The prompt string.
IFS
The word-splitting separators. Changing it globally is a way to break a script subtly.

2.5 Parameter Expansion

${...} is a small string-manipulation language, and it is worth knowing because it replaces a subprocess with a built-in operation.

p=/var/log/app.log
echo "${p##*/}  ${p%/*}  ${p%.log}  ${#p}"
echo "${UNSET:-default}  ${UNSET:+set}"

app.log  /var/log  /var/log/app  16
default
${v#pat}
Remove the shortest match from the front.
${v##pat}
Remove the longest from the front — ${p##*/} is basename.
${v%pat}
Shortest from the end — ${f%.jsx} strips an extension.
${v%%pat}
Longest from the end. ${p%/*} is dirname.
${v:-d}
Use d if unset or empty. Does not assign.
${v:=d}
Use d and assign it.
${v:?msg}
Error and exit if unset. The one-line required-argument check.
${v:+alt}
Use alt only if v is set. For conditionally adding a flag.
${#v}
Length.
${v/a/b}
Replace the first a; ${v//a/b} replaces all.
${v:2:5}
Substring from offset 2, length 5.

The patterns are globs, not regexes (§3.4). ${v%.*} strips the last dot-suffix; ${v%%.*} strips from the first dot.

${v:?message} is the argument check worth adopting:

target="${1:?usage: deploy.sh <environment>}"
echo "deploying to $target"

If $1 is missing, the shell prints the message with the script name and line number and exits non-zero. It is one line and it is better than most hand-written checks.

Arithmetic is $(( )), with integer-only semantics:

echo $(( 7 / 2 )) $(( 7 % 2 )) $(( 2 ** 10 ))

3 1 1024

Integer division truncates. For anything with a decimal point you need awk, bc or Python — a fact worth knowing before you write a percentage calculation in a monitoring script and get 0.

2.6 Startup Files: the Table That Ends the Confusion

"Why is my PATH not set in this shell?" has one answer: bash reads different files depending on how it was started, and there are two independent axes.

Interactive
Non-interactive
Login
/etc/profile, then the first of ~/.bash_profile, ~/.bash_login, ~/.profile. On exit, ~/.bash_logout.
Same as login-interactive when invoked with --login; otherwise nothing.
Non-login
/etc/bash.bashrc and ~/.bashrc.
Nothing — unless $BASH_ENV is set.

Which one you get:

  • SSH into a machine → login, interactive → ~/.bash_profile.
  • Open a new terminal tab on a desktop → non-login, interactive → ~/.bashrc.
  • su - user → login. su user → non-login.
  • A cron job or a systemd service → non-interactive, non-login → neither file is read (§9.11). This is the whole explanation for "it works in my terminal and not in cron".

Note that only the first of ~/.bash_profile, ~/.bash_login and ~/.profile that exists is read. Creating a ~/.bash_profile on a system that was using ~/.profile silently stops the latter being read at all.

Note that /etc/profile and /etc/bash.bashrc are the system-wide halves of the same pair, read before the per-user files. On Debian and Ubuntu, /etc/profile also sources every .sh file in /etc/profile.d/, which is where a package drops an environment change that should apply to every user — and the right place to put one yourself, rather than editing /etc/profile and having the next upgrade overwrite it.

su - versus su is the same distinction in miniature: su - starts a login shell for the target user, so the target's profile runs and you get their environment and PATH; plain su keeps yours. Almost every confusing "root cannot find the command" report is a plain su where su - was meant.

The convention that makes this manageable is to put everything in ~/.bashrc and have ~/.bash_profile source it:

[ -f ~/.bashrc ] && . ~/.bashrc

Then one file holds your configuration and both paths reach it. The stricter version — environment variables in ~/.profile, interactive settings like aliases and the prompt in ~/.bashrc — is more correct, because a login shell exports the environment once and every child inherits it, and it is worth doing if you use graphical applications that need to see your variables.

The source builtin (also spelled .) runs a file in the current shell, so its variables, functions and directory changes persist. Note that the source builtin is a builtin rather than a program precisely because a program could not do this — a child process cannot modify its parent. Executing it (./file) forks a child and none of them do. That single distinction is why source venv/bin/activate cannot be ./venv/bin/activate.

2.7 PATH, and What a Name Actually Runs

When you type a name with no slash, bash resolves it in a fixed order, and knowing that order is how command not found and "it ran the wrong version" get diagnosed:

1
Alias
Interactive shells only. Text substitution before anything else.
2
Function
A shell function of that name.
3
Builtin
cd, echo, export, test — implemented in the shell itself.
4
PATH
Directories searched left to right; the first match wins.

The type builtin is what reports the result of that search. type -a name shows every candidate in order, which is the command to reach for whenever a program behaves unexpectedly:

type -a echo

echo is a shell builtin
echo is /usr/bin/echo
echo is /bin/echo

Three candidates with slightly different flag handling, and the builtin always wins. Discovering that a name is a function or an alias that someone put in a shared profile — shadowing the real binary — is a five-second diagnosis with type -a and a very long one without it.

Note that the type builtin also tells you what kind of thing a name is, which is a different question from where it lives — type cd says builtin, type ll says alias, and type -a gives the whole list. That makes the type builtin the first command to run when a name behaves unexpectedly, before which and before reading any configuration file.

command -v name prints just the resolved path and is the portable existence check for scripts:

command -v jq > /dev/null || { echo "jq required" >&2; exit 1; }

Use it rather than which, which is an external program with inconsistent behaviour across systems and does not know about builtins, functions or aliases at all.

The PATH variable is a colon-separated list, searched left to right, and it is worth being able to read yours at a glance — echo "$PATH" | tr ':' '\n' puts one directory per line. Because the PATH variable is an ordinary environment variable, it is inherited by every child, which is why changing it in a script changes it for everything that script runs and for nothing else.

Two PATH rules worth internalising. Order matters: PATH=~/bin:$PATH puts your directory first (your versions win), PATH=$PATH:~/bin puts it last. And never put . in PATH — an attacker who can write a file named ls into a directory you cd into gets code execution the next time you type ls. Type ./script explicitly instead; the extra two characters are the security boundary.

Bash caches command locations, so installing a new binary earlier in PATH can leave the old one still being used — hash -r clears it, and is the answer to "I installed it, which finds it, and the shell still runs the old one".

2.8 Line Editing, History, Aliases and Functions

Everything you type at a bash prompt is handled by readline, the GNU line-editing library — which is why the same key bindings work in python, psql, gdb and every other program that links it. Learning six of them removes most of the arrow-key tapping that fills a working day.

One habit worth pairing with them: mkdir -p in a function, as below, is the small case where a shell function beats an alias — mkdir -p plus cd cannot be expressed as an alias because the argument has to appear twice.

Ctrl-a / Ctrl-e
Start and end of line.
Ctrl-w
Delete the word before the cursor. Ctrl-u deletes to the start of the line, Ctrl-k to the end.
Ctrl-y
Paste back whatever the last of those deleted — readline keeps its own kill ring.
Alt-.
Insert the last argument of the previous command. Press repeatedly to walk further back.
Ctrl-x Ctrl-e
Open the current line in $EDITOR — for the command that has grown too long to edit in place.

readline is configured in ~/.inputrc, and it has a vi mode (set editing-mode vi) for anyone who would rather have §5.7's grammar at the prompt as well. The default is emacs mode, which is where the bindings above come from. The setting worth adding whatever your preference:

set completion-ignore-case on
set show-all-if-ambiguous on

History is stored in ~/.bash_history and is worth a moment of configuration:

Ctrl-r
Reverse incremental search. The single highest-value key combination in the shell.
!!
The previous command. sudo !! is the classic.
!$
Last argument of the previous command; Alt-. does the same interactively.
history | grep
Search the list.
HISTCONTROL=ignoreboth
Skip duplicates and lines starting with a space — so a command prefixed with a space (a password, a token) is not recorded.
HISTSIZE/HISTFILESIZE
How much to keep in memory and on disk.

An alias is text substitution and cannot take arguments in the middle:

alias ll='ls -alh'
alias gs='git status'

A function can, and is what you want for anything with logic:

mkcd() {
    mkdir -p -- "$1" && cd -- "$1"
}

Note that this has to be a function: a script could not change your shell's directory (§2.4). Functions defined in ~/.bashrc are available in interactive shells; export -f makes them available to child bash processes, though that is rarely a good idea.

A shell function is defined once and behaves like a command thereafter, which makes it the right unit for anything you repeat. Unlike an alias, a shell function takes arguments, contains control flow, and has a return status; unlike a script, it runs in the current shell, so it can change your directory or your environment.

Aliases are not expanded in non-interactive shells, so an alias in ~/.bashrc does nothing in a script. Scripts should call the real commands, which is a feature — a script whose behaviour depends on the invoking user's aliases would be unreproducible.

2.9 Reading the Manual

man command is the reference, and the section numbers matter because names collide:

1
User commands. man 1 printf is the program.
2
System calls. man 2 open.
3
Library functions. man 3 printf is the C function — a different page entirely.
5
File formats. man 5 fstab, man 5 crontab.
7
Overviews and concepts. man 7 namespaces, man 7 signal.
8
Administration commands. man 8 mount.

Section 5 is the underused one. When configuring anything, man 5 <thing> is usually the authoritative description of the file's syntax, and it is where the option you are searching the internet for is documented.

A man page is divided into standard sections — NAME, SYNOPSIS, DESCRIPTION, OPTIONS, EXIT STATUS, ENVIRONMENT, FILES, EXAMPLES, SEE ALSO — and reading a man page usually means jumping straight to one of them rather than starting at the top. SEE ALSO at the bottom is the underrated one: it is a curated list of the related pages, and it is how you discover the tool you did not know to search for.

man -k pattern (equivalently apropos) searches the summaries when you do not know the name. apropos and man -k are literally the same program, and both depend on a database built by mandb, so apropos finding nothing on a freshly built container usually means the database was never generated rather than that the page is absent. man -f (whatis) gives the one-line summary. Inside man you are in less (§5.1), so /pattern searches and G jumps to the end where the examples and files usually are.

Two other sources: --help on the command itself, which is often more current and always more concise; and info for GNU tools, where the man page is sometimes a stub pointing at a much fuller info manual — info coreutils 'sort invocation' is a genuinely better document than man sort.

Exercises

Exercise 2.1 — Count the arguments. For each line, say how many arguments the command receives, and what they are. file is a variable containing my report.txt, and the current directory contains a.txt and b.txt.

rm $file
rm "$file"
rm *.txt
rm "*.txt"
rm $(echo a.txt b.txt)
touch "$file" && ls -l $file
Solution

rm $file — two: my and report.txt. Word splitting (stage 6) applies to the unquoted expansion. rm tries to delete two files that do not exist and the intended file survives — the harmless version of this bug. The dangerous version is when a file called my happens to exist.

rm "$file" — one: my report.txt. Correct.

rm *.txt — two: a.txt and b.txt, produced by pathname expansion (stage 7). Note it does not include my report.txt unless that file exists in this directory.

rm "*.txt" — one: the literal string *.txt. Quoting suppressed globbing, so rm looks for a file with an asterisk in its name and reports that it does not exist.

rm $(echo a.txt b.txt) — two. Command substitution (stage 4) produces a.txt b.txt, which stage 6 then splits on whitespace. This is the same mechanism as the $(ls) trap in §2.3, and it works here only because neither name contains a space.

touch "$file" && ls -l $file — touch gets one argument and creates my report.txt. ls -l gets two, my and report.txt, and prints an error for my and a listing for report.txt — a partially successful command, which is the version of this bug that gets shipped because the output looks nearly right.

The rule the whole exercise demonstrates: quoting is not about strings, it is about how many arguments the program receives. printf '[%s]\n' $var is the way to check, since it prints one line per argument.

Exercise 2.2 — Works in the terminal, fails in cron. A script runs perfectly by hand and fails from cron with line 4: aws: command not found. Explain precisely why, and give two fixes.

Solution

A cron job runs a non-interactive, non-login shell, which reads neither ~/.bashrc nor ~/.bash_profile (§2.6). Your interactive PATH — with ~/.local/bin, a version manager's shims, or /usr/local/bin added by one of those files — does not exist there. aws is on disk and not on cron's PATH.

Confirm it in one line rather than guessing, by making cron tell you what it has:

* * * * * env > /tmp/cron-env.txt

Compare that against env in your terminal and the difference is the whole problem. This is worth doing once in your life, because it also reveals the absence of HOME overrides, LANG and anything a version manager sets.

Fix 1 — absolute paths in the script. Unambiguous and immune to whatever the caller's environment holds:

/usr/local/bin/aws s3 sync ...

Fix 2 — set PATH explicitly at the top of the script, which is better when there are several commands:

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
export PATH=/usr/local/bin:/usr/bin:/bin

Setting it in the script rather than in the crontab means the script behaves identically however it is invoked, which is what makes it testable.

What not to do: source ~/.bashrc at the top of the script. It appears to work, and it makes the script's behaviour depend on an interactive configuration file that changes, may print things, and may exit early — most distribution ~/.bashrc files begin with a guard that returns immediately when not interactive, so it often does nothing at all.

The general principle: a script should declare what it needs rather than inherit it. That is also why a systemd unit specifies Environment= and WorkingDirectory= explicitly, and why §9.11 recommends timers over cron for anything that matters.

Exercise 2.3 — The wrong python. python3 --version reports 3.11 in one terminal and 3.13 in another on the same machine. Diagnose it systematically.

Solution 
type -a python3
echo "$PATH" | tr ':' '\n'
command -v python3
readlink -f "$(command -v python3)"

type -a first, because it enumerates every candidate in resolution order (§2.7) and covers all four possibilities at once:

  • An alias — alias python3='python3.11' in one shell's ~/.bashrc, absent in the other.
  • A shell function wrapping it, which is how some version managers work.
  • Different PATH order — a pyenv or conda shim directory prepended in one shell and not the other. This is the most common answer, and comparing the tr ':' '\n' output between the two terminals shows it immediately.
  • A symlink pointing elsewhere — readlink -f resolves the whole chain.

Why the two terminals differ is then a §2.6 question: one is a login shell (SSH, or a terminal started with --login) reading ~/.profile, and the other is non-login reading ~/.bashrc. A version manager initialised in only one of those files produces exactly this.

A fifth possibility worth checking if the first four come up empty: hash -r. Bash caches the resolved path of each command, so a shell that ran python3 before a new version was installed keeps using the old path even though type -a reports the new one. The cached entry is shown by hash with no arguments.

The durable fix is not to rely on the bare name at all. In a script, #!/usr/bin/env python3 uses the caller's PATH (good for a virtual environment, bad for reproducibility) and an absolute interpreter path pins it exactly. For a project, a virtual environment activated explicitly removes the question — the whole reason venv exists is that "which python3" should not be answered by shell configuration.

Exercise 2.4 — Read the parameter expansions. Given f=/srv/www/site/index.html.bak, give the value of each, and say which pair of them together reimplements dirname and basename.

${f##*/}
${f%/*}
${f%.*}
${f%%.*}
${f#/srv/}
${f/site/blog}
${#f}
${f:0:4}
Solution
${f##*/}
index.html.bak

Longest match of */ from the front — everything up to the last slash.

${f%/*}

/srv/www/site
Shortest match of /* from the end — the last path component removed.

${f%.*}

/srv/www/site/index.html
Shortest from the end: drops only .bak.

${f%%.*}

/srv/www/site/index
Longest from the end: drops .html.bak.

${f#/srv/}

www/site/index.html.bak
Removes a literal prefix.

${f/site/blog}

/srv/www/blog/index.html.bak
First occurrence replaced. // would replace all.

${#f}

28
Length in characters.

${f:0:4}

/srv
Substring: offset 0, length 4.

${f##*/} is basename and ${f%/*} is dirname — and they run in the shell with no fork, which matters in a loop over thousands of files.

The two caveats worth knowing before replacing the real commands everywhere: ${f%/*} on a path with no slash returns the path unchanged rather than ., and on the path /file it returns the empty string rather than /. dirname handles both correctly. For interior loops the expansion is right; for handling arbitrary user input, the command is.

Note ${f%%.*} in particular. It is a common mistake to reach for it as "strip the extension" — on archive.tar.gz it gives archive, but on a path like /srv/v1.2/app.log it strips from the first dot anywhere in the string, giving /srv/v1. Combine with ##*/ first if the value is a path, or use %.* for a single extension.

SECTION 3

Files, Links, Globbing and find

Files are the interface to everything on Linux, so the vocabulary for talking about them has to be exact. This section covers what a file actually is — which is not what most people think — how two names can be the same file, how the shell turns *.log into a list before any program sees it, and how to search a filesystem by criteria rather than by name.

The distinction that organises the whole section is between a file and a name for a file. Getting that right explains hard links, why rm can delete a file you cannot read, why a deleted file can still be consuming disk space, and why mv is instant on the same filesystem and slow across two.

3.1 What a File Is

A file is an inode: a numbered record on a filesystem holding the metadata and the pointers to the data blocks.

Type and mode
Regular, directory, symlink, device, socket, FIFO — plus the twelve permission bits (§6).
Owner and group
Numeric UID and GID.
Size
In bytes.
Timestamps
Access, modify, change — and, on modern filesystems, birth.
Link count
How many directory entries point at this inode. When it reaches zero and no process holds it open, the blocks are freed.
Block pointers
Where the data is.

The name is not in the inode. A directory is a file whose contents are a table of name → inode number pairs, and that indirection is the whole design. It means:

  • A file can have several names, all equally real.
  • Renaming is editing one directory entry and touches no data.
  • Deleting is removing a directory entry — hence unlink, the actual syscall name — and only decrements a counter.
  • Permission to delete a file comes from the directory's write bit, not the file's (§6.3).

ls -i shows the inode number, and stat shows everything:

stat a.txt | tail -4

Access: 2026-08-26 21:00:50.876209210 +0000
Modify: 2026-08-26 21:00:50.876209210 +0000
Change: 2026-08-26 21:00:50.876209210 +0000
 Birth: 2026-08-26 21:00:50.876209210 +0000

The three classic timestamps are worth keeping straight, because they answer different questions:

Access
atime
Last read. Updated lazily (relatime, §10.7) because updating it on every read would be a write per read.
Modify
mtime
Last time the contents changed. What ls -l shows and what find -mtime tests.
Change
ctime
Last time the inode changed — contents, permissions, ownership or link count. Cannot be set by a user, which makes it the one an intruder cannot forge with touch.

stat -c formats one field at a time (%n name, %s size, %i inode, %h link count, %U owner, %a octal mode), which makes it the right tool in scripts where parsing ls would be wrong (§4.9).

3.2 Two Kinds of Link

echo "original" > real.txt
ln real.txt hard.txt
ln -s real.txt soft.txt
ls -li

5685 -rw-rw-r-- 2 dev dev 9 Aug 26 21:00 hard.txt
5685 -rw-rw-r-- 2 dev dev 9 Aug 26 21:00 real.txt
5686 lrwxrwxrwx 1 dev dev 8 Aug 26 21:00 soft.txt -> real.txt

Read the first column. real.txt and hard.txt have the same inode number — 5685 — and a link count of 2. They are not a file and a copy; they are one file with two names, and neither is the original. soft.txt is a different inode entirely (5686), of type l, whose contents are the eight characters real.txt.

stat -c '%n links=%h inode=%i size=%s' real.txt hard.txt soft.txt

real.txt links=2 inode=5685 size=9
hard.txt links=2 inode=5685 size=9
soft.txt links=1 inode=5686 size=8

The symlink's size is 8 bytes — the length of the path it stores. Now delete the name the symlink points at:

rm real.txt
cat hard.txt
cat soft.txt

original
cat: soft.txt: No such file or directory

The hard link still works; the link count simply dropped to 1, and the data was never touched. The symlink is now dangling, because it stores a path and that path no longer resolves. ls -l soft.txt still lists it happily — a broken symlink is a perfectly valid file whose contents happen to name something absent.

Hard link
Symbolic link
Points to
An inode
A path, as text
Crosses filesystems
No
Yes
Links to a directory
No (except . and ..)
Yes
Survives target deletion
Yes — it is the file
No — dangles
Survives target rename
Yes
No
Own permissions
Shared (one inode)
Ignored; the target's apply

Hard links are forbidden across filesystems because an inode number is only meaningful within one filesystem, and forbidden on directories because a cycle would make the tree a graph that no tool could walk safely.

Which to use: symlinks, almost always. They are visible, they can cross filesystems, they can point at directories, and ls -l shows what they mean. Hard links have two genuine uses — deduplicating identical files (this is how rsync --link-dest and Time Machine-style incremental backups store unchanged files for free), and making a file survive the removal of its original name.

Two traps. A relative symlink is resolved relative to the link's own directory, not to your working directory, so ln -s ../config/app.conf behaves differently depending on where the link lives. And cp follows symlinks by default while cp -a (or -d) preserves them — copying a tree without -a silently turns every symlink into a full copy of its target, which is how a 200 MB directory becomes 4 GB.

3.3 Navigating and Manipulating

ls -l
Long listing. Add -a for hidden files, -h for human sizes, -t to sort by time, -S by size, -r reversed, -i for inode numbers.
cp -r
Copy a tree. -a is the one to use — recursive and preserving symlinks, times, ownership and mode.
mv -i
Move or rename. -i prompts before overwriting, -n refuses silently.
rm -rf
Recursive and forced. The command that needs a pause before Enter.
mkdir -p
Create parents as needed, and do not complain if it already exists. Idempotent, which is why scripts always use it.
rmdir
Remove an empty directory, and fail otherwise. rmdir -p removes a chain of now-empty parents.
touch
Create an empty file, or update timestamps on an existing one. -d sets a specific time.
file
Identify content by inspecting it, not by extension.

Two flags earn a mention of their own because their absence is the usual regret. cp -r is the recursive copy, and cp -a is cp -r plus the preservation of everything a plain cp -r drops — timestamps, ownership, mode and symlinks. And mv -i prompts before overwriting; mv -i in an interactive alias plus mv -n (refuse silently) in scripts is the pair that stops a rename from quietly destroying a file that was already there.

mv within one filesystem is instant — it rewrites directory entries and moves no data. Across filesystems it is a copy followed by a delete, so it takes time proportional to size and is not atomic: an interruption leaves a partial copy. This is why "move the file into place when it is complete" only guarantees atomicity within a single filesystem, and why a staging directory should live on the same filesystem as its destination (§4, Exercise 4.1).

Two habits about rm -rf that are worth adopting before you need them:

  • Never build the path with an unquoted variable. rm -rf "$dir/" when dir is empty becomes rm -rf /. Modern rm has a --preserve-root default that catches exactly /, and nothing catches rm -rf /home/. Check the variable is non-empty first.
  • Put the path first and the flags second while you are typing, or better, run ls on the exact expression first and then edit the line to replace ls with rm -rf. The shell's history editing makes this nearly free, and it means the thing you delete is the thing you looked at.

rmdir refusing is a feature, not an obstacle. rm -r deletes whatever is inside without asking; rmdir fails if the directory is not empty, so it is the safe way to clean up a directory you believe is empty. When it fails, the directory had something in it and you have just learned that before destroying it.

The file command is worth using more than people do:

file report.pdf

The file command reads magic numbers rather than trusting the extension, which is how you discover that the "PDF" a browser downloaded is an HTML error page, or that a file is data because a transfer truncated it. file -b drops the filename from the output for scripting, and file --mime-type gives the type in the form a web server would send.

dd is the low-level copy, and its syntax predates Unix conventions: dd if=INPUT of=OUTPUT bs=SIZE count=N. dd if=/dev/zero of=test.img bs=1M count=100 creates a 100 MB file of zeroes; dd if=/dev/sda of=/dev/sdb bs=4M status=progress clones a disk block for block. Two warnings that have to be given together: dd will happily write over a mounted filesystem or the wrong disk with no confirmation — which is why it has an unofficial expansion as disk destroyer — and bs= matters enormously for speed, since the default 512 bytes means one syscall per sector. Check of= twice, and prefer cp, tar or rsync for anything that is not a raw device.

3.4 Globbing Happens in the Shell

This is the most consequential misunderstanding in this section: *.txt is expanded by the shell, before the command runs. rm *.txt does not pass *.txt to rm; the shell replaces it with every matching filename and rm receives a list. Programs never see the pattern.

echo *.txt
echo *

a.txt b.txt hard.txt soft.txt
a.txt b.txt c.log d1 hard.txt soft.txt

Note that * did not match .hidden. A leading dot is excluded from globs by design, which is why dotfiles are "hidden" — there is no hidden attribute anywhere, just a convention the shell honours.

*
Any string, including empty. Does not cross /, and does not match a leading dot.
?
Exactly one character.
[abc]
One of the listed characters; [a-z] a range; [!abc] negated.
**
Any depth of directories — only with shopt -s globstar. Without it, it behaves as a plain *.

Each of those metacharacters is a wildcard, and the term is worth keeping distinct from regular expression: a wildcard pattern is matched against a whole filename by the shell, while a regular expression is matched against a substring of a line by a program. The wildcard set is much smaller and there is no alternation, no quantifier and no capture.

Globs are not regular expressions. * means "any string" here and "zero or more of the previous item" in a regex (§5.3); . is an ordinary character in a glob and "any character" in a regex. The two syntaxes look alike and mean different things, and mixing them up produces patterns that match nothing without erroring.

Brace expansion is not globbing and happens earlier:

echo file{1..3}.txt
echo {a,b}{1,2}

file1.txt file2.txt file3.txt
a1 a2 b1 b2

The crucial difference is that braces generate text whether or not the files exist, which makes cp config.yml{,.bak} a working idiom for "copy to the same name plus a suffix" and mkdir -p src/{main,test}/{java,resources} a one-line tree.

The behaviour that surprises people is what happens when a glob matches nothing:

echo *.nope

*.nope

Bash passes the pattern through unchanged. So rm *.nope calls rm with the literal argument *.nope and produces a confusing error, and worse, for f in *.log runs once with f set to the literal *.log when the directory is empty — a loop body that then operates on a file that does not exist. shopt -s nullglob makes a non-matching glob expand to nothing instead, and failglob makes it an error. For a script, nullglob is nearly always the behaviour you meant.

Quoting disables globbing entirely, which is what you want when a program does its own pattern matching: find . -name "*.log" passes *.log to find, while find . -name *.log lets the shell expand it first and gives find a list of filenames it will misinterpret as arguments. Quote the pattern argument to find and to grep — always.

3.5 find

find walks a tree and applies tests. Its syntax is unlike anything else because it is a small expression language: find WHERE TESTS ACTIONS.

The examples below run against this tree, and use GNU findutils — worth stating because find's time and -printf options vary between implementations:

find . | sort

.
./.env
./.git
./logs
./logs/app.log
./logs/old.log
./src
./src/app.py
./src/util.py
-name '*.py'
Match the basename. -iname for case-insensitive.
-path './src/*'
Match the whole path.
-type f d l
Regular file, directory, symlink. Also b c p s.
-size +100M
Bigger than. -100k smaller than. Bare number means 512-byte blocks — nearly always a mistake.
-mtime +30
Modified more than 30 days ago; -mmin -60 in the last hour.
-newermt '2026-01-01'
Modified after a specific time. Clearer than day arithmetic.
-user alice -perm -4000
By owner; by permission bits (the setuid audit of §15.9).
-maxdepth 1
Limit recursion. Must come before other tests or find warns.
-empty
Empty files and directories.

Tests combine with -a (implicit), -o, ! and parentheses (escaped from the shell, \( \)):

find . -type f -mtime +30

./logs/old.log

-prune is the one worth learning properly, because it is how you skip a directory rather than merely filtering its results out:

find . -name .git -prune -o -type f -print | sort

./.env
./logs/app.log
./logs/old.log
./src/app.py
./src/util.py

Read it as: if the name is .git, prune (do not descend, and do not print, because -prune is true and the -o short-circuits); otherwise if it is a regular file, print. The explicit -print is required — adding any action turns off the implicit one, so without it the -prune branch would print the pruned directories. -prune also saves the work of walking a huge node_modules or .git, which on a large tree is the difference between one second and thirty.

Actions do something with each result: -print (default), -print0 (NUL-separated, for xargs -0, §4.7), -delete, -ls, and -exec:

find . -type f -printf '%TY-%Tm-%Td %6s %p\n' | sort

2026-07-17      8 ./logs/old.log
2026-08-26     12 ./src/app.py
2026-08-26     14 ./src/util.py
2026-08-26     18 ./logs/app.log
2026-08-26      4 ./.env

-printf is GNU-specific and enormously useful: it gives you exactly the fields you want, in a parseable form, without going near ls (§4.9).

-exec cmd {} \; runs the command once per file; -exec cmd {} + batches as many arguments as fit, which is much faster. Prefer -exec … + or -delete over piping to xargs, because they cannot be confused by whitespace in filenames at all.

Two smaller search tools complete the set. whereis looks for the binary, the source and the manual page of a command in a fixed list of standard locations — whereis mount reports /usr/bin/mount and man 8 mount in one line — and it is faster than find because it does not walk anything. It is limited to those standard locations, so whereis finding nothing does not mean the program is absent, only that it is not where packages put things.

And find -type is the test you will use in almost every invocation: -type f for regular files, -type d for directories, -type l for symlinks. Leaving find -type off is the reason a -exec rm catches directories, and the reason a size search returns the directories themselves alongside their contents.

Finally, locate is the different tool for the different question. It searches a prebuilt database (refreshed by updatedb, usually from a timer), so it answers "where is a file called X" across the whole filesystem in milliseconds — and it is stale by up to a day and knows nothing about size, time or ownership. Use locate to find a known name fast; use find when the criteria matter or the answer must be current. updatedb is the command that builds that database, and it is normally run from a timer overnight; running updatedb by hand is what you do after installing something you want locate to know about immediately, and /etc/updatedb.conf is where directories are excluded from it — which is why locate never finds anything under a network mount.

Exercises

Exercise 3.1 — Read the listing. Explain what each line tells you, and what happens to each file if rm data.csv is run.

2311 -rw-r--r-- 3 alice staff  4096 Aug 26 10:00 data.csv
2311 -rw-r--r-- 3 alice staff  4096 Aug 26 10:00 backup/data.csv
2311 -rw-r--r-- 3 alice staff  4096 Aug 26 10:00 archive/2026/data.csv
2544 lrwxrwxrwx 1 alice staff    17 Aug 26 10:05 current.csv -> archive/2026/data.csv
2545 drwxr-xr-x 2 alice staff  4096 Aug 26 10:05 backup
Solution

Lines 1–3 are one file with three names. Same inode (2311), link count 3 — three hard links to a single 4096-byte file. The data exists once on disk; du counts it once, and any of the three names reads and writes the same bytes.

Line 4 is a symlink: a different inode (2544), type l, and a size of 17 — exactly the length of archive/2026/data.csv. Its rwxrwxrwx mode is meaningless; a symlink's permissions are never consulted, only the target's.

Line 5 is a directory, type d, with a link count of 2 — the entry in its parent plus its own .. A directory's link count is 2 plus the number of subdirectories it contains, which is how find used to optimise tree walks.

After rm data.csv:

  • The directory entry data.csv is removed and the link count drops to 2. Nothing else changes.
  • backup/data.csv and archive/2026/data.csv still work and still hold the data. There was never an "original".
  • current.csv still works, because it points at archive/2026/data.csv, which still exists.
  • No disk space is freed.

To actually delete the data, all three names must be removed — and current.csv would then dangle, still listed by ls and failing on cat. To find the other names first: find /mount/point -inum 2311, restricted to one filesystem because that is the only place the inode number means anything.

Exercise 3.2 — The loop that ran once. A cleanup script does this, and on a directory with no .tmp files it reports "removing *.tmp" and then an error:

for f in *.tmp; do
    echo "removing $f"
    rm "$f"
done

Explain and give two fixes.

Solution

A glob that matches nothing is passed through literally (§3.4). With no .tmp files, *.tmp is not replaced, so the loop runs exactly once with f set to the four characters *.tmp — hence the message, and then rm fails because no file has that name.

The quoting is correct, incidentally, and that is what saves this script: rm "$f" passes the literal *.tmp as one argument and fails harmlessly. Unquoted rm $f would have been re-globbed by rm's caller and could have matched something — the same bug with a much worse ending.

Fix 1 — nullglob, which makes a non-matching glob expand to nothing so the loop body never runs:

shopt -s nullglob
for f in *.tmp; do
    rm -- "$f"
done

Fix 2 — guard inside the loop, which is portable to sh where shopt does not exist:

for f in *.tmp; do
    [ -e "$f" ] || continue
    rm -- "$f"
done

[ -e "$f" ] is false for the unexpanded pattern, so the loop skips it. This also handles the related case of a dangling symlink, where -e is false and -L is true.

Or avoid the loop entirely, which is the best answer when there is nothing to do per file:

find . -maxdepth 1 -name '*.tmp' -type f -delete

find prints nothing and exits 0 when there are no matches, handles any filename, and needs no shell options.

One more detail worth adopting: the -- before "$f". It marks the end of options, so a file literally named -rf is treated as a filename rather than as flags. Every command that takes filenames from a variable should have it.

Exercise 3.3 — Find the space. A 500 GB partition is 95% full. Find the ten largest files, excluding anything under /proc, /sys and any other mounted filesystem, and report their sizes and modification dates.

Solution 
find / -xdev -type f -printf '%s %TY-%Tm-%Td %p\n' 2>/dev/null | sort -rn | head -10

-xdev is the key flag. It stops find from descending into other mounted filesystems, which excludes /proc, /sys, /dev, any network mount and any other disk in one option — and it is correct where a list of -prune clauses is merely long. On a machine with a hung NFS mount it also avoids blocking forever (§7, Exercise 7.1).

-printf '%s ...' puts the size first as a plain number, so sort -rn orders it correctly. 2>/dev/null discards the permission-denied noise from directories you cannot read, which would otherwise bury the results.

Human-readable sizes, if you want them:

find / -xdev -type f -printf '%s\t%p\n' 2>/dev/null | sort -rn | head -10 | numfmt --to=iec --field=1

For the complementary question — where is the space by directory — use du:

du -xh --max-depth=2 / 2>/dev/null | sort -h | tail -20

-x is du's equivalent of -xdev, -h gives human sizes and sort -h orders them correctly (a plain sort -n puts 9.9G after 500M). Large directories often matter more than large files — ten million small session files do not appear in a top-ten-by-size list at all.

And check the two cases where du and df disagree (§10.9) before concluding anything: a deleted-but-open file (lsof +L1) and a filesystem mounted over a non-empty directory. If du totals far less than df reports used, the largest files are not where you are looking.

Exercise 3.4 — Safe bulk rename. Every file under src/ named *.jsx must become *.tsx, including in subdirectories, and some paths contain spaces. Give the procedure with a dry run.

Solution

Preview first, always:

find src -type f -name '*.jsx' -print0 | while IFS= read -r -d '' f; do
    printf '%s -> %s\n' "$f" "${f%.jsx}.tsx"
done

Then run it:

find src -type f -name '*.jsx' -print0 | while IFS= read -r -d '' f; do
    mv -n -- "$f" "${f%.jsx}.tsx"
done

Every part of that loop is doing work:

  • -print0 with read -d '' — NUL-separated, the only separator that cannot appear in a filename. This is what makes spaces, newlines and quotes safe (§4.7).
  • IFS= on the read prevents leading and trailing whitespace being stripped from the filename.
  • -r stops read interpreting backslashes.
  • ${f%.jsx} is parameter expansion removing the shortest matching suffix — no sed, no subprocess, and it cannot accidentally match .jsx in the middle of a path.
  • mv -n refuses to overwrite an existing file rather than prompting, which matters in a non-interactive loop where a prompt would either hang or be auto-answered.
  • -- ends option parsing, so a file named -x.jsx is a filename.

The rename alternative, if the rename package is installed (it is not present by default on a minimal Ubuntu, which is why this is shown as a form rather than a transcript):

find src -type f -name '*.jsx' -print0 | xargs -0 rename -n 's/\.jsx$/.tsx/'

-n is its dry-run flag. Note there are two different programs called rename — the Perl one and the util-linux one, with completely different syntax — so rename --version before trusting a recipe. The loop above works everywhere and has no such ambiguity.

And if this is a git repository, git mv instead of mv keeps the rename tracked in one step, which makes the resulting diff a rename rather than a delete plus an add.

SECTION 4

Streams, Pipes, Redirection and Exit Status

The idea that makes the command line a programming environment rather than a menu is this: every program starts life with three connections it did not create, it does not know what is on the other end of them, and the shell decides. A program that reads standard input and writes standard output can be fed from a file, from a keyboard, from a network socket or from another program, and can send its results anywhere, without a single line of it changing.

This section is about those connections — how to redirect them, how to join programs with them, and how to know whether what you ran actually worked. The last part matters more than it sounds: a pipeline that fails silently in the middle is the most common way a shell script destroys something.

4.1 Three Streams

Every process starts with three open file descriptors (§8.2):

0
stdin
Input. The terminal by default.
1
stdout
Results. The data the program was asked to produce.
2
stderr
Diagnostics. Errors, warnings and progress — anything that is about the work rather than the work itself.

The separation of 1 and 2 is the design decision that makes pipelines possible. If errors went to stdout they would be piped into the next program as data; because they go to stderr, they reach your terminal even when stdout is redirected. That is why a long-running command with output going to a file still shows you its errors immediately.

The corollary matters when you write scripts: put results on stdout and everything else on stderr. A progress message on stdout corrupts whatever consumes your output, and it is why echo "Processing..." in a function whose output is captured by $(...) produces the classic bug where the progress line ends up inside a variable.

4.2 Redirection

> file
stdout to file, truncating it.
>> file
stdout to file, appending.
2> file
stderr to file.
< file
stdin from file.
&> file
Both stdout and stderr (bash-specific shorthand).
2>&1
Make stderr go wherever stdout is currently going.
2>/dev/null
Discard errors.
<> file
Open for both reading and writing.

2>&1 is the one that must be understood rather than memorised, because redirections are applied left to right and it copies the destination stdout has at that moment.

ls /nonexistent > out1.txt 2>&1
cat out1.txt

ls: cannot access '/nonexistent': No such file or directory

Stdout is pointed at the file first, then stderr is pointed at the same place. Both land in the file. Reverse the two:

ls /nonexistent 2>&1 > out2.txt          <- counter-example; the two are swapped

That run prints ls: cannot access '/nonexistent': No such file or directory on the terminal, and leaves out2.txt empty. Stderr was pointed at the terminal (where stdout was at that moment), and then stdout was moved to the file — leaving stderr behind. The redirection did exactly what it says; the mental model of "combine them" is what is wrong.

&> file avoids the whole question in bash, and >> file 2>&1 is the portable append form.

Two related notes. > truncates the target before the command runs, which is why sort file > file produces an empty file — the shell has already emptied it before sort opens it. And set -o noclobber makes > refuse to overwrite an existing file, with >| as the explicit override. noclobber is a reasonable safety net in an interactive shell and a poor one in a script, because a script that depends on noclobber being set behaves differently for every user who has not set it.

4.3 Here Documents and Here Strings

A here document feeds inline text to a command's stdin:

cat <<EOF
expanded: $HOME
EOF

expanded: /home/dev

Variables, command substitution and backslash escapes are all processed — usually what you want for generating a configuration file. Quoting the delimiter turns all of that off:

cat <<'EOF'
literal: $HOME
EOF

literal: $HOME

That distinction is the whole of here-document safety. A script that writes another script, or a sed program, or anything containing $, needs the quoted form — otherwise the outer shell expands variables that were meant for the inner one, usually to nothing.

<<-EOF (with the dash) strips leading tabs, so a heredoc inside an indented block can be indented too. Note tabs specifically, not spaces, which is a persistent annoyance.

A here string is the one-line version: grep foo <<< "$variable" feeds the string to stdin without a temporary file or an echo pipeline.

4.4 Pipes

| connects one program's stdout to the next program's stdin through a kernel buffer. The two run concurrently, not one after the other — which is why tail -f app.log | grep ERROR produces output as lines arrive rather than waiting for the file to end, and why a pipeline can process a stream larger than memory.

The buffer is finite (64 KB by default). When it fills, the writer blocks until the reader consumes; when it empties, the reader blocks until the writer produces. That back-pressure is automatic and is why find / | head -5 does not enumerate the whole filesystem.

The tee command splits a stream — writing to a file and passing it on. It is named after a plumbing T-piece, and it is the answer whenever you want to keep a copy of something that is on its way somewhere else:

printf 'a\nb\nc\n' | tee copy.txt | wc -l
cat copy.txt

3
a
b
c

The tee command takes -a to append rather than truncate, and tee file1 file2 writes to several files at once. The tee command idiom worth knowing is command | sudo tee /etc/somefile > /dev/null, which is how you write to a root-owned file from an unprivileged pipeline — sudo command > /etc/file does not work, because the shell performs the redirection before sudo runs, and the shell is not root.

Process substitution gives a command's output a filename, so tools that demand files can be fed pipelines:

diff <(printf "x\ny\n") <(printf "x\nz\n")

2c2
< y
---
> z

Process substitution is worth distinguishing from command substitution: $(cmd) gives you the command's output as text, while process substitution gives you a filename that yields that output when read. <(...) becomes something like /dev/fd/63. This is how you diff two remote directory listings, compare sorted outputs, or feed two streams into one program — with no temporary files to clean up. >(...) is the output direction, used with tee >(gzip > log.gz).

4.5 Exit Status

Every process returns a number: 0 is success, anything else is failure. It is backwards from most languages' boolean conventions for a good reason — there is one way to succeed and many ways to fail, so non-zero values can distinguish them.

grep zzz /etc/hostname; echo "grep nomatch: $?"
grep zzz /nope 2>/dev/null; echo "grep error: $?"

grep nomatch: 1
grep error: 2

grep distinguishes three outcomes: 0 found something, 1 found nothing, 2 an actual error. That distinction is load-bearing — a script that treats "no match" and "the file does not exist" the same way will report a clean scan on a machine where the log is missing.

The conventions worth knowing:

0
Success.
1
General failure, or "no results".
2
Misuse — bad arguments, missing file.
126
Found but not executable.
127
Command not found. Instantly recognisable, and usually a PATH problem (§2.7).
128+N
Killed by signal N. 137 = 128+9 = SIGKILL (§15.8), 143 = SIGTERM, 130 = Ctrl-C.

$? holds the last status and is overwritten by the next command — including by an echo. Capture it immediately if you need it twice.

Chaining operators read the status directly:
