mkdir -p /srv/app && cd /srv/app
command -v jq > /dev/null || { echo "jq required" >&2; exit 1; }

&& runs the right side only on success, || only on failure, and ; runs unconditionally. The second line is the standard dependency check, and note the >&2 on the message — a diagnostic belongs on stderr (§4.1).

4.6 Exit Status in a Pipeline

This is the part that silently breaks scripts.

false | true; echo "no pipefail: $?"

no pipefail: 0

A pipeline's exit status is the status of the last command only. false failed; the pipeline reports success. In a real script that is pg_dump | gzip > backup.gz reporting success when pg_dump died, leaving a valid gzip file containing an error message — the backup that is checked for eighteen months and turns out to be empty.

Two fixes. set -o pipefail makes the pipeline return the rightmost non-zero status:

set -o pipefail; false | true; echo "pipefail: $?"

pipefail: 1

Or read the whole array:

false | true | true; echo "${PIPESTATUS[@]}"

1 0 0

PIPESTATUS holds every stage's status, in order, and — like $? — is replaced by the next command, so copy it before using it.

The standard opening for any script that does something consequential:

set -euo pipefail

-e exit on any unhandled failure, -u error on an unset variable, -o pipefail as above. Know its limits rather than trusting it blindly: -e does not trigger inside a command that is part of a condition (if cmd; then), on the left of && or ||, or — in some shells — inside a function called in a conditional context. It is a large improvement and not a guarantee, and || exit 1 on the operations that must not fail is still worth writing.

SIGPIPE is the other pipeline subtlety:

yes | head -2; echo "head exit: $?"

y
y
head exit: 0

yes produces output forever. head reads two lines and exits, closing its end of the pipe; the next write from yes gets SIGPIPE, whose default action is death (§7.4). Nothing hangs and nothing is reported, because the pipeline's status is head's, which is 0. This is normal and desirable — but with pipefail set, a producer killed by SIGPIPE returns 141 (128+13) and can fail a script that did exactly the right thing. That is the one case where pipefail needs a deliberate exception.

4.7 xargs

A pipe connects stdout to stdin. Many programs — rm, mkdir, chmod — take their input as arguments instead, and xargs bridges the two.

printf 'one\ntwo\nthree\n' | xargs -n1 echo "item:"

item: one
item: two
item: three

By default xargs packs as many arguments as fit into each invocation, which is what makes find . -name '*.log' | xargs rm fast: one rm with a thousand arguments rather than a thousand rm processes. -n1 forces one per invocation, and -P4 runs four in parallel, which turns xargs into a very serviceable parallel executor.

The default word-splitting is dangerous, because it splits on whitespace — so a filename containing a space becomes two arguments. The fix is NUL separation, which is the one byte that cannot appear in a filename:

find wn -type f -print0 | xargs -0 -n1 basename

c'd.txt
a b.txt

a b.txt survived as one argument, and so did the one containing a quote, which unquoted xargs would also have mangled. Use -print0 with -0 every time, or use find -exec and skip xargs altogether:

find . -name '*.log' -mtime +30 -exec rm {} +

The + batches arguments like xargs does; a \; terminator runs the command once per file. -exec … + is safe by construction, needs no pipe, and is the form to prefer for anything destructive.

-I{} puts the argument in a chosen position rather than at the end — xargs -I{} mv {} /archive/{}.bak — at the cost of forcing one invocation per item.

4.8 Named Pipes and Extra Descriptors

Two mechanisms complete the picture, and both appear in real scripts more often than people expect.

A named pipe (FIFO) is a pipe with a filename, created by mkfifo. Two unrelated processes — different terminals, different sessions — can connect through it, and it lets a long-running producer stream into a consumer started later. Opening one blocks until both ends are attached, which is a common source of confusion when a script hangs on what looks like an ordinary file.

Descriptors above 2 let a script keep several outputs open at once:

exec 3> three.txt
echo "to fd3" >&3
exec 3>&-
cat three.txt

to fd3

exec 3> file opens descriptor 3 for the rest of the script — not for a single command — and exec 3>&- closes it. The pattern that makes this worth knowing: save the original stdout before redirecting, so a script can log everything to a file while still being able to write to the user's terminal.

exec 3>&1
exec 1> /var/log/myjob.log 2>&1
echo "this goes to the log"
echo "this reaches the terminal" >&3

4.9 The Rule About Parsing Output

Composability has a limit, and it is worth stating plainly because ignoring it causes a specific and recurring class of bug: do not parse the output of tools designed for humans.

ls is the canonical example. Its output changes with terminal width, it quotes and escapes unusual filenames, and it sorts by locale. ls -l | awk '{print $9}' breaks on any filename with a space, and for f in $(ls) breaks on the same. The alternatives are a glob (for f in *), find -print0, or stat for specific fields.

The same applies to ps (column widths truncate), df (long device names wrap onto a second line), and docker ps (which says so in its own output). Where a machine-readable form exists, use it: ps -o pid=,comm= with explicit fields, df --output=source,pcent, ip -j for JSON, systemctl show -p for one property, jq for anything that is already JSON.

The general test: if the output has aligned columns for reading, there is almost always a flag that produces it without them.

Exercises

Exercise 4.1 — The backup that was not. A nightly job has been reporting success for a year:

pg_dump mydb | gzip > /backups/db-$(date +%F).sql.gz
if [ $? -eq 0 ]; then echo "backup OK" | logger -t backup; fi

The files exist and are about 20 bytes each. Explain every defect and give the corrected script.

Solution

Defect 1 — the pipeline's status is gzip's. pg_dump fails (bad credentials, missing database, disk full), writes nothing to stdout and an error to stderr; gzip compresses an empty stream perfectly successfully and exits 0. The pipeline reports success. The 20-byte files are the gzip header and footer around nothing — which is the signature of exactly this bug, and the reason "the backups exist" was mistaken for "the backups work".

Defect 2 — no set -euo pipefail. With pipefail, the pipeline would have returned pg_dump's status.

Defect 3 — the redirection truncates before pg_dump runs. Even a failing run destroys any file of that name, so a retry cannot fall back to what was there.

Defect 4 — nothing verifies the result. A backup that has never been restored is a hypothesis.

Defect 5 — $? after an if is fragile. It works here, but any command inserted between the pipeline and the test — a echo, a date — silently changes what is being checked. Test the command directly.

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

dest="/backups/db-$(date +%F).sql.gz"
tmp="$(mktemp "${dest}.XXXXXX")"
trap 'rm -f "$tmp"' EXIT

if ! pg_dump mydb | gzip > "$tmp"; then
    logger -t backup -p daemon.err "pg_dump failed for mydb"
    exit 1
fi

if ! gzip -t "$tmp"; then
    logger -t backup -p daemon.err "backup archive is corrupt"
    exit 1
fi

size=$(stat -c %s "$tmp")
if [ "$size" -lt 1000000 ]; then
    logger -t backup -p daemon.err "backup suspiciously small: ${size} bytes"
    exit 1
fi

mv "$tmp" "$dest"
logger -t backup -p daemon.info "backup OK: $dest (${size} bytes)"

The shape is what matters: write to a temporary file, verify it, then move it into place atomically. The destination only ever contains a backup that passed its checks, mv within one filesystem is atomic so there is no partial file, and the trap cleans up on any exit path. The size floor catches the empty-dump case even if every status check somehow passes, and logger puts the result in the journal where §11.4 can find it.

Exercise 4.2 — Nothing in the log. A command is run as ./deploy.sh > deploy.log 2>&1 & and the log stays empty for twenty minutes, though the script clearly prints progress. Give two possible causes and how to distinguish them.

Solution

Cause 1 — output buffering. The C library buffers stdout differently depending on what it is connected to: line-buffered to a terminal, fully buffered (4–8 KB) to a file or pipe. Redirecting to a file switches the mode, so nothing appears until 4 KB has accumulated or the program exits. The script is working perfectly and invisibly.

Cause 2 — the script is genuinely stuck, most likely blocked on stdin. A background job that tries to read from the terminal is sent SIGTTIN and stopped, and a sudo password prompt or an interactive tool inside the script does exactly that.

Distinguish them:

jobs -l
ps -o pid,stat,wchan,comm -p "$PID"
ls -l /proc/"$PID"/fd
cat /proc/"$PID"/wchan

State T in ps (§7.3) means stopped — cause 2, and jobs will show Stopped (tty input). State S or R with the script still making syscalls means it is running — cause 1. strace -p "$PID" settles it in one command: an active program shows a stream of calls, a blocked one shows a single read(0, ...) that never returns.

Fixes for cause 1:

stdbuf -oL ./deploy.sh > deploy.log 2>&1 &

stdbuf -oL forces line buffering. script -qec './deploy.sh' deploy.log is the heavier alternative that gives the program a pseudo-terminal, so it line-buffers of its own accord — useful when a program also changes its output format based on whether it sees a terminal. Inside a script you control, flush explicitly (print(..., flush=True), $| = 1).

Fix for cause 2: run it under tmux (§7.8) so there is a real terminal to interact with, and remove the interactive step — sudo -n, a credential file, a non-interactive flag.

The habit that avoids both: long jobs belong in tmux, not in &. You keep the terminal, you keep the scrollback, and a prompt for input is something you can answer instead of a mystery.

Exercise 4.3 — Delete the old logs. Write a command to delete every .log file under /var/log/myapp not modified in 30 days. The directory contains filenames with spaces. Give a safe command, and explain what is wrong with three tempting alternatives.

Solution 
find /var/log/myapp -type f -name '*.log' -mtime +30 -print
find /var/log/myapp -type f -name '*.log' -mtime +30 -delete

Run the -print form first, always. A destructive find is one keystroke away from a wrong one, and the preview costs nothing.

-delete is safe by construction: no shell parsing, no argument list, no separate process, and find refuses it in combination with options that would make it dangerous. Where -delete is unavailable, -exec rm {} + is equivalent and batches arguments.

The tempting alternatives, and why each is wrong:

1 — rm $(find ... ) — command substitution splits on whitespace, so app error.log becomes two arguments app and error.log, and rm deletes whatever those happen to name. It also blows up with "argument list too long" on a large directory, and a filename beginning with - is read as an option.

2 — find ... | xargs rm — the same word-splitting problem, plus xargs also treats quotes specially, so it's.log breaks it as well. find -print0 | xargs -0 rm is correct, but there is no reason to add a pipe and a process when -delete exists.

3 — ls /var/log/myapp/*.log | while read f; do ... done — three problems: parsing ls (§4.9), the glob failing on a large directory, and read without -r mangling backslashes. while IFS= read -r -d '' with find -print0 is the correct loop form when a loop is genuinely needed.

Two details about the criterion itself, which matter more than the deletion mechanics:

  • -mtime +30 means "more than 30 full 24-hour periods ago", and the truncation means +30 actually excludes files aged between 30 and 31 days. If the boundary matters, -mtime +29 or -newermt '30 days ago' (negated) is more predictable.
  • -type f excludes directories and symlinks, which is what you want — without it, a symlink to somewhere important is a candidate.

And the better answer to the underlying problem: if these are application logs, logrotate (§11.6) already does this correctly, with compression, retention counts and a signal to the writing process. A cron job running find -delete over a log directory is usually a sign that rotation was never configured.

Exercise 4.4 — Read the redirection. For each, say exactly where stdout and stderr go, and why.

cmd > out.txt 2>&1
cmd 2>&1 > out.txt
cmd &> out.txt
cmd > out.txt 2> out.txt
cmd 2>&1 | grep error
Solution

1 — cmd > out.txt 2>&1. Both to out.txt. Left to right: stdout is pointed at the file, then stderr is pointed at stdout's current destination, which is the file. This is the correct idiom.

2 — cmd 2>&1 > out.txt. Stderr to the terminal, stdout to out.txt. Stderr is duplicated from stdout while stdout is still the terminal; stdout is then moved to the file, and stderr does not follow. Not a mistake in syntax — a mistake in the mental model of what 2>&1 copies.

3 — cmd &> out.txt. Both to out.txt. Bash shorthand for case 1, and clearer to read. Not POSIX, so use case 1 in a #!/bin/sh script.

4 — cmd > out.txt 2> out.txt. Both nominally to out.txt, and the result is usually corrupt. The file is opened twice, giving two independent file offsets, so each stream overwrites the other's bytes as they both write from their own positions. This looks like the explicit, careful version and is the one genuinely broken entry in the list. Use case 1.

5 — cmd 2>&1 | grep error. Both into the pipe. The shell sets up the pipeline first — stdout becomes the pipe — and then applies 2>&1, so stderr follows stdout into the pipe. This is the correct way to grep a program's error messages, and it is the one case where the ordering rule works in your favour without needing thought. (Bash also offers |& as shorthand for exactly this.)

The rule that generates all five answers: a redirection is an assignment applied in order, and 2>&1 means "make 2 a copy of whatever 1 is right now", not "join them together from here on".

SECTION 5

Text Processing and Editing

Unix's founding bet was that if every program reads text and writes text, programs compose. Sixty years on, that bet is why a log analysis that would be a small script in most environments is one line here, and why the same handful of tools work on a web server log, a CSV export, a ps listing and the output of an API call.

This section covers that toolkit: the filters that transform a stream, the regular expression syntax the searching tools share, sed and awk for the jobs the simple filters cannot do, and vi — because on a machine you have just been handed, vi is the editor that is certainly there.

Every example below runs against one file, an eight-line web server log:

10.0.0.5 - - [26/Aug/2026:14:32:07 +0000] "GET /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 200 1043
10.0.0.9 - - [26/Aug/2026:14:32:08 +0000] "POST /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 201 87
10.0.0.5 - - [26/Aug/2026:14:32:11 +0000] "GET /api/orders/42 HTTP/1.1" 404 61
10.0.0.7 - - [26/Aug/2026:14:32:12 +0000] "GET /health HTTP/1.1" 200 2
10.0.0.5 - - [26/Aug/2026:14:32:19 +0000] "GET /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 500 512
10.0.0.9 - - [26/Aug/2026:14:32:20 +0000] "GET /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 200 1043
10.0.0.7 - - [26/Aug/2026:14:32:25 +0000] "GET /health HTTP/1.1" 200 2
10.0.0.3 - - [26/Aug/2026:14:32:31 +0000] "DELETE /api/orders/7 HTTP/1.1" 500 512

5.1 Viewing: cat, less, head, tail

The cat command concatenates files to standard output — the name is short for concatenate, and joining two files with cat a b > c is its original purpose rather than an afterthought. The cat command's useful flags are -n (number lines), -A (show tabs as ^I, line ends as $ and other control characters visibly — the way to see why a config file is being rejected), and -s (squeeze repeated blank lines).

Do not cat a file into a pipe you could have opened directly. cat file | grep x spawns an extra process and prevents grep from seeking; grep x file is the same result with less work. This matters less for correctness than for the habit — grep x file also lets grep report the filename when you add a second one.

The less pager is where you will spend a lot of time, so it earns a few keystrokes of investment. The less pager is a strict improvement on more — hence the name — because it scrolls backwards and does not read the whole file before showing you the first page, which is what makes it usable on a 4 GB log:

/pattern
Search forward; ?pattern backward; n and N for next and previous.
G / g
End of file; start of file.
F
Follow, like tail -f — and Ctrl-C stops following without leaving the file.
-S
Chop long lines instead of wrapping. Essential for wide logs.
-N
Show line numbers.
&pattern
Show only matching lines — grep inside the pager, without losing your place.

head -n 20 and tail -n 20 take the ends — head -n counts from the start, and head -n -20 (with a minus) prints everything except the last twenty lines, which is the form people rarely know. tail -f is the one you will use most, and tail -F is the one you should usually use instead: -f follows the file descriptor, so it goes silent when the file is rotated (§11.6), while -F follows the name and picks up the replacement.

5.2 The Filters

Each of these does one transformation to a stream, and the value is in combining them.

cut
Select fields or character ranges. -d sets the delimiter, -f the fields, -c character positions.
paste
Join files side by side, one line each, with -d between them.
sort
Order lines. -n numeric, -h human sizes (2K < 3M), -r reverse, -k by field, -t field separator, -u unique, -V version numbers.
uniq
Collapse adjacent duplicates. -c counts, -d shows only duplicates, -u only unique lines.
tr
Translate or delete characters. Reads only standard input — no filename argument.
wc
Count. -l lines, -w words, -c bytes, -m characters.
nl
Number lines, with control over which lines count. -b a numbers every line including blanks.
od
Octal (and hex) dump. od -c is how you see the bytes when a file "looks right" and is not.
split
Break a file into pieces. -l 1000 by lines, -b 10M by size.
join
Relational join of two sorted files on a common field.
comm
Compare two sorted files, three columns: only-in-first, only-in-second, both.

Three of those deserve a sentence each, because their argument syntax is where people stall. paste -d sets the character placed between the joined columns — paste -d, names.txt ages.txt builds a two-column CSV from two single-column files, and paste -d with a newline interleaves them line by line instead. nl -b a numbers every line where the default numbers only non-empty ones, and nl -b t is that default made explicit; unlike cat -n, nl -b can restart numbering at a section separator. split -l 1000 big.log part- breaks a file into 1000-line pieces named part-aa, part-ab and so on; split -l is the line-oriented form, split -b 10M does it by size, and -d uses numeric suffixes.

The join command is the one people forget exists and then reimplement badly. Given two files sorted on a common field, join -t, -1 1 -2 1 users.csv orders.csv produces the relational inner join, with -a 1 for a left outer join and -o to choose the output columns. The join command has one hard requirement: both inputs must be sorted on the join field, in the same collation — LC_ALL=C sort on both first (§11.9), or it silently produces fewer rows than it should.

For integrity rather than content, md5sum, sha256sum and sha512sum print a checksum per file, and md5sum -c sums.txt verifies a list of them. The distinction that matters: md5sum is fine for detecting accidental corruption after a transfer and is cryptographically broken, so it must not be used to verify that a download has not been tampered with. sha256sum is the one to use whenever the answer matters to security, and sha256sum output is what distribution checksum files publish.

Two more of the filters have a flag worth naming. cut -d sets the delimiter and -f the fields, and cut -d treats every delimiter character as a separator — so cut -d' ' on a line with double spaces produces empty fields, which is why awk is usually the better answer for whitespace-aligned data. And tr -d deletes characters rather than translating them: tr -d '\r' strips carriage returns from a file written on Windows, and tr -d '[:punct:]' removes punctuation entirely.

uniq only sees adjacent lines, which is why it is almost always preceded by sort. The canonical idiom for "count occurrences and rank them" is worth committing to memory:

awk '{print $9}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn

      4 200
      2 500
      1 404
      1 201

Four successes, two server errors, one not-found and one created. That pipeline — extract, sort, count, rank — answers a large fraction of all log questions, and only the extraction step changes:

cut -d' ' -f1 access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -3

      3 10.0.0.5
      2 10.0.0.9
      2 10.0.0.7

sort deserves two notes. -k takes a range, not a single field: -k3 means "from field 3 to the end of the line", which is rarely what you want. -k3,3 means field 3 exactly. And sort order is locale-dependent (§11.9) — LC_ALL=C sort is the reproducible form.

sort -k deserves its own line, because field-based sorting is where most sort mistakes live: sort -k2,2n sorts numerically on field 2 alone, and sort -k2 — with no end field — sorts on everything from field 2 to the end of the line. Always give sort -k both halves of the range.

comm requires both inputs sorted and is the fastest way to diff two sets:

comm -3 s1 s2

a
	d

comm -3 is the form to remember: comm -3 suppresses the "in both" column; the remaining two columns are "only in the first" (left) and "only in the second" (indented by a tab). comm -12 gives the intersection, comm -23 the first-only set.

For line-oriented differences within a file, diff -u is the universal format:

diff -u d1 d2

--- d1	2026-08-26 20:53:51.008958016 +0000
+++ d2	2026-08-26 20:53:51.008958016 +0000
@@ -1,3 +1,3 @@
 one
-two
+2
 three

od -c is the tool for the moment a file looks correct and behaves incorrectly: od -c prints every byte with control characters shown by name, so a stray \r, a non-breaking space or a BOM becomes visible. od -A d -t x1 gives the hex form with decimal offsets.

That format is what git diff prints and what patch -p1 consumes — the @@ line gives the starting line and length in each file, and the leading space, - and + mark context, removal and addition. The -p1 in patch -p1 strips one leading path component from every filename in the diff, which is what makes a patch generated inside a repository apply at the repository root.

5.3 Regular Expressions, and the Two Dialects

The single most valuable thing to know about Unix regular expressions is that there are two syntaxes, and the difference is which characters need backslashes.

BRE — Basic. The default for grep and sed. +, ?, |, (, ) and { are literal characters; to get their special meaning you must escape them: \+, \|, \(.
ERE — Extended. grep -E, sed -E, awk. The syntax everyone means by "regex": +, ?, | and () are special, and a literal one is escaped.

The difference is not theoretical:

grep -c 'orders+' access.log
grep -cE 'orders+' access.log
grep -c 'GET|POST' access.log
grep -cE 'GET|POST' access.log

0
6
0
7

The BRE runs are looking for the literal text orders+ and the literal text GET|POST, neither of which appears. The ERE runs are matching one-or-more and alternation, and find 6 and 7 lines. Both commands are valid, neither errors, and they give different answers — which is why "my grep pattern does not work" is usually a missing -E.

The recommendation is simple: use -E always. grep -E and sed -E cost two characters and remove the whole class of problem. egrep and fgrep still exist as aliases for grep -E and grep -F and are formally deprecated.

The syntax itself, in one table:

.
Any single character.
* + ?
Zero-or-more, one-or-more, zero-or-one of the preceding item.
{n,m}
Between n and m repetitions. {3} exactly three.
^ $
Start and end of line. Anchors, matching a position, not a character.
[abc] [^abc]
Character class; negated class.
[[:digit:]]
POSIX class. Also alpha, alnum, space, upper, punct. Locale-correct, unlike [a-z].
(…)
Group, and capture for backreferences and replacements.
\1
Backreference to the first captured group — in the pattern, or in a sed replacement.
|
Alternation.

Three of those are worth a second look. A character class in brackets matches exactly one character from the set, so [aeiou] is one vowel and not a word — and a character class is the only place where ., * and ( lose their special meaning without a backslash. A quantifier applies to the single item before it, which is why ab* is a followed by any number of b, and (ab)* is what you want for repetitions of the pair; every quantifier is greedy in POSIX tools. An anchor matches a position rather than a character, so ^ and $ consume nothing — which is why ^$ matches an empty line and grep -c '^' counts every line in the file.

Two things that catch people. Quantifiers are greedy and POSIX tools have no lazy *? — ".*" on a line with two quoted strings matches from the first quote to the last. The fix is a negated class: "[^"]*". And [a-z] is a range in the current collation order, not "the 26 lowercase letters" (§11.9); [[:lower:]] is what you actually mean.

grep's own flags matter as much as the syntax:

-i
Case-insensitive.
-v
Invert: lines that do not match.
-c
Count matching lines (not matches).
-n
Line numbers.
-r
Recurse a directory tree.
-l / -L
Only the names of files that match / do not match.
-o
Print only the matched part, one per line. Turns grep into an extractor.
-w
Whole words only. Stops cat matching concatenate.
-F
Fixed string, no regex. Faster, and the right choice for user-supplied text.
-A n -B n -C n
Context lines after, before, around. Indispensable for stack traces.
grep -oE '"[A-Z]+ [^ ]+' access.log | head -3

"GET /api/orders
"POST /api/orders
"GET /api/orders/42

-o with a well-chosen pattern replaces a cut whose field numbers would have been wrong, because it matches on structure rather than on position.

5.4 sed

sed applies editing commands to a stream, one line at a time. Ninety percent of real use is one command: s.

s/pattern/replacement/flags

Flags: g for every occurrence on the line rather than the first, i for case-insensitive, and a number for "the nth occurrence".

sed -E 's/^([0-9.]+) .*"([A-Z]+) ([^ ]+).*" ([0-9]{3}).*/\4 \2 \3/' access.log | head -4

200 GET /api/orders
201 POST /api/orders
404 GET /api/orders/42
200 GET /health

Three captured groups, reordered in the replacement as \4 \2 \3 — status, method, path. Note [^ ]+ for the path rather than .+, which greediness would run to the end of the line.

The delimiter does not have to be /. s|/usr/local|/opt| avoids escaping every slash in a path, and any character can follow s. This one habit removes most of the unreadability people associate with sed.

Addresses restrict which lines a command applies to:

sed -n '5,10p' file
sed '/^#/d' config
sed '$d' file
sed -E '/^ERROR/s/foo/bar/' log

-n suppresses the automatic printing, so -n ... p prints only what you selected — that pair is how sed becomes a line-range extractor. /^#/d deletes comments. $ is the last line. And an address before s applies the substitution only on matching lines.

sed -i edits in place, and it is the flag to be careful with:

sed -i 's/beta/BETA/' w.txt
cat w.txt

alpha
BETA
gamma

Two safety notes. sed -i.bak keeps a backup with that suffix, which costs nothing and has saved many people. And GNU sed takes an optional suffix attached to -i while BSD/macOS sed requires a separate argument — sed -i '' 's/x/y/' on macOS versus sed -i 's/x/y/' on Linux — so a script that works on one silently does something different on the other. Always run without -i first and look at the output.

5.5 awk

awk is a small programming language for line-and-field data, and where sed edits text, awk computes. A program is a sequence of pattern { action } rules applied to each line.

Fields are split on whitespace by default and named $1, $2, …, with $0 the whole line and NF the number of fields. NR is the record number. To split on anything else, awk -F sets the field separator — awk -F, '{print $2}' for CSV, awk -F'[:,]' for a regex separator matching either character. awk -F is the flag you will reach for most, because real data is rarely whitespace-separated.

awk '{s+=$10} END {print s, NR, s/NR}' access.log

3262 8 407.75

Total bytes, line count, mean — three aggregates in twenty characters. END runs after the last line; BEGIN before the first, which is where you set FS (input separator) and OFS (output separator).

A bare pattern with no action prints the line; a bare action with no pattern runs on every line:

awk '$9 >= 500 {print $1, $7}' access.log

10.0.0.5 /api/orders
10.0.0.3 /api/orders/7

Numeric comparison on field 9 — awk decides field by field whether something looks like a number, which is convenient and occasionally surprising, and $9+0 forces the numeric interpretation when it matters.

The features worth knowing beyond that:

-F,
Set the field separator. -F'\t' for TSV, -F'[:,]' for a regex separator.
Associative arrays
count[$1]++ — the whole of grouping. Keys are strings, no declaration needed.
for (k in arr)
Iterate an array, in unspecified order. Pipe to sort if order matters.
printf
C-style formatting: printf "%-20s %6.2f\n", name, value.
/regex/ { }
A regex pattern; ERE syntax.
NR==FNR
The idiom for "while reading the first of two files" — how you join two files in one pass.

The grouping idiom deserves to be spelled out because it replaces a great many small scripts:

awk '{bytes[$1] += $10} END {for (ip in bytes) printf "%-12s %8d\n", ip, bytes[ip]}' access.log | sort -k2 -rn

That is a GROUP BY in one line. When you find yourself building sort | uniq -c pipelines with several stages, this is usually the shorter and clearer form.

Where to stop with awk. It is excellent for one-liners and for anything field-oriented. Once a program needs functions, data structures, error handling or a library, it should be Python — a 40-line awk program is harder to maintain than a 25-line Python one, and the boundary is roughly "does it fit on one line, or two".

5.6 Archives and Compression

tar
Archives many files into one, preserving permissions, ownership, timestamps and symlinks. Does not compress by itself.
gzip
Fast, universal, moderate ratio. .gz
bzip2
Slower, better ratio. Largely superseded. .bz2
xz
Slowest, best ratio. What source tarballs and package archives use. .xz
zstd
The modern default: near-gzip speed at near-xz ratios, and very fast decompression. .zst

The separation of archiving from compression is why .tar.gz has two extensions, and why tar has a flag per compressor:

tar -czf backup.tar.gz /srv/app
tar -tzf backup.tar.gz
tar -xzf backup.tar.gz -C /restore

tar -c creates, -t lists and -x extracts; -f names the archive file, and it must come last among the bundled flags because it takes the filename as its argument. Note that tar -c writes to standard output when -f is omitted, which is what makes tar -c dir | ssh host tar -x -C /dest a streaming copy with no intermediate file. In full: -z, -j, -J select gzip, bzip2 and xz; --zstd for zstd. Modern GNU tar detects the compression on extraction, so tar -xf works for all of them.

Three habits worth having. -t before -x on any archive you did not create, because an archive containing absolute paths or ../ can write outside the directory you extracted into. -C to choose the destination rather than cd-ing first. And --xattrs --acls if the archive must preserve extended attributes and ACLs (§6.7), which are silently dropped otherwise.

Note that .zip compresses each file separately while .tar.gz compresses the concatenation — so a tarball of many similar files is much smaller, and a zip lets you extract one file without reading the whole archive. The trade shows up in exactly those two situations.

5.7 vi: the Editor That Is Always There

You will eventually be on a machine with no nano, no editor configured, and a file that must be changed. vi is guaranteed by POSIX and present on every Unix system, and thirty minutes with it is a permanent return on investment.

The idea that makes it strange and then makes it fast: vi is modal. Keys do different things depending on the mode, so ordinary letters are commands rather than text.

Normal
Where you start, and where you should return. Keys are commands.
Insert
Keys are text. Entered with i a o I A O; leave with Esc.
Visual
Select a region, then act on it. v characters, V lines, Ctrl-v a rectangular block.
Command-line
Entered with :. Where files are saved, searched and quit.

The minimum that gets you out of trouble:

Esc
Back to normal mode. When lost, press it twice.
:w
Write. :wq or ZZ write and quit.
:q!
Quit, discarding changes. The escape hatch.
u / Ctrl-r
Undo; redo.
/pattern
Search; n next, N previous.
:%s/a/b/gc
Substitute across the file, all occurrences, confirming each — the same regex syntax as sed.

The four modes in one sentence each, because the names appear in every vi tutorial and are rarely defined: normal mode is where keys are commands and where you should spend most of your time; insert mode is where keys are text, and i, a and o are three different ways into insert mode (before the cursor, after it, and on a new line below); visual mode selects; command-line mode takes : commands.

The part that makes it worth learning rather than merely surviving is that commands compose as a grammar: an operator takes a motion, and the pair is one action.

operators
d delete, c change (delete and insert), y yank (copy), > indent.
motions
w word, $ end of line, 0 start, G end of file, gg start, } paragraph, f, to the next comma.
text objects
iw inner word, i" inside quotes, ip inner paragraph, i( inside parentheses.

So dw deletes a word, d$ deletes to end of line, ci" changes the text inside the quotes your cursor is in, and y} copies a paragraph. Prefixing a count multiplies: 3dd deletes three lines. Learning the grammar means you can express edits you have never performed before, which is the difference from memorising a list.

A text object is the third kind of argument an operator can take, alongside a motion and a count: iw is the word under the cursor, i" is the text between the surrounding quotes, ip the paragraph, and i( the contents of the parentheses. A text object is what makes ci" — change what is inside the quotes — work regardless of where in the string the cursor happens to be, and it is the single feature most worth learning after the operator-motion grammar itself.

Edits are stored in a register rather than a system clipboard: "ayy yanks a line into register a and "ap pastes it back, the unnamed register holds the last delete or yank, and registers 1 to 9 hold the last nine deletions — which is how you recover something you deleted three edits ago. A macro is a register holding keystrokes rather than text, which is why q records into one and @ replays it.

Three more that repay their cost immediately: . repeats the last change (so ciwnew then n. . renames every occurrence you step to), q<letter> records a macro and @<letter> replays it, and :g/pattern/d deletes every line matching a pattern — a sed one-liner without leaving the file.

Configuration lives in ~/.vimrc, read at startup; .vimrc settings can also be typed at the : prompt to try them for one session before committing them to the file. A minimal, useful .vimrc:

set number
set expandtab tabstop=4 shiftwidth=4
set incsearch hlsearch ignorecase smartcase
syntax on

And the EDITOR variable is what everything else reads — sudoedit, git commit, crontab -e, visudo — so export EDITOR=vim in your shell profile is what makes those consistent. nano is a perfectly good choice when it is available; the argument for vi is availability, not superiority. Set the EDITOR variable in your shell profile and every tool that spawns an editor will agree with you.

Exercises

Exercise 5.1 — One pipeline. From the access log, produce the three client IPs that generated the most 5xx responses, most first, with counts. Then explain each stage.

Solution 
awk '$9 ~ /^5/ {print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -3

      1 10.0.0.5
      1 10.0.0.3

Only two clients produced 5xx responses in this sample, so head -3 returns two lines — which is the correct behaviour and worth noticing rather than treating as a bug.

Stage by stage:

  1. awk '$9 ~ /^5/' — field 9 is the status code, and ~ /^5/ is a regex match anchored at the start, so it selects 500, 502, 503 and so on. Using a regex rather than $9 >= 500 also excludes any non-numeric junk in that position.
  2. {print $1} — emit only the client IP.
  3. sort — bring identical IPs together, because uniq only sees adjacent lines.
  4. uniq -c — collapse and count.
  5. sort -rn — order by the count numerically, descending. -n is essential: without it 10 sorts before 9.
  6. head -3 — take the top three.

Two variations worth knowing. The whole thing in one awk program, which is faster on a large file because it is one pass and no sort of the raw data:

awk '$9 ~ /^5/ {c[$1]++} END {for (i in c) print c[i], i}' access.log | sort -rn | head -3

And if the field position is unreliable — a log format where the request line contains spaces — match on structure instead of position:

grep -oE '^[0-9.]+ .*" 5[0-9]{2} ' access.log | cut -d' ' -f1 | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn

Exercise 5.2 — The pattern that matches nothing. A colleague reports that grep 'error|warn' app.log returns nothing, though both words appear in the file. Explain, give two fixes, and say which you would use.

Solution

grep defaults to BRE, where | is a literal character (§5.3). The command is searching for the eight-character string error|warn, which does not appear. It is not an error and produces no warning — it simply finds nothing, which is the worst possible failure mode.

Fix 1 — use ERE:

grep -E 'error|warn' app.log

Fix 2 — escape the pipe to make it special in BRE:

grep 'error\|warn' app.log

Use fix 1. -E is the syntax everyone already knows from every other language, it is what sed -E and awk use, and it does not require remembering which characters are inverted. The BRE escaping rule — where escaping makes a character more special rather than less — is a genuine source of confusion and there is no reason to live with it.

Two refinements to the same command:

grep -iE 'error|warn' app.log
grep -iE '\b(error|warn)' app.log

-i because log levels appear as ERROR as often as error. Word boundaries because warn otherwise matches warning (fine) and unwarned (not). And if the strings are genuinely fixed text with no pattern intent, grep -F -e error -e warn is faster and cannot be confused by a metacharacter in the data at all — which is the right choice when the search terms come from user input.

Exercise 5.3 — Rewrite the config. A directory of 200 .conf files contains lines like server = old.internal:8080. They must all become new.internal:9090, and you need to be able to undo it. Give the procedure.

Solution

Look before you leap:

grep -rn 'old\.internal:8080' /etc/myapp/ | head
grep -rlc 'old\.internal:8080' /etc/myapp/ | wc -l

Note the escaped dot — an unescaped . matches any character, which would also match oldxinternal and, more importantly, would make your count wrong.

Preview the substitution without changing anything:

sed -E 's/old\.internal:8080/new.internal:9090/g' /etc/myapp/app.conf | diff -u /etc/myapp/app.conf -

Piping into diff -u against the original shows exactly what would change, in the format you already read every day. Do this on one representative file before touching 200.

Back up, then apply:

cp -a /etc/myapp "/etc/myapp.bak-$(date +%F)"
grep -rlZ 'old\.internal:8080' /etc/myapp/ | xargs -0 sed -i.orig -E 's/old\.internal:8080/new.internal:9090/g'

Four deliberate choices there:

  • cp -a first. A directory copy is the reliable undo; sed -i.orig leaves per-file backups as a second layer, and having both costs nothing.
  • grep -rl to select files rather than sed -i over every file. Only files that actually match are rewritten, so timestamps on the rest are untouched and the change set is exactly what you inspected.
  • -Z with xargs -0 — NUL separation, so filenames containing spaces or newlines are handled correctly (§4.7).
  • g on the substitution, in case a line contains the string twice.

Verify:

grep -rn 'old\.internal' /etc/myapp/ ; echo "remaining: $?"
grep -rn 'new\.internal:9090' /etc/myapp/ | wc -l
diff -r "/etc/myapp.bak-$(date +%F)" /etc/myapp | head -20

The first should find nothing (exit status 1), the second should match the count from before, and the recursive diff shows every change made in one place.

Then restart whatever reads those files — a configuration change on disk does nothing until it is re-read, which is the same lesson as §12.9 one layer up.

Exercise 5.4 — Stuck in vi. You are on a remote server with no nano. sudoedit /etc/nginx/nginx.conf opened vi. You need to change worker_processes 4; to worker_processes auto;, save, and quit. Give the exact keystrokes, and then say how to leave without saving if you make a mistake.

Solution 
Esc                     (make sure you are in normal mode)
/worker_processes       (search; press Enter)

Then, with the cursor on the matched line, either the surgical version:

f4                      (jump forward to the '4' on this line)
cw                      (change word — deletes '4' and enters insert mode)
auto                    (type the replacement)
Esc
:wq                     (write and quit)

or the version that does not require the cursor to be anywhere in particular:

:%s/worker_processes 4;/worker_processes auto;/
:wq

The second is usually the better answer under pressure: it is one line, it is the same regex syntax as sed (§5.4), and adding c at the end (:%s/…/…/gc) makes vi confirm each replacement, so a pattern that matches more than you expected cannot do damage silently.

To leave without saving:

Esc
:q!

The ! is what forces the discard of unsaved changes. Without it, vi refuses to quit and prints E37: No write since last change, which is the message people interpret as being trapped.

Three things that avoid the panic entirely:

  • u undoes, repeatedly, back through the whole session. If you typed something and do not know what it did, Esc then u is almost always the right move.
  • A stray Ctrl-s freezes the terminal (it is flow control, not save) — Ctrl-q resumes. This is regularly mistaken for vi hanging.
  • sudoedit rather than sudo vi is what you were given here, and it is the correct tool: the editor runs as you on a temporary copy, and only the copy-back is privileged, so an editor escape (:!sh) does not give a root shell (§6.11).
SECTION 6

Permissions, Identity, Users and sudo

Every access check the kernel makes compares two things: an identity attached to a process, and permission bits attached to an object. That is the whole model, and it is small enough to hold in your head — which is exactly why it repays learning precisely rather than approximately. Most permission bugs come from a rule that is almost right: that the most permissive entry wins (it does not), that r on a directory lets you read the files in it (it does not), that sudo means "run as root" (it means more than that), or that adding a user to a group takes effect immediately (it does not).

6.1 Who a Process Is

A process carries a user ID, a primary group ID, and a list of supplementary group IDs. Names are a convenience layer on top; the kernel only ever compares numbers.

id

uid=1000(dev) gid=1000(dev) groups=1000(dev),4(adm),24(cdrom),27(sudo),30(dip),46(plugdev),100(users),101(lxd),111(docker)

Each of those IDs actually exists in triplicate, and the distinction is where setuid lives:

Real UID
Who started the process. Used for signal-sending checks and for accounting.
Effective UID
What permission checks use. Normally equal to the real UID; a setuid binary sets it to the file's owner.
Saved UID
A parking space. Lets a privileged process drop to the effective UID of an unprivileged user and later regain privilege — the pattern every well-written setuid program uses.

The supplementary group list is fixed at login, copied from the group database into the process, and inherited by children. This is why adding yourself to the docker group and then running docker ps in the same terminal fails with a permission error: your shell's group list was built before the change. A new login session — not newgrp, not su - $USER, though both work as a shortcut — is what picks it up. id shows the process's list; getent group docker shows the database's, and when the two disagree, the process's is what counts.

6.2 The Triad, and the Rule That Surprises People

ls -l shows ten characters: a type, then three groups of three.

-
r
w
-
r
-
-
r
-
-
type
owner (u)
group (g)
other (o)

The type character is - regular, d directory, l symlink, b block device, c character device, p named pipe, s socket.

Now the rule. When a process opens a file, the kernel picks exactly one of the three triads:

  1. If the effective UID equals the file's owner → use the owner bits. Stop.
  2. Else if the effective GID or any supplementary group equals the file's group → use the group bits. Stop.
  3. Else → use the other bits.

It is first-match, not best-match, and the consequence is genuinely counter-intuitive:

echo hello > owned.txt
chmod 077 owned.txt
ls -l owned.txt
cat owned.txt

----rwxrwx 1 dev dev 6 Aug 26 20:34 owned.txt
cat: owned.txt: Permission denied

The owner cannot read their own file, while everyone else on the machine can read and write it. The owner triad is empty and matched first, so the generous group and other bits are never consulted. Nobody sets 077 deliberately, but chmod g+w on a file whose owner triad was already wrong produces the same shape, and "but the group has write access!" is a debugging dead end until you know this rule.

The owner does keep one power that no permission bit can remove:

chmod 644 owned.txt
cat owned.txt

hello

Ownership, not permission, is what lets you call chmod. The bits govern reading and writing the contents; changing the bits is a property of being the owner (or holding CAP_FOWNER, §15.9). This is also why you cannot lock yourself out of your own file, and why an attacker who owns a file has already won regardless of its mode.

6.3 Directories Are Not Files

The same three letters mean different things on a directory, and this is the single most common source of "permission denied on a file whose permissions are obviously fine".

r
List the names in the directory. Nothing more — not the file types, not the sizes, not the contents.
x
Traverse it: resolve a name inside it to an inode. Required to access anything through this directory, including reading a file whose own permissions allow it.
w
Create, delete and rename entries. Note what this means: deleting a file is a write to its directory, not to the file — so you can delete a file you have no permission to read.

x without r is a working configuration: you may use any path you already know, but you cannot enumerate. That is exactly what /home/alice with mode 711 gives — anyone can reach /home/alice/public/index.html if told the path, and nobody can list what else is there.

r without x is the broken one, and it is worth seeing:

chmod 644 sub
ls sub
cat sub/file.txt

file.txt
cat: sub/file.txt: Permission denied

The name is listed and the file is unreachable. Restoring the traverse bit fixes it with no change to the file itself:

chmod 744 sub
cat sub/file.txt

secret

The x bit is needed on every directory in the path, from the root down. A web server returning 403 for a file that is 644 and owned by the right user is nearly always missing x on a parent directory — namei -l /srv/www/site/index.html walks the whole path and prints the mode of each component, which turns a guessing game into one command.

6.4 chmod, Octal and Symbolic

Each triad is three bits, so it is one octal digit: r = 4, w = 2, x = 1.

644
rw-r--r--
The default for a file. Owner writes, everyone reads.
600
rw-------
Private. What every key, credential and swap file must be.
755
rwxr-xr-x
The default for a directory or an executable.
700
rwx------
A private directory. ~/.ssh must be exactly this.
775
rwxrwxr-x
Group-writable directory, for a shared working area.
777
rwxrwxrwx
Almost always a mistake, and almost always a stand-in for "I do not know which permission is missing". Find out instead.

Octal sets all nine bits at once. Symbolic mode changes some and leaves the rest, which is usually what you want:

chmod u=rw,g=r,o= f
stat -c '%A %a %n' f
chmod g+w,o+r f
stat -c '%A %a %n' f

-rw-r----- 640 f
-rw-rw-r-- 664 f

The grammar is who (u, g, o, a) then how (+ add, - remove, = set exactly) then what (rwx, or X).

Capital X is the one worth knowing: it adds the execute bit only to directories and to files that already have execute set for somebody. That makes the standard recursive repair safe:

chmod -R a+rX,u+w /srv/www

Every directory becomes traversable and every file readable, without turning a tree of HTML into a tree of executables — which is precisely what chmod -R 755 would do.

Ownership is chown user:group path and chgrp group path — chgrp is exactly chown :group, kept as a separate command because changing only the group is the operation an ordinary user is sometimes allowed to perform (on a file they own, to a group they belong to) while chown proper is root-only; chown -R recurses, and chown --reference=other file copies another file's ownership. Only root may give a file away, because otherwise you could evade a disk quota by donating your files to someone else.

6.5 umask: the Bits You Never Get

New files are created with mode 0666 and new directories with 0777, and the umask subtracts from that. It is a mask of bits to clear, not a permission.

umask
touch f
mkdir d
ls -ld f d

002
drwxrwxr-x 2 dev dev 40 Aug 26 20:34 d
-rw-rw-r-- 1 dev dev  0 Aug 26 20:34 f

002 clears the write bit for other: 666 & ~002 = 664, and 777 & ~002 = 775. Note that files never come out executable no matter what the umask is — the x bit is not in the 666 base at all, which is why a downloaded script always needs chmod +x.

The umask is a property of the process, inherited by children, so setting it applies to everything started afterwards:

(umask 077; touch g; mkdir e; ls -ld g e)

drwx------ 2 dev dev 40 Aug 26 20:34 e
-rw------- 1 dev dev  0 Aug 26 20:34 g

077 is the right umask for anything creating secrets. 002 — the Debian and Ubuntu default — is safe only because those distributions give each user a private group of their own, so group-writable means writable by a group containing one person. On a distribution or a legacy configuration where every user's primary group is users, the same umask means every file you create is writable by everyone on the machine. Check which world you are in before deciding 002 is fine.

For a service, set the umask in the unit file (UMask=0077) rather than in a shell profile, which a daemon never reads.

6.6 setuid, setgid and the Sticky Bit

Three more bits sit above the nine, shown as a fourth leading octal digit: setuid 4, setgid 2, sticky 1.

setuid (u+s, 4755) makes a program run with the effective UID of the file's owner rather than the caller. This is how an ordinary user changes their own password: passwd is owned by root and setuid, so it runs as root and writes to /etc/shadow. Every setuid binary is a privilege boundary and therefore a liability — §15.9 lists the ones on this machine and makes the case for file capabilities instead.

setgid on a file does the same for the group. setgid on a directory does something else entirely and far more useful: new files created inside inherit the directory's group rather than the creator's primary group, and new subdirectories inherit the setgid bit too. That is the mechanism for a shared project directory:

chmod 2775 d
stat -c '%A %a %n' d

drwxrwsr-x 2775 d

The s replaces the group x in the display. Combined with a group everyone in the team belongs to and a 002 umask, every file created in that tree is group-writable by the team automatically, which is the only arrangement that survives contact with real collaborators.

The sticky bit on a directory restricts deletion to the owner of each file, regardless of directory write permission. /tmp is the canonical case:

chmod 1777 d
stat -c '%A %a %n' d
ls -ld /tmp

drwxrwxrwt 1777 d
drwxrwxrwt 17 root root 400 Aug 26 20:34 /tmp

The t replaces the other x. Without it, /tmp being world-writable would let any user delete any other user's temporary files — because deletion is a write to the directory (§6.3), not to the file. Any world-writable shared directory needs this bit.

An uppercase S or T in the display means the special bit is set while the corresponding execute bit is not — usually a mistake, since a setuid file that is not executable does nothing.

6.7 ACLs and Extended Attributes

Three triads express "the owner, one group, everyone else". When you need "and also Bob, read-only", you need an access control list.

setfacl -m u:bob:r-- report.txt
getfacl report.txt

getfacl prints the full access control list — the owner, group and other entries that mirror the mode bits, plus every named user and group entry, plus the mask. Reading getfacl output is the only reliable way to see an access control list, because ls -l compresses the whole thing into nine characters and a +.

-m modifies, -x removes, -b strips all ACLs, and -R recurses. The killer feature is the default ACL on a directory, which every newly created entry inherits:

setfacl -d -m g:developers:rwx /srv/project

That is setgid's inheritance idea generalised to arbitrary entries, and it is how you build a directory where several teams have different rights that persist as files are created.

Two things to know. ls -l shows a + at the end of the mode when an ACL is present — that is your only hint, and the one you will see on /usr/bin/ping from §15.9. And the "group" bits become the ACL mask once an ACL exists: they cap every named entry rather than describing one group, so a chmod g-w on an ACL'd file silently removes write access from every named user too. This is the trap that makes chmod and setfacl interact badly; use setfacl -m m::rwx to set the mask deliberately.

ACLs are stored in extended attributes — arbitrary name/value pairs on an inode, in namespaces: user.* for anything you like, system.* for ACLs, security.* for SELinux labels and file capabilities, trusted.* for root-only data. getfattr -d -m - dumps them, setfattr sets them. Note that a cp without -a (or --preserve=xattr) silently drops them, and tar needs --xattrs, which is how ACLs and SELinux labels get lost in a restore.

6.8 Where Accounts Live

getent passwd dev
getent group sudo

dev:x:1000:1000:Dev User:/home/dev:/usr/bin/zsh
sudo:x:27:dev

/etc/passwd is seven colon-separated fields: name : password : UID : GID : GECOS : home : shell. The password field is x, meaning "look in /etc/shadow" — the whole point of shadow passwords, because /etc/passwd must be world-readable so that ls -l can turn UIDs into names, and a world-readable hash is an offline cracking target.

/etc/shadow is root-only and holds nine fields: name, the hash, and six aging fields — last change, minimum days, maximum days, warning period, inactivity period, and expiry date. The hash itself encodes its algorithm in a $id$ prefix: $6$ is SHA-512, $y$ is yescrypt (the current default on Debian and Ubuntu). A ! or * in place of a hash means no password will ever match, which is how an account is locked or made non-interactive.

/etc/group is name, password, GID, and a comma-separated member list. A user's primary group comes from /etc/passwd's fourth field and does not appear in /etc/group's member list, which is why "the group file does not list me but id says I am in it" is normal.

getent rather than grep. Accounts do not have to be local — /etc/nsswitch.conf lists the sources consulted for each database, in order, and can include LDAP, SSSD, systemd-userdb or a container's own mappings. getent passwd bob asks the whole stack; grep bob /etc/passwd asks one file and returns nothing on a machine using directory services. The same applies to getent hosts versus /etc/hosts (§13.7).

6.9 Managing Accounts

useradd
Low-level, and creates no home directory unless given -m. -s sets the shell, -G supplementary groups, -r a system account.
adduser
Debian's interactive wrapper: home directory, password prompt, sane defaults. What a person should use on a Debian machine.
usermod
Change an existing account. -G replaces the supplementary group list; -aG appends. Forgetting the -a removes every other group the user was in.
userdel -r
Delete, and with -r remove the home directory and mail spool. Without it the files stay, owned by an unused UID that a future account may inherit.
groupadd / groupmod / gpasswd -a
Create, modify, and add a member to a group.
chage
Password aging: -l to list, -M maximum days, -E expiry date, -d 0 to force a change at next login.
passwd -l / -u
Lock and unlock an account by prefixing the hash with !. Note -l does not stop SSH key authentication — usermod -s /usr/sbin/nologin and removing authorized_keys is what actually locks someone out.

/etc/skel/ is copied into every new home directory created with -m. Note that /etc/skel is copied once, at account creation — changing a file in /etc/skel does nothing for accounts that already exist, which is why a fleet-wide shell change belongs in /etc/profile.d/ (§2.6) and not there. It is the right place for a default .bashrc, .profile or editor configuration on a multi-user machine.

Three of those commands come in obvious pairs and one does not. groupadd creates a group and groupmod renames it or changes its GID — and groupmod -n newname oldname is the one whose argument order surprises people, since the new name comes first. userdel removes an account and userdel -r removes the home directory with it. And chage -l alice lists an account's aging fields in human form, which is far easier to read than the raw colon-separated numbers in /etc/shadow; chage -E sets an expiry date, which is the field PAM's account stack actually enforces.

System accounts are the ones a service runs as. They conventionally have a UID below 1000 (SYS_UID_MIN/SYS_UID_MAX in /etc/login.defs), no valid password, and /usr/sbin/nologin as their shell. The point is that a compromised service becomes a user who cannot log in, cannot be su'd to, and owns almost nothing. useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /nonexistent appsvc is the whole recipe — and systemd's DynamicUser=yes goes further by allocating one for the lifetime of the service and discarding it afterwards.

6.10 PAM: Where "Log In" Is Actually Decided

Checking a password is not the only thing that happens at login. The account may be expired, the machine may be in maintenance, a home directory may need mounting, a session may need a cgroup, a failed-attempt counter may need incrementing. PAM — Pluggable Authentication Modules — is the framework that sequences all of it, and it is why login, sshd, sudo and su behave consistently without sharing code.

Each service has a file in /etc/pam.d/ with lines of type control module arguments, in four stacks:

auth
Prove identity. Passwords, keys, tokens, fingerprints.
account
Is this account allowed to log in now? Expiry, time-of-day restrictions, group membership.
password
Changing the credential. Quality rules live here (pam_pwquality).
session
Set up and tear down the session: home directory, limits (pam_limits), logging, the systemd user session (pam_systemd).

The control field is the subtle part: required must succeed but the rest of the stack still runs (so a failure does not reveal which check failed), requisite fails immediately, sufficient succeeds the stack outright if it passes, and optional does not affect the result.

You do not often write PAM configuration, but you must recognise its symptoms. "Login works with a key but the session ends immediately" is usually an account or session module. "Correct password rejected on one service only" is that service's PAM file. And a syntax error in a PAM file can lock everyone out of the machine, including root — so the rule is to keep a root shell open in another terminal while editing, and test in the second one.

6.11 su, sudo and sudoers

su - switches to another user by asking for that user's password, and the trailing dash is important: it makes the shell a login shell, so it reads the target's profile and gets their environment and PATH. su without it keeps your environment while changing your identity, which produces confusing hybrids like root running with your PATH.

sudo asks for your own password and consults a policy file. That difference is the entire reason it won: no shared root password to rotate when someone leaves, per-command granularity, and an audit trail naming the human.

/etc/sudoers is edited with visudo, never directly. visudo validates the syntax before installing the file, and a syntax error in sudoers means nobody can use sudo — on a cloud instance with no root password, that is a machine you have locked yourself out of administering. Local rules belong in a file under /etc/sudoers.d/ for the same reason drop-ins are preferred everywhere else.

A rule reads who where=(as-whom) what:

alice     ALL=(ALL:ALL) ALL
%deploy   ALL=(www-data) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/systemctl restart myapp

% marks a group. The second line lets everyone in deploy restart one service as www-data with no password, and nothing else. That is the shape to aim for; ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: ALL is a root shell with extra steps.

Three things that are not obvious and matter:

Command restrictions are weaker than they look. NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/vim is a root shell, because vim can run :!sh. So can less, find -exec, awk, git (via a pager or hooks), and most editors and interpreters. Any command that can execute another command grants everything it was reaching for. Restrict to a script you wrote that takes no arguments, and be suspicious of wildcards.

sudo sanitises the environment, resetting PATH to secure_path and dropping most variables. This is a security feature — LD_PRELOAD (§12.9) into a root process would be catastrophic — and it is why sudo make install may not find a tool that is on your PATH. sudo -E preserves the environment and should be used with a clear reason.

sudo -i versus sudo -s versus sudo command. -i is a full root login shell (root's environment, root's PATH, root's home). -s is a root shell with your environment. A bare sudo command runs one command and returns — and is the form to prefer, because every root shell is an opportunity to forget which terminal you are in.

The timestamp file means a password typed once is remembered for 15 minutes by default, per terminal. sudo -k forgets it immediately, which is worth doing before walking away from a shared machine.

6.12 Limits, and Who Is Logged In

ulimit exposes per-process resource limits, each with a soft value the user may raise up to the hard value, and a hard value only root may raise.

ulimit -n
ulimit -Hn

-n is open file descriptors and is the one you will actually hit — a busy server, a connection pool, or a descriptor leak (§8.2) meets it as Too many open files. -u is processes (a fork bomb's ceiling), -c core file size, -v virtual memory, -s stack size.

Limits set with ulimit last for the shell and its children. Persistent limits for logins go in /etc/security/limits.conf (applied by pam_limits at session setup, §6.10) — and, crucially, limits.conf does not apply to systemd services, which are not logins. For a service, the limit belongs in the unit: LimitNOFILE=65536. Editing limits.conf and restarting a daemon that then still hits the old limit is a well-worn hour, and cat /proc/PID/limits is the check that ends it.

Who is here, and who has been:

who
The who command lists current logins, from /var/run/utmp: user, terminal, login time, source address. who -u adds idle time and the PID of each session's shell.
w
The same plus load average and what each session is running right now.
last
Login history from /var/log/wtmp, most recent first. last -f /var/log/btmp reads failed attempts, which is the fastest way to see a brute-force attempt in progress.
lastlog
The most recent login per account. Its real use is finding accounts that have never logged in — old staff, forgotten service accounts, and anything that should be deleted.

who reads the same file every login writes to, so the who command is only as accurate as whatever last updated utmp — a process detached with setsid (§7.7) does not appear at all. The last command reads the historical file instead, and last -n 20 or last alice narrows it; a last command output that starts abruptly means the file was rotated, not that nobody logged in before then. And id -u prints just the numeric user ID, which is why [ "$(id -u)" -eq 0 ] is the correct root check in a script — testing $USER or whoami compares a name that can be changed.

These files are append-only records written by userspace, not kernel-enforced audit logs; an intruder with root can edit them. Treat them as operational information, and use the journal (§11.2) or a remote log host when you need evidence.

Exercises

Exercise 6.1 — Four denials. For each, give the cause and the one command that proves it:

  1. cat /srv/data/report.csv gives "Permission denied". The file is -rw-r--r-- root:staff and you are in staff.
  2. A web server returns 403 for a file that is 644 www-data:www-data in a directory that is 755 www-data:www-data.
  3. You added yourself to the docker group with usermod -aG docker $USER and docker ps still says permission denied.
  4. A file is ----rwxrwx and owned by you, and you cannot read it.
Solution

1 — A parent directory is missing x. The file's own mode allows the read; something above it does not.

namei -l /srv/data/report.csv

namei -l prints the mode and owner of every path component, so the offending directory is visible at a glance. This beats ls -ld on each level, and it also resolves symlinks along the way.

2 — Almost certainly not a Unix permission at all. The file and directory modes are correct for the server's own user, so look at the layers above: an SELinux label (ls -Z, and the classic cause is a file mv'd in from elsewhere keeping the wrong type), an AppArmor profile denying the path (§15.11), or the web server's own configuration.

ls -Z /srv/www/site/index.html

and check the journal for avc: denied or apparmor="DENIED". Only after those does it make sense to walk the path with namei -l again for a directory higher up than the one quoted.

3 — The process's group list is fixed at login. usermod changed the database, not your running shell.

id -Gn          # your shell's list — no docker
getent group docker   # the database's list — you are in it

The disagreement between those two outputs is the diagnosis. A new login session picks it up; newgrp docker starts a subshell with the group added as a stopgap.

4 — First-match, not best-match (§6.2). You match the owner triad, which is empty, so the kernel stops there and never looks at the generous group and other bits.

stat -c '%A %U:%G' owned.txt

Compare the owner against your id -un: if they match, the first triad is the only one that will ever be consulted. chmod u+rw fixes it, and you may do so because ownership — not permission — is what authorises chmod.

Exercise 6.2 — The shared directory. Three developers in the group webdev must all read and write everything under /srv/site, including files each other creates, indefinitely. Give the setup, and explain why the two obvious approaches fail.

Solution 
groupadd webdev
usermod -aG webdev alice
usermod -aG webdev bob
usermod -aG webdev carol
chgrp -R webdev /srv/site
chmod -R g+rwX /srv/site
chmod g+s /srv/site
find /srv/site -type d -exec chmod g+s {} +

and each developer's umask set to 002 (in /etc/profile.d/ or their shells), or — more robustly — a default ACL:

setfacl -R -m g:webdev:rwX /srv/site
setfacl -R -d -m g:webdev:rwX /srv/site

Why chmod -R 775 alone fails: it fixes the files that exist today. A file created tomorrow gets the creator's primary group (alice, not webdev) and, with the default 022 umask, mode 644 — so nobody else can write it. The problem is not the current permissions; it is the ones new files will be born with.

Why chmod 777 fails: it appears to work and gives every user on the machine — including any compromised service account — full write access to the site. It also does not survive: a new file still arrives as 644, so people re-run the chmod forever and eventually put it in a cron job, which is how a world-writable web root becomes permanent.

Why the working answer works: g+s on the directories makes new entries inherit the webdev group and makes new subdirectories setgid too, so the property propagates. The 002 umask leaves the group write bit intact. X (capital) adds traverse on directories without making every file executable. The ACL version is stronger because it does not depend on each person's umask being right — the default ACL is a property of the directory, so it holds no matter who writes there or how their shell is configured.

Exercise 6.3 — Review the sudoers. A team proposes these rules. Assess each.

%devs   ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/systemctl
%devs   ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/vim /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
%devs   ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /bin/chmod
deploy  ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /opt/deploy/release.sh
Solution

Line 1 — full root. systemctl with no subcommand restriction permits systemctl edit, which opens an editor as root (:!sh), and systemd-run, and starting a unit the caller wrote. Even without those, the ability to stop arbitrary services is a denial-of-service tool. Restrict to specific verbs and units:

%devs ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/systemctl restart myapp, /usr/bin/systemctl status myapp

Line 2 — full root. Any editor as root is a root shell: :!sh in vim, !sh in less, :w /etc/sudoers to write anywhere. The argument restriction changes nothing, because the escape does not need a second file argument. Use sudoedit (sudo -e), which copies the file to a temporary location, runs the editor as the calling user, and copies it back with privilege — the editor never runs as root.

Line 3 — full root. chmod as root can set the setuid bit on any binary: sudo chmod u+s /bin/bash and the next bash -p is a root shell. chown, chmod, dd, tar, find, awk, git and every interpreter have the same property. The general test to apply to any proposed rule: can this command write an arbitrary file, or execute an arbitrary command? If yes, it grants root.

Line 4 — the right shape, with two conditions. A single script with no arguments is the correct pattern. It is only safe if /opt/deploy/release.sh and every directory above it are not writable by deploy — otherwise the user simply rewrites the script and runs it as root. Verify with namei -l /opt/deploy/release.sh. Second condition: the script itself must not take user input that reaches a shell, and should set its own PATH rather than trusting one.

One more thing to add, orthogonal to all of the above: Defaults logfile=/var/log/sudo.log and Defaults log_input,log_output for a real audit trail, since the whole argument for sudo over a shared root password is accountability.

Exercise 6.4 — Lock the account. A developer leaves. Someone runs passwd -l alice and closes the ticket. List everything still possible, and give the complete procedure.

Solution

passwd -l only prefixes the password hash with !. It stops password authentication and nothing else. Still possible:

  • SSH public-key login. ~alice/.ssh/authorized_keys is untouched, and key auth never consults the password hash. This is the big one, and on a machine where everyone uses keys it means the "lock" changed nothing at all.
  • Existing sessions. Any shell, tmux session or port forward already open keeps running.
  • Scheduled jobs. crontab -u alice -l, at jobs, and systemd user units all still fire.
  • sudo from any of the above, if alice is in a privileged group.
  • Services running as alice, and anything using her SSH keys or tokens elsewhere.

The complete procedure:

usermod -L -e 1 -s /usr/sbin/nologin alice      # lock, expire the account, no shell
mv /home/alice/.ssh/authorized_keys /root/retired/alice.authorized_keys
crontab -u alice -r
pkill -KILL -u alice
loginctl terminate-user alice
gpasswd -d alice sudo
gpasswd -d alice docker

-e 1 expires the account (as opposed to the password), which PAM's account stack enforces for every service including SSH keys — that is the step that closes the key-authentication hole properly. Killing processes and terminating the systemd session ends anything already running. Removing group memberships matters because a re-enabled account should not silently come back with sudo and docker.

Then, separately, the credentials that are not on this machine: revoke her keys from the deploy system and any git forge, rotate any shared secret she held, and audit last -f /var/log/wtmp alice for what happened before the ticket was raised. Deleting the account with userdel -r is the last step and often the wrong one — the home directory may hold the only copy of something, and the UID may be reused by the next account created, silently granting the new person ownership of any file left behind.

SECTION 7

Processes, Signals and Job Control

A process is the unit of everything on Linux. It owns memory, file descriptors, an identity, a working directory, a set of namespaces and a cgroup; it is what gets scheduled, what gets killed, and what shows up in every tool in this section. Understanding processes well is the difference between "the service is stuck, restart it" and knowing what it is stuck on.

The other half of this section is signals, which are how one process interrupts another. They are a small, strange, forty-year-old mechanism — asynchronous, mostly carrying no data, with two members that cannot be refused — and every graceful shutdown, every Ctrl-C, every container stop and every log rotation depends on getting them right.

7.1 fork, exec, and the Tree

Linux creates processes by cloning an existing one. There is no "create a process from this program" call; there are two calls that do half the job each.

fork()
Duplicates the calling process. The child gets a copy of the memory (copy-on-write, so nothing is really copied until written), the same open file descriptors, the same working directory. It returns 0 in the child and the child's PID in the parent — the same line of code, two different answers.
execve()
Replaces the current process's program with a new one, keeping the PID, the descriptors and the identity. Nothing new is created; the process is re-inhabited.

A shell running ls does both: fork, then in the child, exec ls. The separation looks baroque and is the source of Unix's power — everything you want to change about the child's environment (redirect its output, drop privileges, set a umask, enter a namespace, apply a seccomp filter) happens in between, in ordinary code, with no special API. bwrap in §15 is that gap, elaborated.

The process ID the child receives is a small integer, unique at any moment, allocated sequentially and wrapped at kernel.pid_max (§8.8). A process ID is reused once the process has exited and been reaped, which is why a script that records a PID and signals it later can — very occasionally — signal something else entirely; systemctl kill and cgroup membership avoid that race, and a raw PID does not.

Because every process is forked from another, they form a tree with PID 1 at the root:

pstree -p 1 | head -5

systemd(1)-+-ModemManager(1189)-+-{ModemManager}(1256)
           |                    |-{ModemManager}(1258)
           |                    `-{ModemManager}(1264)
           |-agetty(1088)
           |-chronyd-starter(978)---chronyd(1111)---chronyd(1136)

Names in braces are threads, not processes. A thread on Linux is a process that shares memory with its siblings — created by the same clone() call with different flags. That is why ps -L can show threads and why "process" and "thread" are much closer here than on other systems. clone() is the syscall fork() is now a thin wrapper around, and the flag argument to clone() is what decides which resources are shared and which are copied: memory, file descriptors, the filesystem view, the signal handlers, and — the ones §15 is about — the namespaces.

7.2 Reading ps

ps has two incompatible option styles for historical reasons: BSD (no dash, ps aux) and UNIX (with a dash, ps -ef). Both are universal; pick one and learn it.

ps aux | head -4

USER         PID %CPU %MEM    VSZ   RSS TTY      STAT START   TIME COMMAND
root           1  0.6  0.4  24808 16360 ?        Ss   19:38   0:22 /usr/lib/systemd/systemd --switched-root --system --deserialize=51
root           2  0.0  0.0      0     0 ?        S    19:38   0:00 [kthreadd]
root           3  0.0  0.0      0     0 ?        S    19:38   0:00 [pool_workqueue_release]

Two things in those three lines. Names in square brackets are kernel threads — they have no user-space memory, which is why VSZ and RSS are 0, and they cannot be killed. And TTY of ? means no controlling terminal: a daemon.

VSZ versus RSS is the memory distinction that matters. VSZ is virtual size — everything mapped, including shared libraries, memory-mapped files and allocations never touched. It is routinely enormous and almost never useful. RSS is resident set size: physical pages actually in RAM. Even RSS over-counts, because shared library pages are charged in full to every process sharing them, so summing RSS across processes gives a total larger than the machine has. For a real per-process number use PSS (proportional set size) from /proc/PID/smaps_rollup, which divides shared pages by the number of sharers.

The far more useful form is a custom column list with a sort:

ps -eo pid,ppid,user,stat,ni,rss,etime,comm --sort=-rss | head -8

    PID    PPID USER     STAT  NI   RSS     ELAPSED COMMAND
   1268       1 mysql    Ssl    0 528520      59:25 mysqld
  21844    2791 dev      Rl+    0 455332      40:00 claude
   1391       1 root     Ssl    0 86472       59:23 dockerd
   1105       1 root     Ssl    0 55740       59:27 containerd
    987       1 root     Ss     0 52464       59:29 glances
  13528       1 root     Ssl    0 44320       48:45 fwupd
   1164       1 postgres Ss     0 33968       59:26 postgres

-e is every process, -o picks columns, --sort=-rss sorts descending by memory. Swap in --sort=-pcpu for CPU. The other columns worth adding: etime (elapsed run time, for spotting the process that has been running since Tuesday), wchan (the kernel function it is sleeping in, which names what it is waiting for), and args in place of comm for the full command line.

7.3 Process States

The STAT column is a state letter plus modifiers.

R
Running or runnable. On a CPU, or in the queue waiting for one.
S
Interruptible sleep. Waiting for something — a read, a timer, a lock — and will wake for a signal. The state most processes are in most of the time.
D
