Uninterruptible sleep. Blocked in the kernel, usually on I/O, and will not respond to any signal, including SIGKILL. A process stuck in D is the reason "I killed it and it is still there".
T
Stopped by a job-control signal (SIGSTOP, SIGTSTP) — or t, stopped by a debugger.
Z
Zombie. Exited, but its exit status has not been collected. Occupies a PID and nothing else.
I
Idle kernel thread. Deliberately excluded from the load average.

The suffixes: s session leader, l multi-threaded, + in the foreground process group, < high priority (negative nice), N low priority.

So Ssl on mysqld above is a multi-threaded session-leading daemon asleep, and Rl+ on claude is a multi-threaded foreground process that is actually running. Reading STAT before anything else usually tells you which question to ask.

D deserves special attention because it breaks the mental model that root can kill anything. A process in uninterruptible sleep is inside a kernel call that cannot safely be unwound — waiting on an NFS server that stopped responding, or a disk that is failing. kill -9 is queued and delivered when the call returns, which may be never. The fix is at the I/O layer: bring back the server, or reset the device. It is also why load average on Linux counts D processes (§8.3) — twelve processes stuck on a dead mount give a load of 12 on a completely idle CPU.

7.4 Signals

A signal is a number delivered asynchronously to a process. The kernel interrupts whatever it was doing, runs a handler if one is installed, and otherwise applies a default action — usually terminate.

kill -l | head -4

HUP INT QUIT ILL TRAP IOT BUS FPE KILL USR1 SEGV USR2 PIPE ALRM TERM STKFLT CHLD CONT STOP TSTP TTIN TTOU URG XCPU XFSZ VTALRM PROF WINCH POLL PWR SYS

The ones worth knowing by number, because you will type them:

1
SIGHUP
Originally "the terminal hung up". Now conventionally means reload your configuration — nginx, sshd and rsyslog all use it that way.
2
SIGINT
Ctrl-C. Polite interrupt; catchable.
3
SIGQUIT
Ctrl-\. Terminate and dump core. In the JVM and in Go it prints a stack dump of every thread instead — the fastest way to see where a hung Go process is stuck.
9
SIGKILL
Cannot be caught, blocked or ignored. The kernel destroys the process. No cleanup runs.
11
SIGSEGV
Invalid memory access. Sent by the kernel, not by you.
13
SIGPIPE
Wrote to a pipe with no reader. Default action is death — which is why head closing early kills the producer upstream of it (§4.6).
15
SIGTERM
The default for kill. "Please shut down." Catchable, and the whole basis of graceful shutdown.
17
SIGCHLD
A child exited. Sent to the parent, and how reaping is triggered (§7.5).
18/19/20
SIGCONT / SIGSTOP / SIGTSTP
Resume; unstoppable stop; Ctrl-Z stop.

The two uncatchable signals are SIGKILL and SIGSTOP. Everything else can be handled, and the difference is visible in one experiment. This program installs handlers for SIGTERM and SIGINT that simply print:

import os, signal, time

def handler(signum, frame):
    print(f"caught {signal.Signals(signum).name}", flush=True)

signal.signal(signal.SIGTERM, handler)
signal.signal(signal.SIGINT, handler)
print(f"pid={os.getpid()}", flush=True)
for _ in range(30):
    time.sleep(0.2)

Send it all three:

kill -TERM "$P"
kill -INT "$P"
kill -KILL "$P"

pid=59519
caught SIGTERM
caught SIGINT

Two signals were caught and survived; the third produced no output at all and the process was gone. That asymmetry is the entire design. SIGTERM asks and can be refused, which is what lets a program finish a request, flush a buffer and close a connection. SIGKILL cannot be refused, which is what guarantees an administrator can always reclaim resources — at the price of no cleanup whatsoever: no finally block, no destructor, no flush, no unlock. A database killed with -9 recovers from its journal on the next start, and one killed with -15 does not need to.

Reach for -9 last, not first. The common habit of typing kill -9 immediately is how partially written files and stale lock files happen.

7.5 Zombies, Orphans and Reaping

When a process exits, the kernel keeps a small record — the exit status and some accounting — until the parent collects it with wait(). Between exit and collection, the process is a zombie: no memory, no descriptors, just a PID and a status.

Zombies are normal and momentary. They become a problem only when the parent never calls wait(), and then they accumulate: each one holds a PID, and enough of them exhaust kernel.pid_max so that nothing new can start. The fix is never to kill the zombie — it is already dead — but to fix or restart the parent, which is what ps -eo stat,ppid | awk '$1 ~ /Z/' is for: it gives you the parent's PID, which is the process that needs attention.

An orphan is the other case: the parent exits first. The kernel re-parents the child to PID 1 (or to the nearest ancestor marked as a subreaper), and PID 1 reaps it. This is why nohup command & survives your shell exiting — the orphaned process is adopted, not killed.

This is the container init problem. In a container, your application is PID 1 (§15.2), and PID 1 inherits init's duty to reap orphans. An application that never calls wait() accumulates zombies for every process it spawns, and — worse — PID 1 has different signal semantics: the kernel does not apply default actions to PID 1, so a SIGTERM to an application that installs no handler does nothing at all, and docker stop falls back to SIGKILL after its ten-second timeout. That is the real reason a container takes exactly ten seconds to stop, and the fix is either a real init (docker run --init, tini, dumb-init) or handling SIGTERM in the application.

7.6 Sending Signals

kill PID
Sends SIGTERM by default, despite the name. kill -HUP, kill -9, kill -s TERM are all accepted.
kill -0 PID
Sends nothing; just checks whether the process exists and you may signal it. The correct existence test in a script.
pkill -f pattern
Match on the full command line, which is what you want for java -jar app.jar. Test with pgrep -af pattern first — always.
killall name
Match on the executable name exactly. Note it is not portable in meaning: on some non-Linux systems killall kills every process on the machine.
kill -TERM -PGID
A negative PID targets a whole process group — the parent and everything it started. The way to kill a pipeline or a build.
systemctl kill unit
Signals every process in the unit's cgroup, which is the only method that cannot miss a double-forked child (§9.8).

The habit worth forming: run pgrep -af before every pkill. pkill -f python on a machine running three Python services kills all three, and the command that would have told you gives its output in the same shape.

7.7 Job Control

Job control is the shell's ability to run several things at once in one terminal, and it is built on signals and process groups. Every pipeline the shell starts becomes a process group; exactly one group is the terminal's foreground group and receives keyboard signals.

command &
Start in the background. The shell prints [1] 12345 — job number and PID.
Ctrl-Z
SIGTSTP to the foreground group: suspend it, leaving it in state T.
jobs
List this shell's jobs with their numbers and states.
fg %1
Bring job 1 to the foreground and continue it.
bg %1
Continue it in the background (SIGCONT).
wait
Block until background jobs finish. In a script, wait $PID also yields that job's exit status.

Ctrl-Z then bg is the everyday move: you started a long job in the foreground, realised you need the prompt back, and did not want to restart it. Ctrl-Z then fg after checking something is the other.

A background job is not a detached job. When the terminal closes, the kernel sends SIGHUP to the session's foreground group, and most shells forward it to every job. Three ways to survive:

nohup cmd &
Ignores SIGHUP and redirects output to nohup.out if it is a terminal. Decided before starting.
disown -h %1
Removes an already running job from the shell's table so it is not sent SIGHUP. The rescue when you forgot nohup.
setsid cmd
Runs in a brand-new session with no controlling terminal. The strongest of the three, and what a daemon does.

disown is worth understanding precisely, because it is the one that works retroactively: disown %1 removes the job from the shell's job table so no SIGHUP is sent to it, and disown -h %1 keeps the job listed but marks it not to be signalled. disown changes nothing about the process itself — its descriptors, its terminal and its parent stay exactly as they were.

All three share a flaw: you cannot get back to the program. Its output is a file, and it has no terminal to type into.

7.8 tmux: the Right Answer

tmux (and its predecessor screen) solves the problem properly by putting a terminal on the far side of the detach, not just a process. The program keeps a real TTY, keeps its scrollback, and you can reattach from a different machine tomorrow.

tmux new -s work
Start a named session.
Ctrl-b d
Detach. The session keeps running.
tmux ls
List sessions.
tmux attach -t work
Reattach, from any terminal on any machine.
Ctrl-b c / n / % / "
New window; next window; split vertically; split horizontally.
Ctrl-b [
Copy mode — scroll back through output with the arrow keys or vi motions.

screen is still installed on many machines and does the same job with a different prefix key (Ctrl-a rather than Ctrl-b) and a smaller feature set; if you find screen and not tmux on a server, screen -S work, Ctrl-a d to detach and screen -r work to reattach are the three commands you need.

The operational rule that follows: start any long or risky remote command inside tmux. A dropped SSH connection during a six-hour database migration is a very different event depending on whether that command was in a tmux session, and the cost of the habit is four keystrokes.

7.9 Priorities

Niceness is a scheduling hint from −20 (most favourable) to +19 (least). Higher nice value means lower priority — the name is literal: a nice process yields to others. Only root may lower a niceness, so an unprivileged user can give up CPU but not claim more.

nice -n 10 ./batch-job
renice -n 5 -p 12345
renice -n 5 -u backupuser

Niceness is a weight, not a cap: a nice-19 process still uses 100% of an otherwise idle machine. Cgroup cpu.weight (§15.8) is the same idea applied to a group rather than a process, and it is what to use for a service.

ionice does the equivalent for disk I/O, which is often what actually matters. A backup that is nice-19 still saturates the disk queue and adds latency to everything else, because CPU niceness has no effect on the block layer:

ionice -c 3 -p 12345

Class 3 is "idle" — I/O only when nothing else wants the device. Class 2 is best-effort with a priority 0–7, class 1 is real-time and needs privilege. nice -n 19 ionice -c 3 command is the standard pair for a batch job that must stay out of the way, and the ionice half is usually the effective one.

7.10 top and What to Read in It

top refreshes a process list continuously. top -b is the batch form for scripts and cron: no cursor control, plain lines, and top -b -n 1 takes a single snapshot and exits, which is what you want when capturing state during an incident (§16.8). The keys worth knowing: P sort by CPU, M sort by memory, 1 show each CPU separately, H show threads, c toggle full command lines, k kill, f choose fields. htop adds colour, mouse support and scrolling and is worth installing.

The header line most people misread is the CPU line:

us
User time — your code.
sy
System time — the kernel on your behalf. High sy with low us means syscall-heavy work: too many small reads, too many context switches.
ni
User time by niced processes.
id
Idle.
wa
I/O wait — idle, but with at least one process blocked on I/O. High wa means the CPU is not the bottleneck, the disk is.
st
Steal — time the hypervisor gave to someone else. Non-zero st on a VM means a noisy neighbour, and no amount of tuning inside your machine will fix it.

wa and st are the two that redirect the investigation entirely, and they are the two people skip over.

7.11 The Graceful Shutdown Contract

Everything in this section converges on one pattern, and it is the same at every layer.

1 · SIGTERM
The supervisor asks the process to stop. A well-written program stops accepting new work, finishes what is in flight, flushes, closes, and exits.
2 · Wait
A grace period. systemd's TimeoutStopSec= defaults to 90 seconds; Docker's --stop-timeout to 10; Kubernetes' terminationGracePeriodSeconds to 30.
3 · SIGKILL
Time is up. The kernel destroys it, in-flight work and all.

Two failure modes follow directly, and both are common. A program that ignores SIGTERM always takes the full timeout and is always killed hard — every restart drops requests, and deploys are slow for a reason nobody investigates. A grace period shorter than the work truncates legitimate long requests; if your slowest request takes 45 seconds, a 30-second grace period kills it on every deploy.

In systemd the whole contract is configurable — KillSignal=, TimeoutStopSec=, and KillMode= (which decides whether the signal goes to the main process or to every process in the cgroup). KillMode=mixed — SIGTERM to the main process, SIGKILL to the whole cgroup at the end — is the setting that stops orphaned children surviving a service restart, and it is the fix for "I restarted it but the old workers are still holding the port".

Exercises

Exercise 7.1 — It will not die. A process shows as D in ps, kill -9 has been run three times, and it is still listed. wchan shows nfs_wait_bit_killable. What is happening, and what are the options?

Solution

It is in uninterruptible sleep inside a kernel call, waiting on an NFS server that is not responding. Signals — including SIGKILL — are recorded as pending but cannot be delivered until the process leaves kernel mode, and it cannot leave until the I/O completes or the call gives up. The process is not ignoring you; the kernel has not had an opportunity to hand it the signal.

wchan naming an NFS function is the confirmation, and it is why that column is worth adding to ps -eo.

Options, in order of preference:

  1. Fix the I/O. Restore the NFS server or the network path. Every stuck process completes and the pending SIGKILL is delivered immediately. This is the only clean answer.
  2. umount -f -l /mnt/nfs. Force and lazy: detaches the mount so pending operations fail with EIO rather than waiting. Frequently enough to unwedge things.
  3. Reboot. Genuinely the standard answer for a hard-mounted NFS share whose server is gone, and not an admission of defeat.

Prevention is a mount option: soft with a timeo= makes NFS operations fail with an error instead of waiting forever, and intr (on kernels that honour it) allows signals. The trade-off is real — soft can return a short read to an application that does not check, which is why databases are always mounted hard. Choose per workload rather than globally.

The general lesson: kill -9 is guaranteed to destroy the process, not to do so immediately. State D is the exception people meet.

Exercise 7.2 — Ten seconds every time. A containerised service takes exactly 10.0 seconds to stop, every time, and its logs show no shutdown messages. Explain, and give two fixes.

Solution

The application is PID 1 in the container and installs no SIGTERM handler. The kernel does not apply default signal actions to PID 1, so SIGTERM — whose default action would be "terminate" for any other process — is simply discarded. docker stop waits its full default timeout of 10 seconds and then sends SIGKILL, which PID 1 cannot ignore.

The exactness of "10.0 seconds every time" is the tell: a timeout, not work.

The absence of shutdown log lines is the second tell: the process never began shutting down, and SIGKILL runs no cleanup.

Fix 1 — handle the signal. In the application, install a handler that stops accepting connections, drains in-flight requests and exits. This is the right fix, because it is also what makes rolling deploys not drop requests.

Fix 2 — give the container a real init. docker run --init inserts tini as PID 1; it forwards signals to your process (which then gets normal PID semantics, so the default action applies) and reaps orphans (§7.5). In a Dockerfile, ENTRYPOINT ["tini", "--"].

Two related traps worth checking at the same time. If the entrypoint is a shell command string, sh -c becomes PID 1 and does not forward signals to its child — use the JSON exec form so the binary is PID 1. And if the process legitimately needs more than 10 seconds to drain, raise the timeout (--stop-timeout, or terminationGracePeriodSeconds) as well as handling the signal, or the fix changes a silent kill into a slightly later silent kill.

Exercise 7.3 — Nice does nothing. A nightly rsync backup is started with nice -n 19. The API on the same machine still shows p99 latency tripling while it runs, and CPU usage during the window is under 20%. Explain and fix.

Solution

Niceness only affects CPU scheduling, and the CPU is not the contended resource. rsync reads and writes large volumes of data; the contention is in the block layer, where a nice-19 process's requests sit in the same device queue as everyone else's. With CPU at 20%, the diagnosis is already visible in the question — the API is waiting on disk, not on the scheduler.

Confirm with iostat -xz 1 (look at %util and await on the device) or top's wa column, and with cat /sys/fs/cgroup/io.pressure for time actually lost to I/O stalls.

Fix:

nice -n 19 ionice -c 3 rsync -a /data/ /backup/

ionice -c 3 is the idle class: the backup gets device time only when nothing else wants it. Keep the nice as well; it costs nothing.

More robust, as a systemd unit, because it survives someone editing the cron line:

[Service]
Nice=19
IOSchedulingClass=idle
IOWeight=10
MemoryHigh=1G

IOWeight is the cgroup-level control (§15.8) and applies to every process the unit spawns, which ionice on the parent does not guarantee. MemoryHigh is worth adding for a different reason: a large rsync fills the page cache with backup data and evicts the API's working set, so the latency can persist after the backup finishes. Capping the backup's memory keeps the cache warm for the service that needs it.

Also consider --bwlimit on rsync itself. Rate-limiting at the application is cruder but entirely predictable, and on a network-bound backup it is the knob that matters.

Exercise 7.4 — Read the table. Interpret this output and say what you would do next.

    PID    PPID USER     STAT  NI   RSS     ELAPSED COMMAND
   4821       1 www-data Ssl    0 1204832  21-04:11 gunicorn
  19022    4821 www-data Z      0       0     18:22 gunicorn
  19023    4821 www-data Z      0       0     18:19 gunicorn
  19024    4821 www-data Z      0       0     18:15 gunicorn
  29981    4821 www-data D      0  502100      02:14 gunicorn
Solution

Four separate findings, and they are probably one story.

1 — Three zombies with the same parent. State Z, RSS 0, PPID 4821. These children have exited and the master has not called wait(). Three is not a crisis, but the pattern is: they are minutes apart, so they are accumulating steadily and will exhaust PIDs eventually. The action is never kill -9 on a zombie — it is already dead. The parent is the problem.

2 — The master has been up 21 days and holds 1.2 GB RSS. For a process whose job is to supervise workers, that is a lot, and combined with the un-reaped children it suggests the master is stuck or looping rather than doing its supervisory work.

3 — One worker in state D for two minutes. Blocked in the kernel on I/O. cat /proc/29981/wchan and cat /proc/29981/stack name what it is waiting on; ls -l /proc/29981/fd and strace -p 29981 (which will show the call it never returns from) narrow it further. A disk, a network filesystem, or a database connection over a dead path.

4 — Workers are short-lived. 18 minutes of elapsed time on children of a 21-day master means they are being recycled, which is either configured (max_requests) or crashes.

What to do next, in order:

cat /proc/29981/wchan; cat /proc/29981/stack
ls -l /proc/4821/fd | wc -l; grep 'Max open files' /proc/4821/limits
journalctl -u gunicorn --since '30 min ago' -p warning

The hypothesis worth testing first is that the master is itself blocked — on a full pipe to a log file that nothing is draining, or on the same I/O the D worker is stuck on — which would explain both the missing reaps and the worker churn in one cause. If the descriptor count is near the limit, that is a second, independent problem to fix.

The immediate mitigation is a rolling restart of the master (systemctl reload if it supports SIGHUP re-exec, otherwise restart), which clears the zombies and the stuck worker. That fixes the symptom; the wchan output is what fixes the cause.

SECTION 8

The Kernel Interface: /proc, /sys, Devices and Modules

Everything a program does that touches the outside world — reading a file, sending a packet, creating a process, asking the time — is a system call: a controlled jump from user space into the kernel, which does the work on the program's behalf and returns. That boundary is the only thing separating your code from the hardware, and this section is about looking at it from both sides.

Linux exposes an unusual amount of its own state as files. Not files on a disk — files that do not exist until you read them, generated on demand by the kernel out of live data structures. /proc and /sys are the two big ones, and once you can read them, a very large class of question stops needing a tool at all. "How much memory is this process using", "which files does it have open", "what is the scheduler doing", "is this disk rotational" — all of them are cat.

8.1 The Syscall Boundary

A process runs in user mode, where the CPU forbids it from touching hardware, other processes' memory, or the page tables. To do anything real, it puts a syscall number in a register, its arguments in others, and executes a syscall instruction. The CPU switches to kernel mode, jumps to a fixed entry point, the kernel validates every argument, does the work, and returns.

Two consequences follow, and both shape how fast software is written.

A syscall costs. The transition, the argument validation, and the cache effects of running kernel code total a few hundred nanoseconds — hundreds of times a function call. That is why read() on a buffered stream does not call read() per byte, why printf buffers, and why a program doing a million one-byte writes is ten times slower than one doing a thousand thousand-byte writes.

The syscall list is the kernel's entire attack surface, which is why filtering it with seccomp (§15.10) is such a cheap security win.

strace shows the boundary directly. Ask for a summary of everything /bin/true does:

strace -c -f /bin/true

  4.09    0.000020          10         2           close
  3.48    0.000017           8         2           fstat
  2.86    0.000014          14         1           read
  2.66    0.000013          13         1           set_tid_address
  2.45    0.000012          12         1           set_robust_list
  2.25    0.000011          11         1           brk
  2.25    0.000011          11         1           prlimit64
  2.25    0.000011          11         1           getrandom
  1.84    0.000009           9         1           arch_prctl
  1.64    0.000008           8         1         1 access
  1.64    0.000008           8         1           rseq
  0.00    0.000000           0         1           execve
------ ----------- ----------- --------- --------- ----------------
100.00    0.000489          16        30         1 total

Thirty syscalls to do nothing at all. Almost every one of them is the dynamic loader finding and mapping libc (§12.8) before main is reached — which is the honest cost of starting any dynamically linked program, and the reason a shell loop that forks a process per line is slow in a way no amount of clever shell code will fix.

8.2 /proc: the Process Table as a Filesystem

/proc is a procfs mount, generated entirely on demand. Its numbered directories are processes; /proc/self is a magic symlink to whichever process is reading it, which is why examples can use it without knowing a PID.

ls /proc/self | head -30

arch_status
attr
autogroup
auxv
cgroup
clear_refs
cmdline
comm
coredump_filter
cpu_resctrl_groups
cwd
environ
exe
fd
fdinfo
gid_map
io
ksm_merging_pages
ksm_stat
latency
limits
loginuid
map_files
maps
mem
mountinfo
mounts
mountstats
net
ns

The ones worth knowing, and what each answers:

status
Human-readable summary: UIDs, GIDs, memory, thread count, capability sets, Seccomp, NoNewPrivs. The first file to read about any process.
cmdline
The full argument vector, NUL-separated. Immune to the truncation ps applies.
environ
The environment the process was started with — not its current one. Readable only by the owner and root, because it routinely contains secrets.
cwd, exe, root
Symlinks to the working directory, the executable and the process's root. exe still resolves after the binary is deleted, which is how you identify a process running an upgraded-away version.
fd/
One symlink per open file descriptor. The single most useful directory in /proc.
maps
The memory map: every mapped region, its permissions, and its backing file.
limits
Effective rlimits, soft and hard. Answers "did my ulimit change actually apply to this process".
io
Bytes read and written, and how many actually reached the block layer.
ns/
One symlink per namespace (§15.2).
cgroup
Which cgroup the process is in (§15.7).

fd/ earns its reputation:

ls -l /proc/self/fd

lr-x------ 1 dev dev 64 Aug 26 20:27 0 -> /dev/null
l-wx------ 1 dev dev 64 Aug 26 20:27 1 -> pipe:[119026]
l-wx------ 1 dev dev 64 Aug 26 20:27 2 -> /tmp/errors.log
lr-x------ 1 dev dev 64 Aug 26 20:27 3 -> /proc/49913/fd

Descriptor 0 is standard input, redirected from /dev/null; 1 is standard output, which is a pipe because this command's output is being captured; 2 is standard error, going to a file. Descriptor 3 is the ls process reading the directory it is listing — a small piece of self-reference that shows how live this filesystem is.

Two things this makes possible that nothing else does. Recovering a deleted file that is still open: a process holding an open descriptor to an unlinked file keeps the inode alive, and cp /proc/PID/fd/N /recovered copies it out — the classic save after someone deletes a log file that a daemon is still writing to, where the disk space also does not come back until the process closes it. And counting descriptors to catch a leak: ls /proc/PID/fd | wc -l against the Max open files line of /proc/PID/limits is the diagnosis for "too many open files" in one line.

head -6 /proc/self/limits

Limit                     Soft Limit           Hard Limit           Units
Max cpu time              unlimited            unlimited            seconds
Max file size             unlimited            unlimited            bytes
Max data size             unlimited            unlimited            bytes
Max stack size            8388608              unlimited            bytes
Max core file size        0                    unlimited            bytes

Note Max core file size is 0 — core dumps are disabled for this shell, which is worth knowing before you go looking for one after a crash (§7.4).

8.3 /proc's System-Wide Files

The non-numeric entries in /proc describe the machine rather than a process.

cat /proc/loadavg

0.34 1.39 2.03 1/226 49531

Five fields: the 1-, 5- and 15-minute load averages, then running/total kernel scheduling entities, then the last PID allocated. The three averages are a gradient, and reading them as one is the point — 0.34 1.39 2.03 is a machine that was busy and is recovering, and it means something completely different from 2.03 1.39 0.34, which is one getting into trouble. On Linux, note that load counts processes in uninterruptible sleep (state D, usually blocked on I/O) as well as runnable ones, so a high load average on Linux does not necessarily mean the CPU is busy at all — a stuck NFS mount produces a load of 50 on an idle machine.

head -6 /proc/meminfo

MemTotal:        3480372 kB
MemFree:          657456 kB
MemAvailable:    2081488 kB
Buffers:           39108 kB
Cached:          1584580 kB
SwapCached:         1900 kB

/proc/meminfo has around fifty lines and four of them carry most of the information. MemFree is not the number you want; MemAvailable is. Free memory is wasted memory, and Linux deliberately fills it with page cache — here, 1.58 GB of Cached. MemAvailable is the kernel's own estimate of what a new allocation could get, counting reclaimable cache. Every "my server has no free memory!" panic is a misreading of the first number instead of the third.

The other /proc/meminfo lines worth recognising are Dirty (data written but not yet flushed to disk — a large and growing value means the writeback is behind), Slab (kernel data structures, which a leak in a driver will grow without bound), and Committed_AS against CommitLimit, which is the overcommit accounting of §8.8.

The rest of the useful set:

/proc/cpuinfo
One block per logical CPU, with model, flags and current MHz.
/proc/mounts
What is mounted right now, from the kernel — unlike /etc/mtab, it cannot be stale.
/proc/mountinfo
The same, plus mount IDs, propagation flags and the source subtree. What you need for bind mounts and namespaces.
/proc/cmdline
The kernel command line this machine booted with (§9.2).
/proc/modules
Loaded modules; what lsmod formats.
/proc/interrupts
Interrupt counts per CPU per device. Where you find an interrupt storm.
/proc/diskstats
Per-device I/O counters; the source for iostat.
/proc/net/*
Sockets, routes, ARP cache — the source for ss and ip, rendered per network namespace.
/proc/sys/
Writable tunables. The same tree sysctl edits (§8.8).
cat /proc/cmdline

BOOT_IMAGE=/vmlinuz-7.0.0-30-generic root=/dev/mapper/ubuntu--vg-ubuntu--lv ro crashkernel=2G-4G:320M,4G-32G:512M,32G-64G:1024M,64G-128G:2048M,128G-:4096M

That one line says the root filesystem is an LVM logical volume (§10.5), that it was mounted read-only at boot and remounted later, and that a crash kernel is reserved on a sliding scale by RAM size.

8.4 /sys: the Device Model

/proc grew organically and mixes process information with kernel tunables and device state. sysfs was created to do the device half properly: one file per attribute, one directory per object, and a strict structure.

ls /sys

block
bus
class
dev
devices
firmware
fs
hypervisor
kernel
module
power

Three small tools read that tree for you rather than making you walk it. lspci lists every device on the PCI bus — network cards, storage controllers, GPUs — with lspci -k adding the kernel module currently driving each one, which is the fastest answer to "is this card supported by this kernel". lsusb does the same for USB, with lsusb -t drawing the tree of hubs and devices. Both read from /sys and /proc/bus, so neither needs privilege for the basic listing; lspci -vv and lsusb -v want root to read the deeper configuration registers.

The pattern is worth noticing, because the whole family is named the same way: lsblk for block devices (§10.1), lscpu, lsmem, lsns (§15.2), lsof (§11.10), and lspci/lsusb here. When you want to know what a machine has, ls plus the thing is usually a real command.

/sys/devices is the truth — a tree mirroring how devices physically hang off buses. Everything else is a set of symlink views into it: /sys/block by block device, /sys/class by function (net, tty, power_supply), /sys/bus by bus (pci, usb), /sys/dev by major:minor number. The same device appears in several of them, which is a feature: you approach it from whichever direction you know.

cat /sys/block/sda/size /sys/block/sda/queue/rotational /sys/block/sda/queue/scheduler

182255616
1
none [mq-deadline]

Three facts, three files. size is in 512-byte sectors regardless of the device's real sector size — so this is 182255616 × 512 = 93,314,875,392 bytes, and multiplying by 4096 because the disk has 4K sectors is a classic wrong answer. That is 93.3 GB in the decimal units a drive is sold in and 86.9 GiB in the binary units lsblk prints (§10.1), which is the same disk twice and the origin of most "my new drive is smaller than advertised" complaints. rotational is 1, so the kernel believes this is a spinning disk. And the I/O scheduler in use is mq-deadline, with the alternatives listed and the active one in brackets.

That third file is writable, which is the sysfs pattern: echo bfq > /sys/block/sda/queue/scheduler changes the scheduler immediately, and the change is lost at reboot unless a udev rule sets it. Almost every performance knob for storage, networking and power management is a file like this one.

8.5 Device Files, Major and Minor Numbers

A device file has no contents. It is an inode carrying a type and a pair of numbers, and opening it connects you to a driver:

ls -l /dev/sda /dev/null /dev/urandom /dev/tty

crw-rw-rw- 1 root root 1, 3 Aug 26 19:38 /dev/null
brw-rw---- 1 root disk 8, 0 Aug 26 19:38 /dev/sda
crw-rw-rw- 1 root tty  5, 0 Aug 26 19:58 /dev/tty
crw-rw-rw- 1 root root 1, 9 Aug 26 19:38 /dev/urandom

The first character is the type — b for block, c for character — and where a regular file shows a size, these show two numbers.

Block devices are addressed in fixed-size blocks, can be seeked, and go through the kernel's buffer cache and I/O scheduler. Disks and their partitions. A filesystem can be mounted on one.
Character devices are byte streams with no block structure and usually no seeking. Terminals, serial ports, /dev/null, /dev/urandom, sound devices.

A character device is the one you meet most often without noticing: every terminal, every pseudo-terminal behind an SSH session, /dev/random, /dev/zero and the sound devices are character devices, and a character device is what a program gets when it opens a serial port. The major number and minor number are how the kernel routes an open on that node to a driver.

The major number selects the driver — 8 is SCSI/SATA disks, 1 is memory devices, 5 is terminals — and the minor selects which device that driver handles. /dev/sda is 8,0 and /dev/sda1 would be 8,1: same driver, different unit. The major number appears in the MAJ:MIN column of lsblk (§10.1) and in the fifth column of ls -l on any device node, and it is the number io.max uses to name a device in a cgroup (§15.8) — which is why a cgroup I/O limit is written 8:0 rbps=1048576 rather than with a path.

The permission bits on device nodes are the real access control for hardware. /dev/sda is brw-rw---- owned by root:disk, so membership of the disk group is read and write access to the raw disk — which is every file on it, regardless of file permissions. That is the same shape of hazard as the docker group in §15.9: a group membership that is quietly equivalent to root.

8.6 udev: How /dev Gets Populated

/dev is a devtmpfs — created by the kernel at boot, and populated dynamically. When a device appears, the kernel emits a uevent; systemd-udevd receives it, applies rules from /usr/lib/udev/rules.d/ and /etc/udev/rules.d/, and creates the node, sets its ownership and permissions, and adds symlinks.

udevadm info shows what udev knows about a device — and this is the way to discover the attributes you can match on:

udevadm info --query=property --name=/dev/sda

DEVPATH=/devices/pci0000:00/0000:00:0d.0/ata3/host2/target2:0:0/2:0:0:0/block/sda
DEVNAME=/dev/sda
DEVTYPE=disk
DISKSEQ=11
MAJOR=8
MINOR=0
SUBSYSTEM=block
USEC_INITIALIZED=10678902
SCSI_TPGS=0
SCSI_TYPE=disk
SCSI_VENDOR=ATA

DEVPATH is the path in /sys/devices — the physical position of the device, through PCI to the ATA controller to the port. That path is why udev can distinguish "the disk in bay 3" from "the disk that happened to enumerate first", and why persistent naming works at all.

A rule is a list of match keys and assignment keys on one line:

SUBSYSTEM=="block", ATTRS{queue/rotational}=="0", ATTR{queue/scheduler}="none"

== matches, = assigns. That rule sets the I/O scheduler to none on every non-rotational device, at the moment it appears — the persistent version of the echo in §8.4.

Two things udev does that you rely on daily without noticing. Persistent names: /dev/sda depends on probe order and can change between boots, so udev creates stable symlinks under /dev/disk/by-uuid/, /dev/disk/by-label/, /dev/disk/by-id/ and /dev/disk/by-path/. This is precisely why /etc/fstab should use UUID= rather than /dev/sdb1 (§10.7) — the UUID is a property of the filesystem, and it follows the disk into a different port. And predictable network interface names: enp0s3 is not a random string, it encodes the PCI bus and slot, so an interface cannot become eth1 after a reboot and take your firewall rules with it.

To debug a rule, udevadm monitor prints events live while you plug something in, and udevadm test /sys/class/block/sda replays the rule evaluation and shows which rules matched.

8.7 Kernel Modules

The kernel is one program, but most of it can be loaded on demand. A module is a compiled object loaded into the running kernel's address space, with full kernel privilege — there is no isolation between a module and the rest of the kernel, which is why loading one requires CAP_SYS_MODULE and why an unsigned module can be refused entirely under Secure Boot.

lsmod | head -5

Module                  Size  Used by
nf_conntrack_netlink    61440  0
veth                   45056  0
tcp_diag               20480  0
inet_diag              24576  1 tcp_diag

The third column is a reference count and the list of dependants. inet_diag shows 1 tcp_diag, meaning one module depends on it — so inet_diag cannot be removed until tcp_diag is. A non-zero count with no names listed usually means the kernel itself, or an open device, is holding it.

Note veth in that list: virtual Ethernet pairs, loaded because Docker is installed. Reading lsmod tells you what the machine has actually been asked to do.

modinfo overlay

filename:       /lib/modules/7.0.0-30-generic/kernel/fs/overlayfs/overlay.ko.zst
alias:          fs-overlay
license:        GPL
description:    Overlay filesystem
author:         Miklos Szeredi <miklos@szeredi.hu>
srcversion:     D03D38E693FA8175265FC53
depends:
intree:         Y

The commands, and the distinction that matters:

lsmod
What is loaded. Formats /proc/modules.
modinfo
Metadata for a module, loaded or not — including its parameters, which modinfo -p lists.
modprobe
Load or unload by name, resolving dependencies. Reads /etc/modprobe.d/. The one to use.
insmod / rmmod
Load or unload a single file with no dependency resolution. Debugging tools; using them where modprobe would do is how you get "unknown symbol" errors.
depmod
Rebuilds the dependency database (modules.dep). Run automatically when a kernel package is installed.

insmod and rmmod are the raw pair beneath modprobe: insmod /path/to/module.ko loads exactly that file and nothing else, and rmmod name unloads exactly that module. Because insmod resolves no dependencies, loading a module whose symbols live in another produces unknown symbol in module, and because rmmod refuses when the reference count is non-zero, unloading in the wrong order fails; modprobe and modprobe -r do both correctly and are what you should type.

/etc/modprobe.d/ holds configuration, and blacklisting is its main use on a working machine:

ls /etc/modprobe.d/ | head -5

amd64-microcode-blacklist.conf
blacklist-ath_pci.conf
blacklist-firewire.conf
blacklist-framebuffer.conf
blacklist-rare-network.conf

One caveat with a long history: blacklist foo prevents automatic loading by alias, but not loading as a dependency of something else, and not an explicit modprobe foo. To make a module genuinely unloadable, the line is install foo /bin/false.

8.8 sysctl: Tunables That Are Also Files

Everything under /proc/sys is a kernel parameter, and sysctl is a thin wrapper that turns dots into slashes.

sysctl vm.swappiness net.ipv4.ip_forward fs.file-max kernel.pid_max

vm.swappiness = 60
net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1
fs.file-max = 9223372036854775807
kernel.pid_max = 4194304

sysctl vm.swappiness and cat /proc/sys/vm/swappiness are the same operation. Setting one at runtime is sysctl -w vm.swappiness=10 or echo 10 > /proc/sys/vm/swappiness; both are lost at reboot. Persistence is a file in /etc/sysctl.d/ ending in .conf, applied at boot and re-applied by sysctl --system.

Note net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1 above — this machine routes packets between interfaces, because installing Docker turned it on. A sysctl reading something unexpected is often a package's doing rather than a person's.

The ones worth recognising:

vm.swappiness
0–200, default 60. How eagerly the kernel swaps anonymous pages rather than evicting page cache. Lower on a database host; it is a preference, not a switch, and 0 does not disable swap.
vm.overcommit_memory
Whether malloc may promise more memory than exists. The default (0, heuristic) is why a successful allocation can still be killed later by the OOM killer.
net.ipv4.ip_forward
Route between interfaces. Required for NAT, containers and any router role.
net.core.somaxconn
Maximum accept-queue length. The ceiling that silently truncates an application's listen() backlog.
fs.file-max
System-wide open-file limit. Effectively unlimited here; per-process RLIMIT_NOFILE (§6.11) is nearly always the real constraint.
kernel.pid_max
Highest PID before wrapping. 4194304 on 64-bit, which is why PIDs on a busy machine are seven digits.
kernel.dmesg_restrict
Whether unprivileged users may read the kernel log (§8.9).

8.9 dmesg and the Kernel Ring Buffer

The kernel cannot write to a log file — it has no filesystem when it starts and no business blocking on I/O later. It writes to a fixed-size ring buffer in memory, and dmesg prints it.

This is where hardware speaks: disk errors, USB devices appearing, OOM kills with their scoring, filesystem remounts to read-only, network link state, and segfaults with the faulting address. Being a ring, it is finite: on a machine that has been up for months with a chatty driver, the boot messages are long gone, and this is exactly what journald's -k flag solves by persisting kernel messages (§11.2).

dmesg

dmesg: read kernel buffer failed: Operation not permitted

That failure is itself the lesson. Modern distributions set kernel.dmesg_restrict=1, so an unprivileged user cannot read the kernel log — it leaks kernel addresses useful for exploiting kernel bugs. sudo dmesg works, and so does journalctl -k for users in the adm or systemd-journal groups. The flags worth knowing are -T (human timestamps instead of seconds since boot), -w (follow), and -l err,warn (severity filter).

8.10 strace: Watching a Program Talk to the Kernel

strace is the tool for "it does not work and the error message is useless", because it shows exactly which operation failed and on what.

The default output is one line per syscall, with arguments decoded and the return value:

strace -e trace=openat /bin/echo hi

openat(AT_FDCWD, "/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6", O_RDONLY|O_CLOEXEC) = 3
openat(AT_FDCWD, "/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libpcre2-8.so.0", O_RDONLY|O_CLOEXEC) = 3
openat(AT_FDCWD, "/proc/filesystems", O_RDONLY|O_CLOEXEC) = 3
openat(AT_FDCWD, "/proc/self/maps", O_RDONLY|O_CLOEXEC) = 3
openat(AT_FDCWD, "/usr/share/coreutils/locales/uucore/en-US.ftl", O_RDONLY|O_CLOEXEC) = 3
openat(AT_FDCWD, "/usr/share/coreutils/locales/echo/en-US.ftl", O_RDONLY|O_CLOEXEC) = -1 ENOENT (No such file or directory)
hi
+++ exited with 0 +++

Look at the fifth line. echo opened /usr/share/coreutils/locales/uucore/en-US.ftl — a Fluent localisation file, which the GNU implementation does not have. This machine's echo is the Rust uutils implementation, which Ubuntu ships as the default coreutils from 25.10 onward; the GNU package is installed alongside it. Nothing else in this course would have revealed that, and it matters, because uutils and GNU coreutils differ in small ways at the edges of option handling and error text. strace did not just show a syscall — it identified which program you are actually running.

The last line, = -1 ENOENT, shows the other half of what makes strace useful: a syscall that failed, the errno, and its text. A program that says "cannot load configuration" while strace shows openat(... "/etc/foo/config.yml") = -1 ENOENT has told you in one line what an hour of reading its source would.

The flags that matter, and when:

-f
Follow forks. Nearly always needed — without it you trace a shell wrapper and see nothing.
-p PID
Attach to a running process. The tool for "it is hung and I do not know where".
-e trace=openat,stat
Filter by syscall. Also groups: %file, %network, %process, %memory.
-c
Summary table instead of a trace. Where time and syscall count actually went.
-y
Print the path behind each file descriptor, so read(3, ...) becomes readable.
-s 2048
Show more than 32 bytes of string arguments. Essential when reading what was written to a socket.
-T, -tt
Time spent in each call; wall-clock timestamps. For finding the one call that took four seconds.

ltrace is strace's counterpart for library calls. Where strace shows the boundary between the process and the kernel, ltrace shows the boundary between the program and its shared libraries (§12.8) — every malloc, strcmp and getenv. That makes ltrace useful for a different question: not "which file did it fail to open" but "what did it do with the string before it gave up". It is slower and more fragile than strace, it works only on dynamically linked binaries, and it is often not installed — but when a program is failing inside a library rather than at a syscall, ltrace is the tool that sees it.

Three honest limits. strace is slow — it stops the process at every syscall, and a syscall-heavy program can run 20× slower, which is enough to make a timing-dependent bug vanish. It cannot see anything that is not a syscall, so a CPU-bound loop or a hot mutex is invisible; perf is the tool there. And attaching requires CAP_SYS_PTRACE or the same UID, with kernel.yama.ptrace_scope restricting even same-UID attachment to direct children on many distributions — the reason strace -p on your own process can still say "Operation not permitted".

The habitual workflow is worth stating as a recipe, because it solves a specific and very common class of problem: when a program fails with a bad error message, run strace -f -e trace=%file, look for the last failing call before the error is printed, and read the path. Nine times in ten it is a file in the wrong place, a permission on a directory rather than the file, or a socket that is not there.

Exercises

Exercise 8.1 — Read the process. A service is misbehaving and you have its PID in $PID. Without installing anything, answer these from /proc alone: (a) what command line it was started with, (b) how many files it has open and what its limit is, (c) whether it is confined by seccomp, (d) which directory it considers its working directory, and (e) whether its binary has been deleted from disk since it started.

Solution 
tr '\0' ' ' < /proc/"$PID"/cmdline; echo
ls /proc/"$PID"/fd | wc -l
grep 'Max open files' /proc/"$PID"/limits
grep -E '^(Seccomp|NoNewPrivs)' /proc/"$PID"/status
readlink /proc/"$PID"/cwd
readlink /proc/"$PID"/exe

(a) cmdline is NUL-separated, so cat runs the arguments together — tr '\0' ' ' is the standard fix. This beats ps output, which truncates.

(b) The count from fd/ against the soft limit from limits. A count sitting just under the limit is a descriptor leak, and ls -l /proc/$PID/fd | awk '{print $NF}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn tells you what is leaking — usually sockets to one host, or the same log file opened repeatedly.

(c) Seccomp: 2 means a filter is installed (§15.10). 0 means none.

(d) cwd is a symlink. This is how you discover that a service writing to data/output.csv is writing it into /, because it was started by an init script that never changed directory.

(e) readlink /proc/$PID/exe appends (deleted) when the binary has been replaced or removed. That is the diagnosis for "I upgraded the package but the old behaviour is still there" — the running process is still executing the old inode, and only a restart picks up the new one. lsof +L1 finds every such process at once.

Exercise 8.2 — Free memory panic. A monitoring alert fires: "server has only 657 MB free of 3.4 GB". /proc/meminfo shows MemFree: 657456 kB, MemAvailable: 2081488 kB, Cached: 1584580 kB. Is there a problem? What should the alert have measured, and what would be a real warning sign?

Solution

No problem. 1.58 GB of the "missing" memory is page cache — file data the kernel is keeping around because the memory would otherwise be idle. It is reclaimable on demand: an allocation that needs it gets it, at the cost of a later disk read. MemAvailable at 2.08 GB is the kernel's own estimate of what a new allocation could obtain, and it is the number to alert on.

The alert should measure MemAvailable as a fraction of MemTotal — here 60%, which is healthy. Free memory on Linux is not a resource being saved; it is a resource not yet being used, and a well-run server tends toward low MemFree over time.

Real warning signs, in order of severity:

  • MemAvailable falling toward zero while MemTotal is unchanged. That is genuine pressure.
  • Swap in/out rate, from vmstat 1's si/so columns. Swap being used is normal; swap being actively paged is the machine thrashing. The distinction is why "swap used: 2 GB" is not itself an alert.
  • PSI: some avg60 in /proc/pressure/memory (§15.8) is time actually lost to memory stalls, which is the direct measurement of the harm rather than a proxy for it.
  • oom_kill counts in the cgroup's memory.events, or Killed process lines in dmesg.

The general trap: MemFree, disk %util and CPU %idle are all metrics whose "bad" value is often perfectly healthy. Alert on the stall, not the utilisation.

Exercise 8.3 — The device that moved. After adding a second disk, a machine fails to boot: /etc/fstab has /dev/sdb1 /data ext4 defaults 0 2, and /data is now empty while the new disk holds the data. Explain what happened and give the durable fix.

Solution

Kernel device names are assigned in probe order, not by identity. sda, sdb and sdc reflect whichever controller answered first on this particular boot, and adding a disk — or a slower disk that enumerates later, or a controller firmware update — reshuffles them. The old data disk is now /dev/sdc1, and /dev/sdb1 is the new empty one, mounted at /data exactly as instructed.

The fix is to name the filesystem rather than the port:

lsblk -o NAME,SIZE,FSTYPE,UUID,LABEL,MOUNTPOINT
blkid /dev/sdc1

then in /etc/fstab:

UUID=1b3f0a12-... /data ext4 defaults,nofail,x-systemd.device-timeout=10 0 2

The UUID is stored inside the filesystem's superblock, so it follows the data into any port on any machine. LABEL= works the same way and is more readable, at the cost of being easy to duplicate by accident.

nofail is the other half of the answer. Without it, a /etc/fstab entry whose device is missing stops the boot at an emergency prompt — which is correct for / and /var and disastrous for a data disk on a remote machine with no console. nofail plus a short x-systemd.device-timeout means a missing data disk produces a degraded boot you can log into and fix.

Where to look for stable names: /dev/disk/by-uuid/ (filesystem identity, changes on reformat), /dev/disk/by-id/ (hardware serial, follows the physical drive), and /dev/disk/by-path/ (the physical slot, which is what you want for "the disk in bay 3" in a hot-swap chassis).

Exercise 8.4 — Trace the failure. A CLI tool exits with Error: could not load configuration and no further detail. It works for you and fails for a colleague. Design the investigation using this section's tools, and say what you would expect each of the three most likely causes to look like.

Solution 
strace -f -e trace=%file -o /tmp/trace.txt the-tool --whatever
grep -n 'ENOENT\|EACCES\|EPERM' /tmp/trace.txt | tail -20

Trace file operations, follow forks, write to a file so the program's own output is not tangled with the trace, then look at the last failures before the error. The three shapes, and how they differ on the screen:

1 — Wrong path. A run of openat(...) = -1 ENOENT walking a search list, ending with the file never being found:

openat(AT_FDCWD, "/home/alice/.config/tool/config.yml", O_RDONLY) = -1 ENOENT (No such file or directory)
openat(AT_FDCWD, "/etc/tool/config.yml", O_RDONLY) = -1 ENOENT (No such file or directory)

This is a configuration problem, and the trace has just told you every path the tool will accept.

2 — Permissions. A single EACCES on a path that exists:

openat(AT_FDCWD, "/etc/tool/config.yml", O_RDONLY) = -1 EACCES (Permission denied)

Check the file and every directory above it — a missing x bit on a parent directory denies access to everything beneath it (§6.3), and this is the case people miss because the file's own permissions look fine.

3 — It found the file and could not parse it. openat returns a descriptor, read succeeds, and the error comes afterwards. Then the difference between the two machines is the file's content, and -s 2048 will show you what was read.

Why it works for you: almost always an environment variable in your shell profile that your colleague does not have — strace -f -e trace=execve shows the exact environment, and tr '\0' '\n' < /proc/PID/environ on a running instance does the same. -e trace=%file is the right first filter because these three causes are all file operations, and it cuts the noise by an order of magnitude compared with a full trace.

SECTION 9

Boot, PID 1, systemd and Scheduled Work

A running Linux system is the result of a chain of programs, each of which exists only to load the next one and then get out of the way. Firmware finds a bootloader, the bootloader loads a kernel, the kernel mounts a temporary root and starts one process, and that process starts everything else and never exits. Understanding that chain is what turns "the server won't come up" from a panic into a bisection: you know which of five stages you are in, because each one fails visibly differently.

The last link in the chain — PID 1 — is systemd on essentially every current distribution, and it is far more than an init system. It is the machine's service manager, its dependency resolver, its logger, its resource-limit enforcer, its scheduler and its sandbox front end. This section covers the chain, then covers systemd properly, and finishes with the two ways to make something happen later: timers and cron.

9.1 Firmware to PID 1: the Chain in Five Links

1 · Firmware
BIOS or UEFI. Power-on self test, then find something bootable. BIOS reads the first 512 bytes of a disk and executes them; UEFI reads a filesystem and executes a named .efi program. The difference matters, and §9.2 explains why.
2 · Bootloader
GRUB 2 almost always. Presents a menu, loads a kernel image and an initramfs into memory, hands the kernel a command line, and jumps to it.
3 · Kernel
Decompresses itself, initialises the CPUs and memory, brings up built-in drivers, unpacks the initramfs into a RAM filesystem and mounts it as /.
4 · initramfs
A tiny userspace whose only job is to make the real root filesystem reachable: load the storage and filesystem modules, assemble RAID or LVM, unlock LUKS, then pivot_root onto the real root (§15.6) and exec the real init.
5 · PID 1
systemd. Reads units, resolves dependencies, starts everything the default target requires, and stays alive as the parent of last resort for the machine's whole lifetime.

Which firmware a machine uses is a one-line question, because UEFI exposes a directory that BIOS cannot:

[ -d /sys/firmware/efi ] && echo UEFI || echo BIOS

BIOS

That test is worth memorising. Half of all bootloader repair advice on the internet applies to exactly one of the two, and the first thing to establish is which one you are in.

9.2 The Bootloader and the Kernel Command Line

BIOS boot is the older scheme. The firmware loads the first 512-byte sector of the disk — the MBR — into memory and executes it. 512 bytes is not enough for a bootloader, so GRUB's MBR stage is a stub that locates and loads a larger stage from a gap after the partition table or from a /boot partition. The partition table itself lives in those same 512 bytes, which is where the four-primary-partition limit and the 2 TB disk limit come from (§10.2).

UEFI boot replaces all of that with something less baroque: the firmware understands FAT32, reads a dedicated EFI System Partition — the ESP, conventionally mounted at /boot/efi — and executes a .efi binary from it. No sector-level trickery, multiple bootloaders coexisting as ordinary files, and a firmware-level boot order stored in NVRAM and editable with efibootmgr.

The EFI System Partition is a FAT32 partition, conventionally 512 MiB, flagged with the ESP partition type and mounted at /boot/efi. Because the EFI System Partition is plain FAT, its contents can be read and repaired from any operating system, which is what makes UEFI recovery so much less painful than reconstructing an MBR by hand.

GRUB 2 is configured indirectly, and this is the part that catches people:

/etc/default/grub
The file you edit. Timeout, default entry, and GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX_DEFAULT — the kernel parameters appended to every normal entry.
/etc/grub.d/
Scripts that generate menu entries — one per kernel found, plus os-prober results.
/boot/grub/grub.cfg
The generated output. Never edit this file; the next kernel update overwrites it.
grub-mkconfig
Runs the scripts and writes grub.cfg. Debian and Ubuntu wrap it as update-grub.
grub-install
Writes the bootloader itself to the MBR or the ESP. Needed after replacing a disk, not after changing a setting.

The workflow is therefore always the same two steps: edit /etc/default/grub, then run grub-mkconfig -o /boot/grub/grub.cfg (or its update-grub wrapper) to regenerate. grub-mkconfig reads the scripts in /etc/grub.d/ and the settings in /etc/default/grub and writes a file you never edit.

Editing grub.cfg directly is the single most common way to lose a change silently — it works, it survives a reboot, and it vanishes the next time a kernel package is installed.

The kernel command line is how you talk to the kernel before any configuration file is readable, which makes it the recovery tool of last resort. The current one is always visible:

cat /proc/cmdline

Parameters worth knowing by heart, because each one rescues a different failure:

  • single or systemd.unit=rescue.target — boot to a single-user root shell with local filesystems mounted and no network services. The way in when a service is crash-looping at boot.
  • systemd.unit=emergency.target — even earlier: root shell, root filesystem mounted read-only, nothing else. The way in when /etc/fstab has an entry that fails and the boot hangs.
  • init=/bin/bash — skip PID 1 entirely and get a shell. The way in when systemd itself is broken. Remount / read-write by hand before changing anything.
  • ro / rw — how the root filesystem is first mounted. It is normally ro, and the initramfs or systemd remounts it read-write after fsck.
  • quiet splash — suppress kernel messages. Removing these two is the first diagnostic step for a boot that fails with no visible reason.

In the GRUB menu, pressing e on an entry gives an editable copy for one boot only. Nothing is written to disk, so a mistake is undone by a power cycle — which is exactly the property you want when the machine is already broken.

9.3 initramfs: a Filesystem Before the Filesystem

The kernel has a chicken-and-egg problem. To mount the root filesystem it needs a driver for the storage controller, a driver for the filesystem, and possibly LVM, RAID or LUKS support. Compiling all of those into the kernel would make it enormous and would still miss the one your hardware needs.

The initramfs solves it: a compressed cpio archive, loaded into memory by the bootloader alongside the kernel, unpacked into a RAM filesystem, and mounted as /. It contains a minimal userspace — busybox or systemd, a handful of modules, and a script — whose entire job is to make the real root reachable and then get out of the way with pivot_root and an exec of the real init.

Three practical consequences:

  1. An initramfs can be out of date. It contains a snapshot of modules and configuration from when it was generated. Change your root filesystem's UUID, or add an encrypted volume, and you must regenerate it — update-initramfs -u on Debian and Ubuntu, dracut -f on Fedora and RHEL. Forgetting is the classic "worked until I rebooted three weeks later" failure.
  2. initramfs failures drop you to a prompt inside it, typically (initramfs) with busybox. Nothing on the real root is mounted, so none of your usual tools exist. cat /proc/cmdline and blkid are how you find out whether the root device was even detected.
  3. The initramfs is where full-disk-encryption passphrases are entered, because the real root is unreadable until then.

9.4 What PID 1 Does — and What SysVinit Did

PID 1 is special to the kernel in three ways. It is started directly by the kernel; it cannot be killed by signals it has not explicitly handled, including SIGKILL from root; and it inherits every orphaned process on the machine, making it responsible for reaping them (§7.5). If PID 1 exits, the kernel panics. That is the whole contract.

The program that fills that contract is the machine's init system, and Linux has had three in common use: SysVinit, Upstart and systemd. Whichever init system is in place, its job is the same — bring the machine to a usable state, supervise what runs, and reap orphans.

SysVinit filled that contract with shell scripts and a number. The number was the runlevel — 0 halt, 1 single user, 2–5 various multi-user configurations, 6 reboot — set in /etc/inittab, changed with telinit, and implemented by /etc/init.d/ scripts symlinked into /etc/rc*.d/ directories with S/K prefixes and two-digit ordering numbers. Booting meant running every S-prefixed script in the target directory, in filename order, one at a time.

Three problems killed it, and knowing them explains every systemd design decision:

  • Ordering by filename is not dependency resolution. S20foo runs before S21bar because 20 sorts before 21, and nothing anywhere states why. Inserting a service means renumbering.
  • Strictly sequential. One slow script blocks the whole boot, on a machine with 16 idle cores.
  • No supervision. The script exits and init forgets the service exists. Crashed daemons stay dead; PID files go stale; "is it running?" is answered by parsing ps output.

You still meet SysVinit vocabulary, so it is worth being able to translate:

Runlevel 0 / 6
poweroff.target / reboot.target
Runlevel 1
rescue.target
Runlevel 3
multi-user.target — the normal server target
Runlevel 5
graphical.target
telinit 3
systemctl isolate multi-user.target
wall
Unchanged — the wall command still broadcasts a message to every logged-in terminal, and shutdown uses it to warn users before a reboot.
service foo start
systemctl start foo — the old command still works, redirected by a shim
chkconfig foo on
systemctl enable foo

One SysVinit-era command survives unchanged and is still the right tool: the wall command broadcasts a line to every logged-in terminal. shutdown calls the wall command for you to warn users before a reboot, and wall "maintenance in 5 minutes" is how you do it by hand.

systemd also still runs leftover /etc/init.d/ scripts through a generator that synthesises a unit for each one, which is why a package from 2011 installs and works.

9.5 Units: the Noun Everything Is Built From

A unit is systemd's word for anything it can manage. A service unit is by far the most common kind, and every .service file is one; the other types exist mostly to trigger or group service units. There are eleven types; six matter daily.

.service
A process to run and supervise.
.target
A named group with no behaviour of its own — a synchronisation point, and the replacement for runlevels.
.socket
A listening socket systemd owns, which starts a service on first connection (§9.9).
.timer
A schedule that activates another unit (§9.10).
.mount
A mount point. Generated automatically from /etc/fstab, or written by hand (§10.7).
.slice
A cgroup node used to group units for resource control (§15.7).

Units live in three directories, and the precedence between them is the thing to know:

/usr/lib/systemd/system/
Shipped by packages. Never edit — a package update overwrites it.
/run/systemd/system/
Runtime, created by systemd-run and generators. Gone at reboot.
/etc/systemd/system/
Local administrator configuration. Highest precedence, and where your changes belong.

A unit file is an INI file with three sections. Here is a real one, from this machine:

systemctl cat chrony.service

# /usr/lib/systemd/system/chrony.service
[Unit]
Description=chrony, an NTP client/server
Documentation=man:chronyd(8) man:chronyc(1) man:chrony.conf(5)
Conflicts=openntpd.service ntp.service ntpsec.service
ConditionVirtualization=|!container

[Service]
Type=notify
PIDFile=/run/chrony/chronyd.pid
EnvironmentFile=-/etc/default/chrony
User=_chrony
ExecStart=!/usr/lib/systemd/scripts/chronyd-starter.sh -n $DAEMON_OPTS
NotifyAccess=all

CapabilityBoundingSet=~CAP_SYS_ADMIN
DevicePolicy=closed
LockPersonality=yes
MemoryDenyWriteExecute=yes
NoNewPrivileges=yes

Read that as three separate statements. [Unit] is metadata and relationships — note Conflicts=, which means starting chrony stops any other NTP daemon, and ConditionVirtualization=|!container, which silently skips the unit inside a container where setting the clock is meaningless. [Service] is how to run it. And the last five lines are §15's hardening directives applied to a real daemon: no CAP_SYS_ADMIN, no device access, no writable-and-executable memory, and no privilege escalation. A time daemon is a good example of why: it needs exactly one power, CAP_SYS_TIME, and every other one it could have had is a liability.

The - prefix on EnvironmentFile= means "ignore if missing", and the ! prefix on ExecStart= means "run this with full privileges even though User= is set". Prefix characters like these are how unit files stay one line long; systemd.service(5) lists them all.

Note that this service unit sets User=, so the daemon does not run as root even though PID 1 started it, and that a service unit's [Service] section is where every hardening directive in §15.12 goes.

[Install] — absent above because chrony's is in a drop-in — is only read by systemctl enable, and it says where the symlink goes:

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target

9.6 Dependencies and Ordering Are Different Things

This is the single most common systemd misunderstanding, and it produces bugs that reproduce only on slow machines.

Wants= and Requires= say WHAT must also be started. They pull another unit in. Requires= additionally fails this unit if that one fails to start; Wants= is the soft version and is what you almost always want.
After= and Before= say WHEN, relative to each other. They order units that are already being started. They pull nothing in — After=postgresql.service on a machine where postgres is not being started orders you after nothing at all.

The trap is that they are orthogonal, so Requires=db.service without After=db.service starts both simultaneously and your service races the database. It will pass every test on your laptop, where postgres opens its socket in 40 ms, and fail on the production box where it replays a write-ahead log first. The correct form is almost always both:

[Unit]
Requires=postgresql.service
After=postgresql.service network-online.target

Two further points that are pure experience:

network.target is not what you want. It means "the network stack is configured", not "there is a usable address". A service that must reach the internet at startup wants After=network-online.target and Wants=network-online.target, because network-online.target is not pulled in by default and ordering after an inactive target orders after nothing.

Requires= propagates stops as well as starts. If the required unit is stopped later, yours is stopped too. Where you only want the start-time relationship, Wants= plus After= is the pair that expresses it.

Targets are how these compose. multi-user.target has no content; it is Wants= a list of services, and reaching it means all of them have been started. graphical.target requires multi-user.target and adds a display manager. The default is one command:

systemctl get-default

graphical.target

9.7 Service Types and the Readiness Problem

Type= tells systemd how to know a service has finished starting. Get it wrong and every unit ordered after yours starts too early.

simple
The default. The process is the service, and systemd considers it started the instant it forks — before the program has read its config or bound a port. Fast and honest about being a guess.
exec
Like simple, but ready after a successful execve(). Catches "the binary does not exist" as a start failure instead of an immediate crash. A strictly better default than simple.
forking
For classic daemons that background themselves. Ready when the parent exits. Needs PIDFile= to track the child reliably.
notify
The program tells systemd it is ready, by writing READY=1 to the socket in $NOTIFY_SOCKET. The only type that is actually correct, and what chrony uses above.
oneshot
Runs to completion and exits; the unit stays "active" afterwards if RemainAfterExit=yes. For migrations, mounts and setup steps.
dbus
Ready when the service takes its configured name on the D-Bus.

Restart policy is the other half of supervision, and the defaults are not what people assume. Restart=no is the default — a crashed simple service stays dead. Restart=on-failure restarts on a non-zero exit or a signal but not on a clean exit; Restart=always restarts even after a clean exit, which is right for a daemon that should never stop and wrong for anything that can legitimately finish.

Two options that prevent the classic crash-loop:

Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5
StartLimitIntervalSec=60
StartLimitBurst=3

That says: wait five seconds between attempts, and if the service fails three times in a minute, stop trying and leave it failed. Without the limit, a service that crashes instantly will be restarted thousands of times a minute, filling the journal and hiding the original error. When you meet a unit that refuses to start with start request repeated too quickly, this is what happened — systemctl reset-failed clears the counter.

9.8 systemctl in Practice

Five distinctions cover most of the confusion around this command.

start is now; enable is at boot. They are completely independent. systemctl enable --now foo does both, and is what you almost always mean.

enable just makes a symlink. It reads [Install] and links the unit into multi-user.target.wants/. That is why a unit with no [Install] section cannot be enabled, and why enable on a unit systemd has not re-read yet does nothing useful.

daemon-reload after every unit file change. systemd caches the parsed unit graph. Editing a file and running systemctl restart restarts the old definition, silently. systemctl edit handles the reload for you, which is one of several reasons to prefer it.

Override with a drop-in, never by copying. systemctl edit foo creates /etc/systemd/system/foo.service.d/override.conf, which is merged over the packaged unit. You change three lines instead of forking a file that then never receives upstream fixes. One gotcha: list-valued directives append, so overriding ExecStart= requires clearing it first:

[Service]
ExecStart=
ExecStart=/usr/local/bin/my-wrapper --flag

mask is stronger than disable. disable removes the boot symlink; something else can still pull the unit in as a dependency. mask symlinks it to /dev/null so it cannot be started at all, by anyone, including as a dependency. It is the right tool for "this must never run on this machine", and the wrong tool for everything else — a masked unit produces confusing failures in units that depend on it.

Reading systemctl status well is a skill in itself. The fields that matter, in order: Loaded tells you which file and whether it is enabled; Active tells you the state and how long it has held it; Main PID is the process to look at; the cgroup tree at the bottom lists every process the unit owns, including ones it forked, which is the reliable answer to "what is this service actually running"; and the last ten journal lines are appended so you rarely need a second command.

9.9 Socket Activation

systemd can own a listening socket and start the service only when the first connection arrives. The socket exists from boot; the service does not.

systemctl cat systemd-journald.socket

[Unit]
Description=Journal Sockets
DefaultDependencies=no
Before=sockets.target
IgnoreOnIsolate=yes

Three things this buys, and only the first is the obvious one:

  1. Lazy start. A rarely used service costs nothing until used.
  2. Ordering for free. Because the socket is created before any service starts, a client can connect and block while the server is still starting. The connection is queued by the kernel, not refused. This removes a whole class of "service A started before service B was ready" races — the dependency problem of §9.6 disappears rather than being solved.
  3. Restart without dropping connections. systemd holds the listening socket across a restart of the service, so clients see a pause rather than a refusal.

The service side needs to accept the inherited file descriptor rather than opening its own — the descriptors arrive as fd 3 upwards with $LISTEN_FDS set. This is the same mechanism inetd and xinetd used decades ago, with the important difference that systemd passes the listening socket to a long-lived process, where inetd forked a new process per connection. Where you meet /etc/xinetd.d/ on an old machine, a .socket unit is the modern replacement.

9.10 Timers: Schedules as Units

A systemd timer — a unit whose name ends in .timer — activates another unit on a schedule. A systemd timer never contains the work itself: it names a companion unit (by default, the same name with .service) and starts it when it fires, which is what lets the job itself carry resource limits, hardening and dependency ordering like any other service. Every timer on the machine is listed with its next and last run:

systemctl list-timers --all

NEXT                             LEFT LAST                              PASSED UNIT                           ACTIVATES
Wed 2026-08-26 20:30:00 UTC      7min Wed 2026-08-26 20:20:15 UTC 2min 34s ago sysstat-collect.timer          sysstat-collect.service
Wed 2026-08-26 20:44:51 UTC     22min Wed 2026-08-26 19:49:15 UTC    33min ago fwupd-refresh.timer            fwupd-refresh.service
Thu 2026-08-27 00:00:00 UTC  3h 37min Wed 2026-08-26 00:53:34 UTC            - dpkg-db-backup.timer           dpkg-db-backup.service
Thu 2026-08-27 00:47:01 UTC  4h 24min Wed 2026-08-26 01:09:13 UTC            - logrotate.timer                logrotate.service

The timer that rotates logs on this machine is eleven lines:

systemctl cat logrotate.timer

[Unit]
Description=Daily rotation of log files
Documentation=man:logrotate(8) man:logrotate.conf(5)

[Timer]
OnCalendar=daily
RandomizedDelaySec=1h
Persistent=true

[Install]
WantedBy=timers.target

Every line there is doing work. OnCalendar=daily is midnight. RandomizedDelaySec=1h spreads the actual run over the following hour, which is why the table above shows 00:47:01 rather than midnight — on a fleet, this is the difference between a thundering herd and a smooth load. Persistent=true means that if the machine was off at the scheduled time, the job runs at the next boot instead of being skipped, which is the property cron famously lacks and anacron was invented to add.

OnCalendar syntax is DayOfWeek Year-Month-Day Hour:Minute:Second, with * for any and / for steps. Never guess at it — there is a command that tells you exactly what an expression means:

systemd-analyze calendar "Mon *-*-* 03:30:00"

Normalized form: Mon *-*-* 03:30:00
    Next elapse: Mon 2026-08-31 03:30:00 UTC
       From now: 4 days left

The other trigger family is monotonic rather than calendar-based: OnBootSec=, OnStartupSec=, OnUnitActiveSec= and OnUnitInactiveSec=. OnUnitActiveSec=15min means "fifteen minutes after the last time the service ran", which — unlike a calendar entry every fifteen minutes — cannot overlap with itself when a run takes twenty minutes.

9.11 cron and at

cron predates all of this and is not going anywhere. It is one line per job, five time fields and a command:

0-59
minute
0-23
hour
1-31
day of month
1-12
month
0-7
day of week

* is any, */5 is every fifth, 1,15 is a list, 1-5 is a range. Day-of-week 0 and 7 are both Sunday. The one genuinely counter-intuitive rule: if both day-of-month and day-of-week are restricted, the job runs when either matches, not both. 0 0 13 * 5 is midnight on the 13th and every Friday, which is rarely what the author meant.

Where jobs live:

crontab -e
The calling user's personal crontab, stored under /var/spool/cron/. Five fields, no user column.
/etc/crontab
System-wide. Six fields — a username between the schedule and the command.
/etc/cron.d/
Drop-in files in the same six-field format. Where packages put their jobs, and where yours belong.
/etc/cron.{hourly,daily,weekly,monthly}/
Directories of executable scripts, run by run-parts. No schedule syntax at all — drop in a script, make it executable, done.
/etc/cron.allow, /etc/cron.deny
Who may use crontab. If cron.allow exists, only users listed in it may; otherwise anyone not in cron.deny may.

The wiring is visible in the system crontab itself:

cat /etc/crontab

SHELL=/bin/sh
# You can also override PATH, but by default, newer versions inherit it from the environment
#PATH=/usr/local/sbin:/usr/local/bin:/usr/sbin:/usr/bin:/sbin:/bin

17 *	* * *	root	cd / && run-parts --report /etc/cron.hourly
25 6	* * *	root	test -x /usr/sbin/anacron || { cd / && run-parts --report /etc/cron.daily; }
47 6	* * 7	root	test -x /usr/sbin/anacron || { cd / && run-parts --report /etc/cron.weekly; }
52 6	1 * *	root	test -x /usr/sbin/anacron || { cd / && run-parts --report /etc/cron.monthly; }

Three things to read out of that. The hourly job runs at 17 minutes past, not on the hour — a small anti-herd measure, the ancestor of RandomizedDelaySec. Each of the daily, weekly and monthly lines is guarded by test -x /usr/sbin/anacron ||, so if anacron is installed it takes over: anacron exists to run periodic jobs on machines that are not on 24 hours a day, catching up on missed runs at the next boot exactly as Persistent=true does for timers. And run-parts --report is what turns a directory of scripts into a schedule.

The environment is where cron jobs die, and the shape of the failure is always the same: it works when you run it by hand and does nothing at 3 a.m. A cron job runs with no terminal, HOME and SHELL set from /etc/passwd, and — historically — a minimal PATH. Note that the comment in this machine's /etc/crontab says newer versions inherit PATH from the environment cron itself was started with, which is not your login environment either. Do not rely on either behaviour:

crontab -l

no crontab for dev

The four rules that make cron jobs reliable, in order of how often they matter:

  1. Absolute paths for everything — the binary, the inputs, the outputs.
