  2. Set PATH explicitly at the top of the crontab rather than assuming one.
  3. Redirect output. cron mails stdout and stderr to the user, and on a machine with no mail transfer agent that output is simply lost. >> /var/log/myjob.log 2>&1, or pipe through logger (§11.4) so it lands in the journal with a tag.
  4. % is special in a crontab line — it means newline, and everything after the first one becomes standard input for the command. A date +%Y-%m-%d in a crontab must be written date +\%Y-\%m-\%d. This bites everyone exactly once.

at is the one-shot counterpart: at 03:00 tomorrow, then type commands and end with Ctrl-D. atq lists the queue, atrm removes a job, and /etc/at.allow and /etc/at.deny control access the same way cron's do. It requires the atd daemon to be running, and it is often not installed at all — on this machine it is not, which is itself the usual state of affairs on a modern server.

Choosing between cron and timers. Timers win on almost every technical axis: dependency ordering, resource limits, logging that lands in the journal automatically with the unit's name attached, Persistent= for missed runs, randomised delays, and systemctl list-timers showing you when something last ran and whether it succeeded. cron wins on two: it is three words in a file that any colleague can read, and it exists on machines with no systemd — inside containers, most obviously. Use timers for anything that matters to production; use cron when the job is trivial and the audience is a person.

9.12 Reading a Slow Boot

systemd instruments its own startup, and three commands answer three different questions.

systemd-analyze

Startup finished in 1.514s (kernel) + 5.952s (initrd) + 36.539s (userspace) = 44.006s
graphical.target reached after 36.538s in userspace.

The split is the first diagnosis. Time in the kernel phase points at hardware and drivers; time in initrd points at storage assembly, LVM or LUKS; time in userspace — 36 of 44 seconds here — points at services, which is where you can actually do something.

systemd-analyze blame

15.150s dev-rfkill.device
15.150s sys-devices-virtual-misc-rfkill.device
14.596s mysql.service
12.137s postgresql@18-main.service
 8.991s fwupd-refresh.service

blame sorts units by their own start time — but it is misleading on its own, because units start in parallel. A unit taking 15 seconds while nine others also run costs the boot nothing. The question is what was on the critical path:

systemd-analyze critical-chain

graphical.target @36.538s
└─multi-user.target @36.537s
  └─mysql.service @21.939s +14.596s
    └─basic.target @20.179s
      └─sockets.target @20.178s
        └─snapd.socket @14.755s +5.335s
          └─sysinit.target @14.365s
            └─snapd.apparmor.service @14.106s +246ms
              └─apparmor.service @5.280s +8.783s
                └─local-fs.target @5.247s
                  └─boot.mount @5.073s +169ms

@ is when a unit became active; + is how long it took. Now the boot is explained: apparmor.service took 8.8 s loading profiles, snapd.socket 5.3 s, and mysql.service 14.6 s — and because each was blocking the next, those three account for 28.7 of the 36.5 seconds, or four fifths of the userspace boot. Compare with blame, which put two rfkill device units at the top; they cost nothing at all, because nothing waited for them. blame finds slow units; critical-chain finds slow boots.

The other two commands worth having: systemctl list-units --state=failed (the first thing to run on any machine you did not just boot yourself), and journalctl -b -p err for this boot's errors only (§11.2).

Exercises

Exercise 9.1 — The service that races. A team's unit file looks like this, and the service works on every developer laptop but fails roughly one boot in five in production with "connection refused to 127.0.0.1:5432":

[Unit]
Description=Orders API
Requires=postgresql.service

[Service]
Type=simple
ExecStart=/usr/local/bin/orders-api
Restart=always

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target

Find every defect and give the corrected unit.

Solution

Defect 1 — Requires= without After=. This is the cause of the reported failure. Requires= pulls postgres in but says nothing about ordering, so both start simultaneously and the API wins the race one time in five. It passes on laptops because postgres there has a tiny database and opens its socket in milliseconds; in production it replays a write-ahead log first.

Defect 2 — Restart=always is masking the bug. The service crashes, is restarted, and by the second attempt postgres is up, so the machine ends up healthy and nobody investigates. This is why the failure was reported as intermittent rather than as a start-order bug.

Defect 3 — no start-limit configuration. With Restart=always and no limits, a genuinely broken database means an infinite restart loop filling the journal.

Defect 4 — Type=simple. Nothing ordered after this service can know when it is really listening.

Defect 5 — Requires= is stronger than intended. It also propagates stops: a maintenance restart of postgres takes the API down with it.

[Unit]
Description=Orders API
Wants=postgresql.service
After=postgresql.service network-online.target
Wants=network-online.target

[Service]
Type=notify
ExecStart=/usr/local/bin/orders-api
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5
StartLimitIntervalSec=60
StartLimitBurst=3

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target

Wants= plus After= gives the ordering without the stop propagation. Restart=on-failure with a burst limit means three fast failures leave the unit visibly failed rather than looping. Type=notify requires the application to send READY=1 — worth the small code change, and if that is not possible, Type=exec is the better fallback.

The deeper point: the unit file cannot fix a service that assumes its dependencies are ready. Even with perfect ordering, postgres can restart at 3 a.m. A retry loop with backoff around the initial connection is the real fix, and the unit ordering is the optimisation that stops it being exercised on every boot.

Exercise 9.2 — Nothing happened at 3 a.m. A backup script runs perfectly from an interactive shell. In cron it produces no output, no log entry and no backup:

0 3 * * * /home/deploy/backup.sh --stamp $(date +%Y-%m-%d)

List everything wrong with that line.

Solution

1 — The unescaped %. In a crontab, % means newline. cron sees the command as ending at date + and passes Y-%m-%d as standard input. The command that runs is /home/deploy/backup.sh --stamp $(date +, which is a syntax error. Written correctly: date +\%Y-\%m-\%d. This alone is enough to produce exactly the reported symptom.

2 — Command substitution at all. Even escaped, $(...) is evaluated by cron's shell, which is /bin/sh and not your login shell. Compute the stamp inside the script.

3 — No output redirection. cron mails output to the user; with no MTA installed it is discarded. That is why there is "no output" to look at, which is the real reason this took an afternoon to find. Add >> /var/log/backup.log 2>&1.

4 — PATH is not your interactive PATH. If the script calls aws, pg_dump or anything else outside /usr/bin, it will not be found. Set PATH= at the top of the crontab and use absolute paths in the script.

5 — No overlap protection. If a backup takes longer than a day, two run at once. Wrap in flock -n /var/lock/backup.lock.

6 — No failure signal. The script's exit status goes nowhere.

The corrected line:

PATH=/usr/local/sbin:/usr/local/bin:/usr/sbin:/usr/bin:/sbin:/bin
0 3 * * * flock -n /var/lock/backup.lock /home/deploy/backup.sh >> /var/log/backup.log 2>&1

with the date stamp computed inside the script. As a timer, most of these problems do not arise: output goes to the journal automatically, Type=oneshot gives a real exit status that systemctl list-timers and is-failed report, and overlap is prevented because a unit cannot be started while it is already active.

Exercise 9.3 — Diagnose the boot. A machine takes 90 seconds to reach multi-user.target. systemd-analyze blame lists NetworkManager-wait-online.service at 45 s and plymouth-quit-wait.service at 38 s. What do you conclude, and what do you run next?

Solution

Conclude almost nothing yet. blame does not distinguish a unit that was blocking the boot from one that happened to take a long time in parallel. The next command is:

systemd-analyze critical-chain multi-user.target

If NetworkManager-wait-online.service appears in that chain, it is genuinely the problem, and the diagnosis is specific: that unit exists to delay network-online.target until an interface has an address, and it waits for its full timeout when an interface is configured but never comes up — an unplugged second NIC, or DHCP on a VLAN with no server. The fix is to configure the unused interface as optional rather than to shorten the timeout, since the timeout is doing its job.

plymouth-quit-wait.service at 38 s is the boot splash waiting for a graphical session to take over. On a headless server, systemctl disable plymouth-quit-wait.service is safe; on a desktop it usually means a display manager is slow to start, and it is a symptom rather than a cause.

The general method, and it applies well beyond boot: a duration is not a delay. Ask what was waiting on the thing before you optimise it. systemd-analyze plot > boot.svg renders the whole parallel timeline and makes the distinction visible at a glance.

Exercise 9.4 — Unbreakable boot. A colleague edits /usr/lib/systemd/system/myapp.service directly, runs systemctl restart myapp, sees the old behaviour, reboots, and now the machine hangs at boot. Explain each step, and describe the recovery.

Solution

Editing the wrong file. /usr/lib/systemd/system/ is package territory; the change works until the next package update silently reverts it. The correct location is a drop-in via systemctl edit myapp.

Old behaviour after restart. systemd caches the parsed unit graph, so restart restarted the cached definition. systemctl daemon-reload is required after any hand edit — and is one reason systemctl edit is better, since it reloads automatically.

The hang. The edit took effect at boot, where whatever is wrong with it now blocks. The most likely culprits are a Requires=/After= pointing at a unit that never becomes active, a Type=notify on a program that never sends READY=1 (so systemd waits out TimeoutStartSec, 90 s by default, per attempt), or an ExecStart= path that does not exist combined with Restart=always.

Recovery, in escalating order:

  1. At the GRUB menu press e, append systemd.unit=rescue.target to the linux line, and boot. That is a one-time change; nothing is written to disk.
  2. If rescue also hangs, use emergency.target — it starts almost nothing.
  3. If systemd itself will not run, init=/bin/bash, then mount -o remount,rw / before editing anything.

Once in: systemctl disable --now myapp — or mask it if something else pulls it in — then journalctl -b -1 -u myapp to read the previous boot's log for the unit, fix the unit properly as a drop-in, daemon-reload, and test with systemctl start before rebooting.

The habit worth taking away: after changing a unit that runs at boot, verify with systemctl start and systemd-analyze verify /etc/systemd/system/myapp.service, which parses the unit and reports unknown directives and unresolvable dependencies without running anything.

SECTION 10

Storage: Devices, Partitions, Filesystems and LVM

Storage is the part of a Linux system where mistakes are permanent. A wrong chmod is undone in a second; a wrong mkfs is not undone at all. It is also the part where the abstractions stack deepest — a file you write passes through a filesystem, possibly a logical volume, possibly an encryption layer, a partition table, a block device, a driver, and a controller before any electron moves. Each of those layers has its own tools, its own failure mode, and its own way of being full.

This section walks the stack from the bottom up, then covers the three operational questions that actually consume time: why the disk is full when it is not, why the mount point is empty, and how to grow a filesystem without a reboot.

10.1 The Storage Stack

Block device
A linear array of 512-byte-addressed sectors, presented by a driver. /dev/sda. Knows nothing about files.
Partition table
MBR or GPT. Divides the device into ranges, each presented as its own block device: /dev/sda1, /dev/sda2.
Volume manager
Optional. LVM pools partitions and carves out resizable logical volumes. Also a block device.
Encryption
Optional. LUKS/dm-crypt presents a decrypted block device backed by an encrypted one.
Filesystem
ext4, XFS, Btrfs. Turns a block range into named files, directories and metadata.
Mount
Attaches a filesystem's root to a directory in the single tree (§1.5).

Every layer is a block device to the layer above, which is why the stack composes freely: LVM on a partition, LUKS on LVM, a filesystem on the LUKS device. lsblk renders the whole thing:

lsblk -o NAME,MAJ:MIN,RM,SIZE,RO,TYPE,FSTYPE,MOUNTPOINT

NAME                      MAJ:MIN RM  SIZE RO TYPE FSTYPE      MOUNTPOINT
sda                         8:0    0 86.9G  0 disk
├─sda1                      8:1    0    1M  0 part
├─sda2                      8:2    0    2G  0 part ext4        /boot
└─sda3                      8:3    0 84.9G  0 part LVM2_member
  └─ubuntu--vg-ubuntu--lv 252:0    0 84.9G  0 lvm  ext4        /
sr0                        11:0    1 1024M  1 rom

That is a complete, ordinary Ubuntu install, and it repays reading. One 86.9 GiB disk — lsblk prints binary units, so its G is GiB, and the same disk is 93.3 GB in the decimal units it was sold in (§8.4). A 1 MiB partition with no filesystem — the BIOS boot partition, where GRUB's second stage lives on a GPT-labelled disk booted through legacy BIOS (§9.2). A separate 2 GiB /boot, because the bootloader must be able to read the kernel before LVM exists. And the remaining 84.9 GiB given to LVM as a physical volume, with one logical volume holding the root filesystem, appearing under major number 252 as a device-mapper device.

The MAJ:MIN column is the major and minor numbers of §8.5: 8 for the SATA driver, 252 for device-mapper.

10.2 Partition Tables: MBR and GPT

MBR dates from 1983 and lives in the first 512 bytes of the disk, alongside the bootloader stub. Four partition entries at 16 bytes each is all that fits, and each records a 32-bit starting sector and length. Two hard limits follow directly: four primary partitions, and 2 TiB maximum addressable size (2³² sectors × 512 bytes). The four-partition limit was worked around with extended and logical partitions — one primary entry pointing at a linked list of further entries — which works and is unpleasant.

GPT replaces it. A 64-bit sector address (so 8 ZiB), 128 partition entries by default, a partition-type GUID and a unique GUID per partition, a UTF-16 name field, and — importantly — a backup copy of the whole table at the end of the disk, plus CRC32 checksums so corruption is detected rather than silently obeyed. It also keeps a "protective MBR" in sector 0 so that MBR-only tools see one partition spanning the disk and refuse to help rather than destroying it.

Use GPT for anything new. The tools:

fdisk
Interactive, handles both MBR and GPT in current versions. fdisk -l lists.
gdisk
GPT-only, with the same interface as classic fdisk. Its recovery menu can rebuild a table from the backup copy.
parted
Scriptable, and the one to use in automation. Note that classic parted commands apply immediately, with no write step to reconsider at.
partprobe
Ask the kernel to re-read the partition table. Needed when a partition was changed while another on the same disk was mounted.

Alignment matters more than it looks. Partitions should start on a 1 MiB boundary, because a partition misaligned against the underlying flash erase block or RAID stripe turns every write into a read-modify-write of two blocks. Every modern tool defaults to this; the only way to get it wrong is to enter sector numbers by hand.

10.3 Filesystems: Choosing One

ext4 — the default nearly everywhere. Extent-based, journalled, endlessly tested, shrinkable as well as growable. Its one structural limitation is that the inode count is fixed at mkfs time. Choose it unless you have a reason not to.
XFS — the RHEL default. Allocates inodes dynamically, scales better to very large filesystems and highly parallel writes, and has excellent behaviour with large files. It cannot be shrunk, ever. That single fact decides many deployments.
Btrfs — copy-on-write, with subvolumes, snapshots, checksummed data, transparent compression and built-in multi-device support. A volume manager and a filesystem in one. The default on Fedora and openSUSE.
vfat / exFAT — no permissions, no ownership, no symlinks, no journal. Their purpose is interoperability: the EFI System Partition must be FAT32, and exFAT is what large removable media use to be readable by every operating system.

Journalling is the property that makes the first three crash-safe. A journaling filesystem keeps a small append-only record of what it is about to do. A metadata update touches several structures — a directory entry, an inode, a block bitmap — and a crash between them leaves the filesystem inconsistent. A journal records the intended change first; after a crash, recovery replays or discards complete transactions, so fsck on a journalled filesystem is a few seconds of replay rather than a full structural scan.

Note that journaling protects metadata consistency, not your data. Note carefully what is protected. ext4's default data=ordered journals metadata only, guaranteeing that you never see a file pointing at someone else's old blocks — it does not guarantee that your data reached the disk. That is what fsync() is for, and it is why a database calls it and a text editor writing to a temporary file and renaming does too.

What this kernel can mount without a module load:

grep -v nodev /proc/filesystems | tr -d '\t' | tr '\n' ' '

ext3 ext2 ext4 squashfs vfat fuseblk

Btrfs subvolumes deserve a note because they are unlike anything else here: they are independently mountable, snapshottable trees inside one filesystem that share a single free-space pool. A snapshot is copy-on-write and therefore instant and initially free, which is what makes "snapshot before upgrade, roll back if it breaks" practical. Multi-device Btrfs adds the RAID-like profiles (raid1 for metadata is the common conservative choice) and transparent zstd compression, which on compressible data is often a net speed gain because less I/O happens.

10.4 Creating Filesystems

mkfs.ext4 -L data /dev/sdb1
mkfs.xfs -L data /dev/sdb1
mkswap /dev/sdb2

mkfs is the front end; mkfs.ext4, mkfs.xfs and friends are the real programs. There is no confirmation prompt worth relying on and no undo. The habit that prevents disasters is to run lsblk -f immediately before, and to confirm that the target shows no filesystem and no mount point.

Two mkfs.ext4 options are worth knowing in advance, because neither can be changed later:

  • -N or -i sets the inode count or the bytes-per-inode ratio. The default is one inode per 16 KB, which is generous for documents and wrong for a mail spool or a node_modules tree full of tiny files. §10.9 covers what running out looks like.
  • -m sets the reserved-blocks percentage, default 5%. That reservation exists so that root can still log in and write when the filesystem fills, and so the allocator can avoid fragmentation. On a 12 TB data volume, 5% is 600 GB of nothing; -m 1 or -m 0 is right there, and leaving the default on the root filesystem is right too.

10.5 LVM: Renaming the Layers

LVM inserts an indirection between partitions and filesystems, and buys resize-without-repartitioning, snapshots, and volumes spanning several disks.

Physical Volume (PV)
A block device handed to LVM. pvcreate /dev/sda3.
Volume Group (VG)
A pool of PVs. vgcreate ubuntu-vg /dev/sda3. The unit of allocation is the extent, 4 MiB by default.
Logical Volume (LV)
A slice of the pool, presented as a block device. lvcreate -L 20G -n data ubuntu-vg appears as /dev/ubuntu-vg/data.

A physical volume is created by writing an LVM header onto a block device with pvcreate, after which the device belongs to LVM and its raw contents must not be touched. A physical volume can be a whole disk, a partition, a RAID array or a LUKS device — anything that presents as a block device.

The naming in lsblk above — ubuntu--vg-ubuntu--lv — is the device-mapper convention: hyphens in a name are doubled, and the VG and LV are joined by a single hyphen. So that is LV ubuntu-lv in VG ubuntu-vg.

The commands come in families of three by first letter: pvs/vgs/lvs for summaries, pvdisplay/vgdisplay/lvdisplay for detail, pvcreate/vgcreate/lvcreate to make things, and vgextend/lvextend to grow them. All of them need root.

Growing a volume is the operation you will actually do, and it is two steps because LVM does not know what is inside the volume:

lvextend -L +20G /dev/ubuntu-vg/data
resize2fs /dev/ubuntu-vg/data

lvextend -r does both in one command by calling the right resizer for the filesystem it finds — resize2fs for ext4, xfs_growfs for XFS. Note that ext4 and XFS can both be grown while mounted; only ext4 can be shrunk, and only while unmounted. Shrinking is the operation to avoid: it means moving data, and an interruption is data loss. Grow generously and often instead.

LVM snapshots are a copy-on-write volume that records the original contents of any extent modified after the snapshot was taken. They are excellent for "take a consistent backup of a database at 3 a.m." and dangerous if neglected, because a snapshot that fills up is invalidated — silently useless — and because every write to the origin while a snapshot exists costs an extra read and write. Take them, use them, delete them.

Thin provisioning allocates extents on first write, so ten 100 GB thin volumes fit in a 200 GB pool. It is exactly as good and as dangerous as it sounds: excellent utilisation, and a pool that reaches 100% takes every volume in it read-only at once. Monitor the pool, not the volumes.

10.6 Swap

swapon --show

NAME      TYPE SIZE USED PRIO
/swap.img file   8G 2.7M   -1

Note the type: a file, not a partition. Modern Ubuntu installs /swap.img by default, and there is essentially no performance difference on any device you would buy today. A file is trivially resizable, which a partition is not, so prefer it.

What swap is for is more subtle than "extra RAM". Its main day-to-day value is letting the kernel evict anonymous pages that are never touched — the initialisation code of a long-running daemon, a leaked allocation nothing reads — and use the freed memory for page cache that is actively helping. A machine with no swap has no choice but to keep every one of those pages resident.

Two consequences people get wrong. Swap is not what makes a machine slow; thrashing is. swapon --show reporting 2 GB used on a healthy server is normal and good. The metric that matters is the rate — si/so in vmstat 1, or pressure (§15.8). And disabling swap does not prevent OOM kills, it makes them earlier and more abrupt: with no swap, the kernel has one fewer way to reclaim, so it reaches the OOM killer sooner.

Adding swap:

fallocate -l 4G /swapfile
chmod 600 /swapfile
mkswap /swapfile
swapon /swapfile

mkswap writes the swap signature onto a device or file, and it must be run before swapon will accept it; running mkswap on the wrong device destroys whatever was there, exactly as mkfs does.

The chmod 600 is not optional — a world-readable swap file is a readable copy of process memory. Add a line to /etc/fstab to make it survive a reboot, and note that a swap file on Btrfs needs particular preparation, because copy-on-write and swap do not mix.

10.7 Mounting, and /etc/fstab

mount attaches a filesystem's root at a directory; umount detaches it. Everything else is options.

findmnt -t ext4 -o TARGET,SOURCE,FSTYPE,OPTIONS

TARGET  SOURCE                            FSTYPE OPTIONS
/       /dev/mapper/ubuntu--vg-ubuntu--lv ext4   rw,relatime
└─/boot /dev/sda2                         ext4   rw,relatime

mount -o is how every option below is passed on the command line — mount -o ro,noexec /dev/sdb1 /mnt — and mount -o remount,rw / applies a changed option to something already mounted without unmounting it.

findmnt is strictly better than reading /proc/mounts by eye — it renders the tree, filters by type or source, and findmnt -x validates /etc/fstab without mounting anything.

Persistent mounts live in /etc/fstab, six fields per line:

grep -v '^#' /etc/fstab

/dev/disk/by-id/dm-uuid-LVM-1CvwrWCLRDS3ordARZyfzvEQgL6TX1D5LxsWMcjHnNqKZH5sM525S1RNpLBJwer3 / ext4 defaults 0 1
/dev/disk/by-uuid/90400869-5c6f-4cbf-9f7c-2a37b501116c /boot ext4 defaults 0 1
/swap.img	none	swap	sw	0	0

Field by field: device, mount point, type, options, dump (an obsolete backup flag, always 0), and pass — the fsck order, where 1 is the root filesystem, 2 is everything else, and 0 means never check.

Note that the installer used /dev/disk/by-id/ and /dev/disk/by-uuid/ paths rather than /dev/sda2. That is the §8.6 lesson applied: kernel names depend on probe order, and stable names do not.

blkid is the tool that reports what is actually on a block device: its filesystem type, its label and its UUID. Running blkid before writing an /etc/fstab line is how you get the UUID right, and blkid -o value -s UUID /dev/sdb1 prints just the UUID for a script.

The options worth knowing:

defaults
rw,suid,dev,exec,auto,nouser,async. Note that it includes suid and exec.
noexec,nosuid,nodev
The hardening trio for any filesystem holding user-supplied data — /tmp, /var/tmp, an uploads volume. Cheap, and it removes the "upload a binary and run it" step from a whole class of attack.
nofail
Do not fail the boot if the device is missing. Essential for anything that is not required to reach a login prompt.
relatime / noatime
relatime is the default and updates access time only if it is older than the modification time. noatime removes those writes entirely — worth it on a read-heavy volume.
ro
Read-only. Also what the kernel remounts a filesystem as when it detects corruption, which is a symptom worth recognising.
x-systemd.*
systemd extensions: x-systemd.automount (mount on first access), x-systemd.device-timeout=, x-systemd.requires=.

systemd turns every /etc/fstab line into a generated mount unit named after the mount point with the slashes escaped — /srv/data becomes srv-data.mount — so systemctl status srv-data.mount reports on it like any other unit and systemctl show lists its options. Writing a mount unit by hand instead of an fstab line buys you the full dependency vocabulary of §9.6, which is what x-systemd.requires= is a shorthand for.

Bind mounts make the same filesystem appear at a second location: mount --bind /data/www /srv/www. It is not a symlink — it is a second mount of the same subtree, so it works across chroot boundaries and inside containers, which is exactly why every container runtime uses bind mounts to pass host directories in (§15.12).

And the mount point's own contents are hidden, not deleted. Mounting over a directory that already contains files makes them inaccessible until the filesystem is unmounted, and they still occupy space on the underlying filesystem. This is the cause of the classic mystery in §10.9: a full disk with nothing visibly on it.

umount failing with "target is busy" is answered by finding who is holding it:

lsof +f -- /mnt/data
fuser -vm /mnt/data

fuser -vm lists every process using a filesystem, with the access type — c for current directory, f for open file, e for a running executable — and fuser -km kills them all, which is the blunt instrument for a mount point that must be freed now.

umount -l (lazy) detaches the tree immediately and cleans up when the last user goes away. It is the right tool when you need the mount point back now, and the wrong one if you were about to physically remove the device.

10.8 Checking and Repairing

fsck /dev/sdb1

Never run fsck on a mounted filesystem. The kernel has cached metadata that the repair tool cannot see, and writing over it corrupts the filesystem you were trying to fix. fsck refuses on most filesystems; do not use -f to talk it round.

fsck is a dispatcher — it inspects the type and runs e2fsck, fsck.xfs or another. The ext-family variants are worth knowing:

e2fsck -n
Check and report, change nothing. Always start here. Safe on a mounted filesystem for a rough look, though results may be inconsistent.
e2fsck -p
Preen: fix only trivially safe problems, exit non-zero otherwise. What the boot process runs.
e2fsck -y
Answer yes to everything. The last resort, run after a backup image if the data matters.
tune2fs -l
Print the superblock: features, mount count, last check, reserved blocks, inode count.
xfs_repair
XFS's tool. XFS has no fsck proper — fsck.xfs does nothing at all by design, because the journal replays on mount. xfs_repair -n to check.

tune2fs also adjusts things a live filesystem can change: -m for the reserved percentage, -L for the label, -c/-i for the check interval, and -e remount-ro to make the filesystem go read-only on error rather than continuing — a good default on anything holding data you care about.

Files recovered by fsck with no reachable directory entry go to lost+found at the filesystem root, named by inode number. It is not junk, and clearing it out without looking is how a recoverable file becomes an unrecoverable one.

10.9 The Disk Is Full and the Files Are Not There

Three distinct conditions all present as "no space left on device", and they need different fixes.

Case 1: really full.

df -hT -x tmpfs -x devtmpfs

Filesystem                        Type  Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
/dev/mapper/ubuntu--vg-ubuntu--lv ext4   84G   42G   39G  52% /
/dev/sda2                         ext4  2.0G  187M  1.7G  11% /boot

df reads the filesystem's own accounting; du adds up files it can walk. Find the weight with:

du -sh /var/* 2>/dev/null | sort -h | tail

-h for human sizes, sort -h to sort them correctly, and descend into the biggest each time. /boot is the classic small filesystem that fills — old kernels accumulate — and apt autoremove --purge is usually the whole fix.

Case 2: out of inodes. Every file consumes one inode, and ext4's count is fixed at mkfs time.

df -i -x tmpfs -x devtmpfs

Filesystem                         Inodes  IUsed   IFree IUse% Mounted on
/dev/mapper/ubuntu--vg-ubuntu--lv 5548800 624046 4924754   12% /
/dev/sda2                          131072    343  130729    1% /boot

IUse% at 100% while Use% sits at 30% means millions of tiny files — a session directory, a cache, a mail queue. Creating a file fails with ENOSPC and df -h looks fine, which is why this one wastes an afternoon if you have not seen it. There is no way to add inodes to an ext4 filesystem; the fix is to delete files, or to recreate the filesystem with -i set lower. XFS does not have this failure mode.

Case 3: deleted but still open. A process holding an open descriptor to an unlinked file keeps its blocks allocated (§8.2). du cannot see the file — there is no directory entry — but df counts the space.

lsof +L1

+L1 lists open files with a link count below 1, which is exactly this condition. The space returns when the process closes the descriptor or exits; deleting more files will not help, and truncate -s 0 /proc/PID/fd/N reclaims it immediately without restarting the service.

The tell that distinguishes case 3 from case 1 in one step: df and du disagree. If du -sh / totals far less than df reports used, either a deleted-but-open file or a filesystem mounted over a non-empty directory (§10.7) is hiding the difference — and for the second, mount --bind / /mnt and looking at /mnt shows the underlying tree with nothing mounted over it.

10.10 Encryption and Quotas

LUKS is the standard on-disk encryption format. cryptsetup luksFormat /dev/sdb1 writes a header containing key slots; cryptsetup open /dev/sdb1 secret prompts for a passphrase and creates /dev/mapper/secret, an ordinary block device you then put a filesystem on. Two properties matter operationally: there are multiple key slots, so several passphrases or a key file can open the same volume and one can be revoked without re-encrypting; and the header is fatal to lose — a damaged header means the data is gone regardless of the passphrase, which is why cryptsetup luksHeaderBackup exists and should be used.

Software RAID is the other layer that can sit under a filesystem, and on Linux it is mdadm. mdadm --create /dev/md0 --level=1 --raid-devices=2 /dev/sdb /dev/sdc builds a mirror; --level=5 and --level=10 build the parity and striped-mirror forms. The array appears as an ordinary block device, so LVM, LUKS and a filesystem stack on top of it exactly as they would on a partition. Two operational notes: cat /proc/mdstat is the live status including rebuild progress, and mdadm --detail --scan >> /etc/mdadm/mdadm.conf plus a regenerated initramfs is what makes an array assemble at boot. RAID protects against a disk failing and against nothing else — not deletion, not corruption above the block layer, and not the building burning down.

Quotas cap what a user or group may consume on a filesystem, in blocks and in inodes, with a soft limit (exceedable for a grace period) and a hard limit (never). The mechanism is a mount option (usrquota, grpquota, or XFS's pquota for project quotas), then quotacheck, quotaon, and edquota to set values. On a modern single-tenant server they are rare; on a shared machine with real users they are the only thing that prevents one person filling /home. Note that a container's disk usage is usually limited by a project quota or a separate volume rather than a user quota, since everything inside may run as the same UID.

Exercises

Exercise 10.1 — 100% full, nothing to delete. A web server alerts at 100% disk. df -h / shows 40G used of 40G. du -sh / totals 12G. The application was restarted last night after someone cleared old logs. Diagnose it, fix it without a restart, and prevent it.

Solution

Deleted files still held open. Someone ran rm on log files that the application had open. Unlinking removes the directory entry, so du — which walks directory entries — cannot see them, while the inodes and their blocks stay allocated until the last descriptor closes. The 28 GB gap between du and df is exactly that.

Confirm:

lsof +L1

+L1 selects open files whose link count is below 1. The output names the process, the descriptor number and the original path with (deleted).

Fix without restarting, truncating through /proc (§8.2):

truncate -s 0 /proc/12345/fd/7

The space is returned immediately. Note truncate, not rm — the file is already unlinked, and the goal is to release its blocks while the process keeps a valid descriptor. A restart also works and costs a request-dropping window.

Prevent it: rotate logs with logrotate using copytruncate, or — better — with the create mode plus a postrotate script that signals the application to reopen its files (§11.5). The underlying rule is that deleting an open log file does not free space, and that is exactly what "clearing old logs" did last night.

Exercise 10.2 — The mount point that eats the disk. A machine has a 500 GB data disk mounted at /data. df -h shows /data at 4% and / at 98%. du -sh /data reports 18 GB, which is consistent with /data being nearly empty. Where is the space on /?

Solution

Almost certainly files written to /data while the disk was not mounted. They landed on the root filesystem, in the directory that later became the mount point, and are now hidden underneath the mounted filesystem — invisible to du /data, which sees the mounted filesystem, and fully counted by df /, which sees the root filesystem's blocks.

The typical history: a reboot where the /etc/fstab entry failed (missing nofail, or the device name changed as in Exercise 8.3), the service started anyway, and it wrote to /data for a week.

Confirm by looking underneath the mount:

mkdir -p /mnt/rootfs
mount --bind / /mnt/rootfs
du -sh /mnt/rootfs/data

A bind mount of / exposes the root filesystem with nothing mounted over it, so /mnt/rootfs/data is the hidden directory. umount /mnt/rootfs when done.

Fix: stop the service, umount /data, then decide — either move the stranded files onto the real disk after remounting, or delete them if the data has been superseded. Then remount and restart.

Prevent: two independent measures. Make the mount reliable (nofail plus x-systemd.requires= so the service unit is ordered after the mount unit), and make the mount point unwritable when nothing is mounted:

umount /data && chattr +i /data

An immutable, empty mount point cannot be written into by anything — including root — so the failure becomes a loud error at write time instead of a silent 500 GB surprise a week later.

Exercise 10.3 — Grow it. A logical volume /dev/vg0/app holds an ext4 filesystem mounted at /srv/app, currently 50 GB and 95% full. The volume group has 200 GB free. Give the exact commands to grow it to 120 GB with no downtime, and say what changes if the filesystem is XFS instead.

Solution 
vgs vg0
lvextend -L 120G /dev/vg0/app
resize2fs /dev/vg0/app
df -h /srv/app

Check the VG has the space first, extend the logical volume, then grow the filesystem into the new space. ext4 grows online, so /srv/app stays mounted and in use throughout; resize2fs on a mounted filesystem is supported and safe for growth only.

lvextend -r -L 120G /dev/vg0/app does both steps and picks the right resizer automatically, which is the form to prefer because it cannot get the two out of step.

Note -L 120G is an absolute target and -L +70G is a relative addition. Mixing them up is the mistake that produces a 190 GB volume, and while that is harmless here it will not be when the VG is nearly full.

With XFS, the only change is the resizer, and it takes the mount point rather than the device:

lvextend -L 120G /dev/vg0/app
xfs_growfs /srv/app

XFS also grows online. The real difference is what you cannot do afterwards: XFS can never be shrunk. If 120 GB turns out to be too much, ext4 could be reduced (unmounted, resize2fs first, then lvreduce), while XFS requires backing up, recreating and restoring. That asymmetry is the argument for extending in the increments you actually need rather than claiming the whole volume group.

One thing to check before any of this: that the filesystem is 95% full and not 95% inodes used (§10.9). Growing the volume adds no inodes to ext4, so if df -i is the number at 95%, this entire procedure changes nothing.

Exercise 10.4 — Design the layout. Specify the disk layout for a 1 TB server that will run a PostgreSQL database and accept user file uploads. Give the partitions, filesystems and mount options, and justify each decision.

Solution 
GPT partition table
  /dev/sda1     1 MiB   BIOS boot (or 512 MiB EFI System Partition for UEFI)
  /dev/sda2     2 GiB   ext4          /boot
  /dev/sda3     rest    LVM PV        vg0

vg0:
  lv_root      40 GiB   ext4          /
  lv_var       40 GiB   ext4          /var
  lv_pgdata   300 GiB   ext4          /var/lib/postgresql
  lv_uploads  200 GiB   ext4          /srv/uploads
  (~370 GiB left unallocated)

/dev/vg0/lv_root      /                    ext4  defaults                       0 1
/dev/vg0/lv_var       /var                 ext4  defaults,nodev,nosuid          0 2
/dev/vg0/lv_pgdata    /var/lib/postgresql  ext4  defaults,noatime,nodev,nosuid  0 2
/dev/vg0/lv_uploads   /srv/uploads         ext4  defaults,noexec,nosuid,nodev   0 2

Separate /var so that runaway logs or a package cache cannot fill / and make the machine unable to log in or write anything. This is the single highest-value split.

Separate database and upload volumes, so that neither can starve the other and each can be sized, snapshotted and backed up independently.

noexec,nosuid,nodev on uploads is the point of the split. Untrusted user content should never be executable, and the mount option removes "upload a binary, then run it" as a step regardless of what the application does with permissions. nodev,nosuid on the others is free.

noatime on the database volume removes a metadata write for every read. Postgres has no use for access times, and the write amplification is real.

Roughly a third of the volume group left unallocated is the deliberate part, and the reason for using LVM at all. You cannot know in advance whether uploads or the database will grow faster; leaving free extents means the answer costs one lvextend -r and no downtime, whereas a fully allocated layout means shrinking something — the one operation you want to avoid. Do not carve out space you have no use for yet.

ext4 rather than XFS here because ext4 can be shrunk in a pinch and the workload is not large enough for XFS's parallel-write advantage to matter. On a database volume above a few terabytes, or with many concurrent writers, XFS is the better answer — accepting that it cannot be shrunk.

What is deliberately absent: a separate /home (no interactive users), a separate /tmp partition (tmpfs is already used and is bounded by RAM), and encryption (add LUKS beneath the PV if the threat model includes physical access — it belongs under LVM, not above it, so one unlock covers every volume).

SECTION 11

Logging, Time and Observability

When something is wrong, three questions come in order: what happened, when did it happen, and what is the machine doing right now. This section is about the tools for each — the journal and syslog for the first, clocks and time synchronisation for the second, and the small set of counters that answer the third.

They belong together because they fail together. A log line with a wrong timestamp is worse than no log line: it puts the cause after the effect, and correlating two machines whose clocks differ by ninety seconds produces a story that is confidently, precisely wrong.

11.1 Two Logging Systems on One Machine

Modern Linux runs journald and usually also rsyslog, and they are not alternatives — on most distributions both are present and one feeds the other.

systemd-journald collects everything: the kernel ring buffer, every service's stdout and stderr, syslog calls from libraries, and audit records. It stores them in an indexed binary format with structured metadata attached to each entry — unit name, PID, UID, boot ID, source file, cgroup — which is what makes filtering by anything other than text possible.
rsyslog reads from journald and writes plain text into /var/log/, and forwards to a central log server. Text files are what every other tool in the world can read, and forwarding is what journald does not do well.

The binary format is the trade. It buys indexed queries, structured fields and cryptographic sealing; it costs the ability to grep a file directly, and it means a corrupted journal needs journalctl --verify rather than a text editor. In practice you query the journal on the machine and ship text or structured records elsewhere.

11.2 journalctl

The whole tool is filters, and they compose.

-u nginx.service
One unit. The most common filter by a wide margin.
-f
Follow, like tail -f. Combine with -u.
-n 50
Last 50 lines.
-b, -b -1
This boot; the previous boot. --list-boots enumerates them.
-p err
Priority and above: emerg alert crit err warning notice info debug.
--since, --until
Accepts "2026-08-26 20:00", "1 hour ago", yesterday, today.
-k
Kernel messages only — dmesg, but persisted across reboots (§8.9).
_PID=, _UID=, _COMM=
Structured field matches. journalctl -o verbose shows every field available.
-o json-pretty
Structured output for machine consumption; -o short-iso for unambiguous timestamps.
-g pattern
Grep the message text, with the other filters still applied.
journalctl -p err -n 3 --no-pager

Aug 26 20:15:35 devbox kernel: Memory cgroup out of memory: Killed process 39246 (python3) total-vm:82676kB, anon-rss:65304kB, file-rss:7544kB, shmem-rss:0kB, UID:1000 pgtables:200kB oom_score_adj:0
Aug 26 20:21:47 devbox networkctl[44732]: Interface "vethad83ae3" not found.
Aug 26 20:21:47 devbox networkctl[44736]: Interface "veth5ea099c" not found.

That first line is the cgroup memory limit from §15.8 seen from the other side: the exact experiment that produced exit status 137 is recorded here, with the process name, the memory it held at death, and the fact that a cgroup rather than the system was out of memory. That is the value of journalctl -p err as a first command on any machine — the kernel's account of what it killed and why is in the same stream as everything else.

The next two lines are the same event from a different angle: veth interfaces (§13.9) disappearing as containers exited.

--no-pager matters in scripts and in any context where a pager would block. -o short-iso gives timestamps with a timezone, which is what you want when comparing against another machine.

Two invocations worth memorising outright:

journalctl -b -p err --no-pager
journalctl -u myapp.service --since "10 min ago" -f

The first is "what went wrong since this machine booted" and is the right opening move on any unfamiliar system. The second is what you leave running in a second terminal while you reproduce a bug.

11.3 Storage, Persistence and Size

By default on many systems the journal lives in /run/log/journal — tmpfs, so it is gone at reboot. That is the single most surprising journald behaviour, because the log you most want is the one from before the machine restarted.

journalctl --disk-usage

Archived and active journals take up 444.5M in the file system.

To get a persistent journal, mkdir -p /var/log/journal and restart journald, or set Storage=persistent in /etc/systemd/journald.conf. A persistent journal is what makes journalctl -b -1 — the previous boot — answerable at all, which is exactly the log you want after an unexplained reboot. The bounds are configured there too:

[Journal]
Storage=persistent
SystemMaxUse=1G
SystemMaxFileSize=128M
MaxRetentionSec=1month

Without limits journald uses up to 10% of the filesystem, capped so as to leave 15% free — reasonable defaults that are still worth pinning explicitly on a machine where /var is small. Manual cleanup is journalctl --vacuum-size=500M or --vacuum-time=7d.

Two more operational facts. journalctl --verify checks integrity, and ForwardToSyslog=, ForwardToKMsg= and ForwardToConsole= control who else receives entries — turning on console forwarding on a busy machine will flood a serial console and is a classic self-inflicted outage. And you can read a journal from a broken machine: journalctl --file /mnt/broken/var/log/journal/*/system.journal or -D /mnt/broken/var/log/journal, which is how you diagnose a system that no longer boots by mounting its disk elsewhere.

11.4 Getting Things Into the Log

A service writing to stdout is already logging. systemd captures both streams and tags every line with the unit, so an application inside a unit needs no log file, no rotation and no permissions — it should just print. StandardOutput= and StandardError= control the destination if you need something else.

For shell scripts, logger sends a line to the system log:

logger -t backup -p daemon.info "nightly backup finished in ${SECONDS}s"
logger -t backup -p daemon.err --stderr "backup FAILED: rsync exit $rc"

-t sets the tag you will later filter on (journalctl -t backup), and -p sets facility and priority (§11.5). This is the right answer to the cron-output problem in §9.11: instead of redirecting to a file nobody rotates, log with a tag and get filtering, timestamps, rotation and forwarding for nothing.

systemd-cat runs a command with both streams connected to the journal — systemd-cat -t import ./import.sh — which is the same idea for a whole program.

11.5 rsyslog: Facilities, Priorities and Actions

Syslog's model is nearly fifty years old and still structures everything. Every message carries a facility (who is speaking) and a priority (how bad it is), and the combination selects an action.

Facilities: auth, authpriv, cron, daemon, kern, lpr, mail, news, syslog, user, uucp, and local0 through local7 — the last eight being deliberately unassigned and therefore the right place for your own applications.

Priorities, most to least severe: emerg, alert, crit, err, warning, notice, info, debug.

A rule is a selector and a target:

auth,authpriv.*                 /var/log/auth.log
*.*;auth,authpriv.none          -/var/log/syslog
cron.*                          /var/log/cron.log
mail.err                        /var/log/mail.err
*.emerg                         :omusrmsg:*
local0.*                        @@logserver.example.com:6514

Three details in that block carry real meaning. .none excludes a facility from a catch-all, which is how auth messages stay out of the general syslog. The leading - means asynchronous writes — do not fsync after every line, which matters enormously for a busy log. And @@ means TCP while a single @ means UDP: UDP syslog silently drops messages under load, which is precisely the moment you need them.

Central logging is rsyslog's main remaining advantage over journald, and the reason to keep it. Logs on the machine are lost when the machine is lost — and an attacker's first move is to edit them. A remote log host is the only copy that is not under the control of whoever compromised the source.

11.6 logrotate

Files under /var/log grow without limit unless something intervenes, and logrotate is that something. It runs from a timer or cron (§9.11) and works from /etc/logrotate.conf plus drop-ins in /etc/logrotate.d/.

head -20 /etc/logrotate.conf

# see "man logrotate" for details

# global options do not affect preceding include directives

# rotate log files weekly
weekly

# use the adm group by default, since this is the owning group
# of /var/log/.
su root adm

# keep 4 weeks worth of backlogs
rotate 4

# create new (empty) log files after rotating old ones
create

A per-application drop-in overrides those globals:

/var/log/myapp/*.log {
    daily
    rotate 14
    compress
    delaycompress
    missingok
    notifempty
    create 0640 myapp adm
    sharedscripts
    postrotate
        systemctl reload myapp.service > /dev/null 2>&1 || true
    endscript
}

The postrotate block is the part that matters, and understanding why requires §10.9. Renaming a log file does not disturb the process writing to it — the process holds an open file descriptor to the inode, so it keeps writing to the renamed file, and the new empty file stays empty forever. Meanwhile the renamed file is eventually deleted while still open, so its disk space is never reclaimed. Both halves of that failure are invisible until the disk fills.

There are two correct fixes:

  • postrotate + reload — signal the application to reopen its files. This is the clean answer, and it is why SIGHUP conventionally means "reopen" (§7.4).
  • copytruncate — copy the file's contents aside and truncate the original in place, so the descriptor stays valid. Use it only when the application cannot be signalled: it loses any lines written between the copy and the truncate, and it briefly doubles the disk usage.

logrotate -d is a dry run showing exactly what would happen; logrotate -f forces a rotation for testing. delaycompress exists for a specific reason: it postpones compression by one cycle so a process that has not yet reopened is not writing into a file being gzipped.

11.7 Clocks

A Linux machine has two clocks. The hardware clock (RTC) is a battery-backed chip that keeps time while the machine is off. The system clock is maintained by the kernel and is what everything actually reads. At boot the system clock is initialised from the hardware clock and then corrected by NTP; hwclock is the tool that reads and writes the hardware clock directly, with hwclock --systohc copying the system clock down to it and hwclock --hctosys doing the reverse. On a systemd machine timedatectl calls hwclock for you, so you rarely invoke it — but on a machine whose clock is wrong at every boot, a stale hardware clock read by hwclock is the thing to check.

timedatectl

               Local time: Wed 2026-08-26 20:47:49 UTC
           Universal time: Wed 2026-08-26 20:47:49 UTC
                 RTC time: Wed 2026-08-26 20:47:49
                Time zone: Etc/UTC (UTC, +0000)
System clock synchronized: yes
              NTP service: active
          RTC in local TZ: no

timedatectl is the single interface: set-timezone, set-time, set-ntp true|false, list-timezones. Timezone data lives in /usr/share/zoneinfo/, and /etc/localtime is a symlink into it. The zoneinfo database is a compiled set of files — one per zone, with the historical table of offsets and daylight-saving transitions for that place — and it is updated by a package like any other, which matters because governments change timezone rules with a few weeks' notice and a stale zoneinfo package means a machine that is an hour wrong on one specific date.

RTC in local TZ: no is the right answer. Keeping the hardware clock in UTC and applying a timezone in software means daylight-saving transitions are a display concern, not a clock concern. Keeping it in local time — which Windows does, and which dual-boot machines therefore inherit — means the two operating systems disagree twice a year and each "corrects" the other.

Set the timezone of servers to UTC and leave it there. Not because UTC is nicer, but because logs from machines in different regions must be comparable without arithmetic, and because a timezone with daylight saving has an hour that occurs twice a year and an hour that does not occur at all — which is a genuine source of duplicated and skipped scheduled jobs.

The kernel offers several clocks, and the distinction matters when you write code. CLOCK_REALTIME is wall-clock time and can jump backwards when NTP corrects it. CLOCK_MONOTONIC only ever moves forward and is the one to measure durations with. Timing a request with wall-clock time is how you get a negative latency in your metrics.

11.8 NTP and chrony

chronyc tracking

Reference ID    : 5BBD5B71 (ntp-nts-3.ps6.canonical.com)
Stratum         : 3
Ref time (UTC)  : Wed Aug 26 20:44:22 2026
System time     : 0.000739446 seconds slow of NTP time
Last offset     : +0.000321417 seconds
RMS offset      : 0.006402932 seconds
Frequency       : 24.764 ppm fast
Residual freq   : +0.009 ppm

Read that as a health report. Stratum 3 means three hops from a reference clock. System time 0.0007 seconds slow is the current error — sub-millisecond, which is fine. And Frequency 24.764 ppm fast is the interesting number: this machine's crystal runs about 25 parts per million fast, which is roughly two seconds per day, and chrony is compensating continuously. That is what an unsynchronised machine drifts by, and it is why "the clock was right last week" is not evidence of anything.

The important behaviour is slewing versus stepping. Rather than jumping the clock, chrony adjusts its rate so it converges gradually — because a backwards jump breaks make, database replication, certificate validity checks and any code using CLOCK_REALTIME for durations. Steps happen only when the offset is too large to slew in reasonable time, which is why makestep is normally allowed for the first few updates after boot and not afterwards.

chronyc sources -v shows the servers being used and which one is selected. pool.ntp.org is a DNS round-robin over several thousand volunteer servers, and the convention is to configure 0.pool.ntp.org through 3.pool.ntp.org so a client gets four independent servers and can detect one that is wrong; using pool.ntp.org once gives you a single source with nothing to compare against. pool.ntp.org is the volunteer pool most defaults point at, though distributions increasingly ship their own — this machine uses Canonical's, with NTS (authenticated NTP), which matters because plain NTP is unauthenticated UDP and therefore spoofable.

ntpd is the older implementation and systemd-timesyncd a minimal SNTP client that is adequate for a laptop and not for a server. ntpdate is deprecated: it steps the clock in one jump, which is exactly the behaviour to avoid.

11.9 Locale

A locale decides how text is compared, how numbers and dates are formatted, and which character encoding is assumed.

locale

LANG=en_US.UTF-8
LC_CTYPE="en_US.UTF-8"
LC_NUMERIC="en_US.UTF-8"
LC_TIME="en_US.UTF-8"
LC_COLLATE="en_US.UTF-8"
LC_MESSAGES="en_US.UTF-8"
LC_ALL=

LANG is the default for every category; an individual LC_* overrides it for its category; and LC_ALL overrides everything, which is why it should be empty in normal use and is the right hammer inside a script.

LC_COLLATE is the one that will bite you. Sort order is locale-dependent: in en_US.UTF-8, sort ignores case and punctuation in ways that surprise people, so a, B, c sorts as written rather than B, a, c. A script that sorts, compares, or expects [a-z] to mean twenty-six letters produces different output for different users — and then fails only in production, where the locale is C.

The fix is one line at the top of any script whose output must be stable:

export LC_ALL=C

C (equivalently POSIX) is the byte-order locale: sorting is by byte value, [a-z] is exactly what it looks like, and messages are unlocalised English. LC_ALL=C.UTF-8 keeps byte-order collation while still handling multi-byte characters correctly, and is usually the better choice for modern text.

Two related notes. LANG=C also makes tools print English error messages, which is what you want when you are going to search for one. And iconv -f ISO-8859-1 -t UTF-8 converts encodings between files, and iconv -l lists the several hundred encodings it knows. iconv fails loudly on a byte sequence that is not valid in the source encoding, which is a feature — iconv -c drops such bytes silently and should only be used when you have decided the data is already damaged — needed whenever a legacy export arrives with accented characters rendered as Ã©, which is UTF-8 bytes interpreted as Latin-1.

11.10 What the Machine Is Doing Right Now

A small number of tools cover almost everything, and the skill is knowing which number in each actually means something.

free -h

               total        used        free      shared  buff/cache   available
Mem:           3.3Gi       1.4Gi       168Mi        22Mi       2.0Gi       1.9Gi
Swap:          8.0Gi       2.7Mi       8.0Gi

available, not free (§8.3). 168 MiB free looks alarming and is irrelevant; 1.9 GiB available out of 3.3 is healthy, because 2.0 GiB of cache is reclaimable.

vmstat 1 3

 r  b   swpd   free   buff  cache   si   so    bi    bo   in   cs us sy id wa st gu
 0  0   2736 174200  40240 2086388    7   13   550   287 1693    8 11 23 66  0  0  0
 0  0   2736 172940  40240 2086416    0    0     0   164 1617  880  5  8 86  0  0  0
 0  0   2736 172448  40240 2086416    0    0     0     0 1637  851  2  8 89  0  0  0

The first row is an average since boot and should be ignored; read from the second. The columns that carry information:

r
Processes runnable and waiting for CPU. Sustained above the core count means the CPU is the bottleneck.
b
Processes blocked on I/O. The state D from §7.3.
si/so
Swap in / swap out, in KB per second. Rate, not total. Sustained non-zero is thrashing; the 2.7 MiB of swap in use here is nothing.
cs
Context switches per second. A very high number with low us means the machine is spending its time switching rather than working.
wa
I/O wait percentage.

free -h and uptime are the two five-second checks. free -h gives the memory picture above; uptime gives how long the machine has been up and the three load averages, and an uptime of eleven minutes on a machine nobody rebooted is itself the incident.

iostat -xz 1 is the disk equivalent: r_await/w_await are the average milliseconds a request waited — the number a user actually feels — and %util is the fraction of time the device had a request in flight. %util is misleading on modern hardware: an NVMe drive handling dozens of parallel requests can read 100% while being nowhere near saturated, because the metric was designed for a device that serviced one request at a time. Trust await and queue depth over %util.

iostat takes the same interval-and-count arguments as vmstat, so iostat -xz 1 5 gives five one-second samples and exits; iostat -xz with no interval prints averages since boot, which is almost never what you want.

The rest of the toolkit, briefly: uptime for load averages read as a gradient (§8.3); lsof for "who has this file open" and fuser -k for the blunt version; sar (from sysstat) for historical data, which is the one thing all the others lack — it answers "what did the machine look like at 3 a.m. last Tuesday" and needs to have been enabled before the incident.

And the framework worth carrying: for each resource — CPU, memory, disk, network — check utilisation, saturation and errors. Utilisation alone routinely misleads (a full page cache, a busy NVMe, %util at 100%); saturation is the queue behind the resource and is what users feel; errors are silent until you look. Pressure stall information (§15.8) measures saturation directly, and is the modern answer to the whole question.

Exercises

Exercise 11.1 — The log that stopped. A service's log file /var/log/myapp/app.log has been zero bytes since last Sunday. The service is running and working. logrotate runs weekly. Explain, and give both fixes.

Solution

logrotate renamed the file and the application never reopened it. The process holds an open descriptor to the inode, not to the name — renaming app.log to app.log.1 does not disturb the descriptor, so the application keeps writing into the renamed file while the fresh app.log stays empty forever.

Two consequences, and the second is worse: the old file is eventually deleted by rotate N while still open, so its disk space is never reclaimed (§10.9) and the application's log output is going into a file with no name. lsof +L1 shows exactly that, and ls -l /proc/PID/fd shows the descriptor pointing at app.log.1 (deleted).

Fix 1 — signal the application to reopen. The clean answer:

postrotate
    systemctl reload myapp.service > /dev/null 2>&1 || true
endscript

This requires the application to handle SIGHUP (or whatever reload sends) by closing and reopening its log files, which is the convention §7.4 describes.

Fix 2 — copytruncate, when the application cannot be signalled:

copytruncate

logrotate copies the contents aside and truncates the original in place, so the descriptor stays valid and points at a now-empty file. The costs are real and worth stating: lines written between the copy and the truncate are lost, and the disk briefly holds two copies.

The better fix than either is to stop writing to a file at all. A service under systemd that prints to stdout is captured by journald (§11.4), with rotation, size limits, structured metadata and filtering already handled and no possibility of this failure. Log files exist mostly because applications were written before that was true.

Test before trusting: logrotate -d /etc/logrotate.d/myapp dry-runs it, and logrotate -f forces a real rotation so you can check that the application resumed writing to the new file.

Exercise 11.2 — Two machines, one incident. An API on web01 calls a service on web02. The API logs a request at 14:32:07 and a timeout at 14:32:37. web02 logs receiving that request at 14:31:52 and responding at 14:31:53. What is going on, and what do you check?

Solution

The clocks disagree by about fifteen seconds. web02 recorded the request before web01 sent it, which is impossible, so at least one clock is wrong. Taken at face value the logs say the response arrived fourteen seconds before the request was made — which is the tell that this is a clock problem and not a distributed-systems puzzle.

Note also that web02 answered in one second while web01 timed out after thirty. That means the response was produced promptly and either never arrived or was not read — a network or client problem — and the clock skew is hiding that by making the timeline nonsensical.

Check, on both machines:

timedatectl
chronyc tracking
chronyc sources -v
journalctl -u chrony --since today

System clock synchronized: no, or NTP service: inactive, or a large System time ... seconds slow of NTP time, identifies the offender. Frequency ... ppm shows how fast the machine drifts when unsynchronised — a 25 ppm error is about two seconds a day, so fifteen seconds is roughly a week without NTP.

Common causes: UDP port 123 blocked outbound by a firewall (the machine tries forever and never succeeds); NTP disabled in a VM template; a container inheriting the host clock while the host is unsynchronised; or a hypervisor pausing and resuming a VM, which is the case where a step rather than a slew is legitimately needed.

Fix and prevent: timedatectl set-ntp true, verify chronyc tracking converges, and monitor the offset as a metric — an alert on System time exceeding a threshold catches this before an incident does.

Then go back to the real bug, which the skew obscured: web02 responded in one second and web01 waited thirty and gave up. Look at the network path, the client's read timeout, and whether the response was large enough to be affected by an MTU problem.

Exercise 11.3 — The script that sorts differently. A deduplication script works on a developer's laptop and produces duplicate entries in production. It ends with sort | uniq. Explain, and give the fix.

Solution

Locale-dependent collation (§11.9). sort orders according to LC_COLLATE. In en_US.UTF-8 the collation rules ignore case and punctuation at the primary comparison level, so Foo-Bar, foobar and FOO BAR can sort adjacently and in an order that depends on the exact locale data version. In C, sorting is by byte value and is completely deterministic.

uniq only removes adjacent duplicates, so the pairing is fragile in both directions: entries that should be adjacent may not be, and entries that sort adjacently under one locale may not under another. The developer's laptop is en_US.UTF-8; production is C or POSIX (the default for a systemd service, which inherits almost no environment), so the two produce different groupings from identical input.

The fix:

export LC_ALL=C

at the top of the script — or, better, LC_ALL=C sort | uniq on the pipeline itself so the setting is visible where it matters. LC_ALL=C.UTF-8 is preferable if the data contains multi-byte characters that must be handled correctly, since it keeps byte-order collation while still treating UTF-8 sequences as characters.

A second, independent bug to fix at the same time: sort | uniq should be sort -u, which is one process instead of two and cannot get the adjacency wrong. And if the intent is case-insensitive deduplication, that must be stated explicitly — sort -uf — rather than obtained by accident from a locale.

The general rule: any script whose output is compared, diffed, checksummed or consumed by another program should pin LC_ALL. The failure mode is not a crash; it is a different answer on a different machine, which is the hardest kind to notice.

Exercise 11.4 — Slow, and everything is green. Users report a service is slow. CPU is 30% idle, free -h shows 1.9 GiB available, disk %util on the NVMe reads 20%, and there are no errors in the log. Where do you look, and why does each dashboard number mislead?

Solution

Every number quoted is a utilisation figure, and utilisation does not measure waiting. A resource can be far from saturated on average and still have a queue that users feel.

Look at saturation directly, which on a modern kernel means pressure stall information:

cat /proc/pressure/cpu /proc/pressure/io /proc/pressure/memory
cat /sys/fs/cgroup/<unit-cgroup>/{cpu,io,memory}.pressure

some avg10 is the fraction of time at least one task was stalled on that resource; full avg10 is the fraction where everything was. Non-trivial some on a resource whose utilisation looks fine is the exact situation described, and it names the resource in one read.

Why each number misleads:

  • CPU 30% idle is an average across cores and across a sampling interval. One saturated core with a single-threaded hot path, or bursts that queue for 200 ms every second, both average out to "fine". Check vmstat 1's r column against the core count, and per-core with mpstat -P ALL 1.
  • 1.9 GiB available says allocation will succeed, not that the working set fits. If the page cache is being churned, every request does disk I/O that used to be free. vmstat's si/so and the major-fault count in /proc/vmstat show it.
  • %util 20% on NVMe is close to meaningless (§11.10): the metric assumes a device serving one request at a time. r_await/w_await from iostat -xz 1 are the latencies that matter.
  • No errors in the log is the weakest evidence of all. Slow is not an error, and a service that is meeting its timeouts logs nothing.

The other candidates, once the machine's own resources are cleared: a downstream dependency (the service is waiting, not working — ss -tn state established and the request-duration histogram distinguish these), lock contention inside the application (invisible to every tool here; a profiler or SIGQUIT for a thread dump), and a noisy neighbour on a VM, which shows as non-zero st in top and cannot be fixed from inside the machine at all.

SECTION 12

Packages, Shared Libraries and Building from Source

Almost nothing on a Linux system was put there by hand. A running machine is a few thousand packages, each a compressed archive of files plus metadata saying what it needs and what it provides, assembled by a dependency solver into something coherent. Understanding that system is what lets you answer the questions that come up constantly: where did this file come from, why can I not install this version, what will apt upgrade actually do, and why does this binary refuse to start on a machine that has the library it is asking for.

The second half of the section is the layer beneath: shared libraries, the dynamic linker, and what happens between typing a command and its main running. That is where "works on my machine" is usually decided.

12.1 What a Package Is

Files
A tree to unpack, with paths, permissions and ownership.
Metadata
Name, version, architecture, description, maintainer.
Dependencies
What must be present. Also conflicts, provides (a virtual name several packages can satisfy) and replaces.
Scripts
Hooks that run before and after install and removal — creating a user, generating keys, running ldconfig, restarting a service.
Signature
A cryptographic signature over the repository index, which is what makes the whole chain trustworthy.

Two families dominate. .deb (Debian, Ubuntu) with dpkg at the low level and apt above it. .rpm (RHEL, Fedora, SUSE) with rpm low and dnf/yum/zypper above. The split is consistent and worth internalising: the low-level tool operates on a file and refuses to resolve dependencies; the high-level tool talks to repositories and resolves.

dpkg -l | grep -c '^ii'

1154

Eleven hundred packages on an ordinary machine, each version-pinned and each with a maintainer who backports security fixes. That is the argument for using the distribution's packages over installing things by hand: the alternative is being that maintainer for every dependency you introduce.

12.2 Debian: dpkg and apt

apt update
Refresh the package indexes. Changes nothing else. Always before installing, or you resolve against stale metadata.
apt install pkg
Install with dependencies.
apt upgrade
Upgrade what can be upgraded without removing anything.
apt full-upgrade
Upgrade, removing packages if that is what the new dependency graph requires. Needed across releases; dangerous unattended.
apt remove / purge
Remove the files; purge also removes configuration in /etc.
apt autoremove
Remove packages installed only as dependencies of something now gone. The usual fix for a full /boot.
apt search / show
Find and describe.

Dependency resolution is the whole reason the high-level tools exist: given "install nginx", the solver finds a set of package versions that satisfies every declared dependency and violates no conflict, then orders the installation so that nothing is configured before what it needs. When dependency resolution fails, the message names the unsatisfiable constraint, and reading it is faster than any amount of --force.

apt is the interactive front end; apt-get is the one to use in scripts, because apt's output format is explicitly not stable and it says so when run non-interactively.

The questions that come up in practice all have one-line answers:

dpkg -S /usr/bin/curl
dpkg -L curl | head -6

curl: /usr/bin/curl
/.
/usr
/usr/bin
/usr/bin/curl
/usr/bin/wcurl
/usr/share

dpkg -S maps a file to its package — the answer to "where did this come from and can I remove it". dpkg -L does the reverse. For a file from a package that is not installed, apt-file search is the equivalent, which is how you find that ldd needs libc-bin.

apt-cache policy is the most underused command in the set:

apt-cache policy curl

curl:
  Installed: 8.18.0-1ubuntu2.4
  Candidate: 8.18.0-1ubuntu2.4
  Version table:
 *** 8.18.0-1ubuntu2.4 500
        500 http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu resolute-updates/main amd64 Packages
        500 http://security.ubuntu.com/ubuntu resolute-security/main amd64 Packages
        100 /var/lib/dpkg/status

It shows what is installed, what would be installed, every version available, and which repository each comes from with its priority. When someone says "apt is installing the wrong version", this output is the answer: the number on the left of each source is its pin priority, and the candidate is simply the highest-priority available version.

dpkg -i installs a local .deb and fails on missing dependencies rather than fetching them — apt install ./file.deb is the form that resolves. The old advice of running apt --fix-broken install afterwards exists because of exactly that difference.

Package states from dpkg -l are worth being able to read: the first character is the desired state and the second the actual one, so ii is installed, rc is removed-but-configuration-remains (what purge cleans up), and iU or iF is a half-configured package that needs dpkg --configure -a.

12.3 RPM: rpm, dnf and zypper

The mapping is nearly one to one, which makes moving between families easy:

apt update
dnf check-update — and dnf refreshes metadata automatically, so it is rarely needed
apt install
dnf install · zypper install
apt upgrade
dnf upgrade · zypper update
dpkg -S file
rpm -qf file
dpkg -L pkg
rpm -ql pkg
dpkg -l
rpm -qa
apt-file search
dnf provides '*/file'

yum is the older Red Hat front end and dnf is its replacement; on RHEL 8 and later yum is a symlink to dnf, so yum install and dnf install are literally the same command and a runbook written for yum still works. Two things rpm does that dpkg does not. rpm -V pkg verifies every installed file against the recorded checksum, size, permissions and ownership, printing a code per difference — the fastest integrity check available on a machine you suspect has been tampered with, and equally useful for "who edited this config". And dnf history records every transaction with an ID, so dnf history undo 42 rolls back an upgrade — genuinely better than anything on the Debian side.

rpm2cpio pkg.rpm | cpio -idmv extracts a package's contents without installing it — rpm2cpio converts the package into a plain cpio archive on standard output, and cpio unpacks it into the current directory, which is how you retrieve one file from a package you do not want.

12.4 Repositories and Trust

head -8 /etc/apt/sources.list.d/ubuntu.sources

Types: deb
URIs: http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu/
Suites: resolute resolute-updates resolute-backports
Components: main restricted universe multiverse
Signed-By: /usr/share/keyrings/ubuntu-archive-keyring.gpg

This is the deb822 format, which replaced the old one-line deb http://... suite components entries. It is more verbose and much clearer, and critically it makes Signed-By a per-repository property.

Signed-By is the whole security model. The repository publishes an index listing every package with its checksum, and attaches a package signature over that index. Note where the signature actually is: it covers the index, and the index covers each package by checksum, so one package signature protects thousands of files transitively. Your machine verifies that signature against a specific keyring, then trusts the checksums, then verifies each downloaded package against them. Break any link and the chain is worthless.

Which is why the old advice — apt-key add — is deprecated and should never be used: it added a key to a global keyring trusted for every repository, so a key added for one third-party PPA could sign a replacement for libc6. The modern form scopes the key to one repository:

curl -fsSL https://example.com/key.gpg | gpg --dearmor -o /usr/share/keyrings/example.gpg

with Signed-By: /usr/share/keyrings/example.gpg in the .sources file. Note also that the transport is HTTP in the output above and that this is fine: integrity comes from the signature, not the transport. HTTPS adds privacy about which packages you fetch, and nothing else.

Pinning controls which repository wins when several offer a package. apt-cache policy shows the priorities; /etc/apt/preferences.d/ sets them. A common and correct pattern is to add a third-party repository at low priority so it provides only the one package you want, rather than silently taking over anything it happens to also ship:

Package: *
Pin: origin example.com
Pin-Priority: 100

Unattended upgrades are worth enabling for security updates specifically (unattended-upgrades on Debian and Ubuntu, dnf-automatic on Fedora). The argument is empirical: unpatched known vulnerabilities cause far more incidents than automatic patches do. Restrict them to the security pocket, and pair them with needrestart — because a patched library does nothing until the processes using it are restarted (§12.9).

12.5 Snap, Flatpak and the Universal Formats

These bundle an application with its dependencies and run it under the confinement mechanisms of §15.

Snap
Canonical's format. A snap package is a squashfs image mounted at runtime — which is why an Ubuntu machine has a dozen loop devices in lsblk (§10.1). Auto-updates, and confined by AppArmor.
Flatpak
Desktop-focused, with shared runtimes so applications do not each carry a whole GNOME stack. Sandboxed with bubblewrap — the same tool as §15.12.
AppImage
A single executable file containing everything. No installation, no daemon, no confinement, no updates.

A snap package is therefore visible in two places an ordinary package is not: snap list rather than dpkg -l, and a loop device per installed snap in lsblk.

The trade is the same in each case: current versions and isolation, at the cost of duplicated libraries and — importantly — security updates that are the application vendor's responsibility rather than the distribution's. A vulnerable libssl bundled inside twenty snaps is twenty separate updates by twenty parties. For a server, prefer distribution packages; for a desktop application that needs to be current, these formats earn their place.

12.6 Building from Source

When there is no package, the classic sequence is three commands:

./configure --prefix=/usr/local
make -j"$(nproc)"
make install
