lsns shows the namespaces on the system. On an ordinary desktop session, everything shares the initial set:

lsns

        NS TYPE   NPROCS   PID USER          COMMAND
4026531832 mnt        16  1838 dev           -zsh
4026531833 net        16  1838 dev           -zsh
4026531834 time       16  1838 dev           -zsh
4026531835 cgroup     16  1838 dev           -zsh
4026531836 pid        16  1838 dev           -zsh
4026531837 user       16  1838 dev           -zsh
4026531838 uts        16  1838 dev           -zsh
4026531839 ipc        16  1838 dev           -zsh

Three of the eight are cheap enough that runtimes take them without thinking. The UTS namespace isolates only the hostname and domain name, which is what lets a container call itself web01 while the host is node-7; the IPC namespace isolates System V message queues, semaphores and shared memory, so two containers cannot collide on a shared-memory key; and the cgroup namespace changes only what a process sees as its cgroup root, so a container reads its own limits at /sys/fs/cgroup rather than seeing its position in the host's tree. The time namespace is the newest and the least used: it offsets the monotonic and boot clocks — useful for checkpoint-and-restore, where a restored process must not see time jump backwards — and it deliberately leaves the wall clock alone, so a time namespace cannot be used to lie to a program about the date.

Those NS numbers are inode numbers in the nsfs pseudo-filesystem. Every namespace is reachable as a file under /proc/PID/ns/, and two processes are in the same namespace exactly when those symlinks point at the same inode:

ls -l /proc/self/ns/
readlink /proc/self/ns/net

That file is not a curiosity — it is the handle. nsenter --target PID --mount --pid --net opens those files for another process and calls setns(), putting your shell in its namespaces; nsenter -t PID -a takes all of them. nsenter is the tool for debugging a container that has no shell in its image — you enter its namespaces from the host with the host's tools. Holding it open keeps a namespace alive after every process in it has exited, and nsenter works by opening it and calling setns(). This is how docker exec gets a shell inside a running container: same namespaces, new process.

15.3 A Namespace Is Not a Permission

This is the most important idea in the section, and it is easiest to prove by breaking something.

bubblewrap is on this machine, so we can create namespaces without being root. Ask for a new PID namespace and nothing else:

bwrap --ro-bind / / --unshare-pid /bin/sh -c 'echo "pid=$$"; ls /proc | grep -c "^[0-9]"'

pid=2
155

Read that carefully. The shell is PID 2 — it is genuinely in a new PID namespace, with its own numbering starting from 1. And it can see 155 host processes, because /proc is still the host's /proc, bind-mounted in from outside.

The PID namespace changed what PIDs mean. It did not change what the process can read. Those are separate mechanisms, and if you only take the namespace you get exactly half the isolation you assumed.

Mount a fresh procfs — which the kernel renders relative to the reader's PID namespace — and the view collapses:

bwrap --ro-bind / / --unshare-pid --proc /proc /bin/sh -c 'echo "pid=$$"; ls /proc | grep -c "^[0-9]"'

pid=2
4

Four entries, and they are this shell and its children. Same PID namespace as before; different mount namespace contents. The isolation you observe is the product of two namespaces, not one, and every container runtime mounts a private /proc for precisely this reason.

The same lesson holds for the network namespace, where the confinement is real because there is nothing to bind in:

bwrap --ro-bind / / --unshare-net /bin/sh -c 'ip -o link show'

1: lo: <LOOPBACK,UP,LOWER_UP> mtu 65536 qdisc noqueue state UNKNOWN mode DEFAULT group default qlen 1000\    link/loopback 00:00:00:00:00:00 brd 00:00:00:00:00:00

One loopback interface, no route to anywhere, and no way for the process to reach the network — not because it lacks a right, but because in its world no network exists. A firewall rule can be wrong; an empty network namespace cannot be argued with. This is why --unshare-net is a far stronger statement than any nftables rule you could write, and why the sandbox in §5.7 of the Building with Claude course needs a proxy process (socat) to give a sandboxed command any network access at all: there is no interface inside to configure.

UTS is the cheapest illustration of the same shape:

bwrap --ro-bind / / --unshare-uts --hostname sandbox /bin/sh -c 'hostname'

sandbox

The hostname changed for that process tree and for nothing else on the machine. No configuration file was touched, and no privilege was involved.

15.4 User Namespaces and the uid_map

The user namespace is the one that makes all the others usable by ordinary users, and it is the one people get wrong.

Creating any other namespace normally requires CAP_SYS_ADMIN — a capability an unprivileged user does not have. But creating a user namespace requires nothing, and inside a fresh user namespace the creating process is given a full set of capabilities — over the namespace's own resources. That is the trick: unprivileged user creates a user namespace, becomes "root" in it, and uses that "root" to create the mount, PID and network namespaces they actually wanted.

Watch what "root" means there:

bwrap --ro-bind / / --unshare-user /bin/sh -c 'id'

uid=1000(dev) gid=1000(dev) groups=1000(dev),65534(nogroup),65534(nogroup),65534(nogroup),65534(nogroup),65534(nogroup),65534(nogroup),65534(nogroup),65534(nogroup)

The supplementary groups collapsed to 65534 (nogroup), which is the overflow ID: the value the kernel substitutes when a UID or GID from outside has no mapping inside. Eight groups the user really belongs to on the host — sudo, docker, adm — became "nobody" the instant the namespace existed. Group membership is a host fact; inside a user namespace it must be re-established by a mapping, and bubblewrap does not map them.

The mapping lives in two files that can each be written once:

cat /proc/self/uid_map
cat /proc/self/gid_map

Each line is inside outside count. A line of 0 1000 1 means "UID 0 inside this namespace is UID 1000 outside". A process that is UID 0 inside such a namespace can chown a file it owns to any inside-UID, but every one of those operations is translated back to a real UID on the host before the kernel checks anything — and the host check is done against the unprivileged outside identity. Root inside a user namespace is root over a translated world, and empty everywhere else.

This is exactly why the distinction in §15.1 matters. A user namespace gives you capabilities. Capabilities are checked against a user namespace, and a capability held in a child namespace is worth nothing against resources owned by the parent.

15.5 When the Distribution Says No: Ubuntu's userns Restriction

Everything above should mean that unshare --user --map-root-user works for any user. On this machine it does not:

unshare --user --map-root-user id

unshare: write failed /proc/self/uid_map: Operation not permitted

That is not a kernel limitation and not a misconfiguration. Ubuntu 24.04 and later ship an AppArmor-based restriction on unprivileged user namespaces, because a decade of kernel CVEs showed that handing every user a namespace in which they hold CAP_SYS_ADMIN widened the attack surface of a great deal of code that had never expected an unprivileged caller. The switch is a sysctl:

sysctl kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns kernel.unprivileged_userns_clone

kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns = 1
kernel.unprivileged_userns_clone = 1

Note that the older knob, kernel.unprivileged_userns_clone, is 1 — permitted. The restriction is the AppArmor one, and it works by requiring the calling program to have an AppArmor profile that explicitly allows userns create. Programs that legitimately need it ship such a profile:

find /etc/apparmor.d -maxdepth 1 \( -name '*userns*' -o -name '*bwrap*' \) -printf '%f\n' | sort

bwrap-userns-restrict
lxc-usernsexec
unprivileged_userns

And the profile says, in its own comments, exactly what the design intends:

# This profile allows almost everything and only exists to allow bwrap
# to work on a system with user namespace restrictions being enforced.
# bwrap is allowed access to user namespaces and capabilities within
# the user namespace, but its children do not have capabilities,
# blocking bwrap from being able to be used to arbitrarily by-pass the
# user namespace restrictions.

That is the whole section in one paragraph written by somebody else: bwrap may create the namespace, but the processes it launches inside do not inherit capabilities — so the namespace is available as an isolation tool and unavailable as a privilege-escalation tool. It is also why every bwrap command in this section worked while the bare unshare did not, and why a script that works on Debian and fails on Ubuntu with Operation not permitted on uid_map is not broken code. Check the sysctl before you debug anything else.

15.6 Building a Root Filesystem: chroot, pivot_root and overlayfs

A container's "own filesystem" is a mount namespace with a different root. Two calls can change the root, and they are not equivalent.

chroot() changes the calling process's idea of /. It is old, it is simple, and it is not a security boundary: a process that retains CAP_SYS_CHROOT can chroot again from a directory it holds open and walk back out, and a process with an open file descriptor to a directory outside the new root can fchdir to it. chroot protects against accident, not against an adversary.

pivot_root() is the real mechanism. It moves the current root mount to a directory and puts a new filesystem at /, so the old root can then be unmounted — after which there is no outside filesystem to escape to, because it is no longer mounted anywhere in the namespace. Every serious runtime uses pivot_root, and the sequence is always: new mount namespace → mount the new root → pivot_root → unmount the old root.

The image itself is almost always an overlay. overlayfs presents a single directory tree assembled from a stack:

lowerdir
One or more read-only layers, searched top-down. These are the image layers, shared between every container using the image — which is why starting ten containers from one image costs one copy of the files.
upperdir
The single writable layer. Every create, modify and delete lands here.
workdir
An empty directory on the same filesystem as upperdir, used for atomic internal operations. Not part of the visible tree.
merged
The mount point where the union appears.

Modifying a file that exists only in a lower layer triggers copy-up: the whole file is copied into upperdir first, then modified. This is why appending one line to a 2 GB file inside a container costs 2 GB of writes and a noticeable pause, and why databases are given a real volume rather than the overlay. Deleting a lower-layer file creates a whiteout entry in the upper layer rather than removing anything — the lower layers are read-only and shared, so removal has to be expressed as an overlay-level annotation.

Docker on this machine reports exactly that driver:

docker info --format '{{.ServerVersion}} {{.Driver}} {{.SecurityOptions}}'

29.1.3 overlayfs [name=apparmor name=seccomp,profile=builtin name=cgroupns]

Read that output as a summary of this entire section: an overlay filesystem for the root, AppArmor as the LSM, a built-in seccomp profile, and a cgroup namespace. Four mechanisms, named separately, because they are separate.

15.7 cgroup v2: One Hierarchy, Explicit Controllers

Namespaces answer "what can it see". Control groups answer "how much can it use".

Version 1 allowed a separate hierarchy per controller, so a process could sit in one place for memory accounting and a different place for CPU accounting; the combinations were unresolvable and the interface accreted inconsistencies. Version 2 has one unified hierarchy: a single tree of directories under /sys/fs/cgroup, in which each process sits at exactly one node.

findmnt -no SOURCE,TARGET,FSTYPE,OPTIONS /sys/fs/cgroup
cat /proc/self/cgroup

cgroup2 /sys/fs/cgroup cgroup2 rw,nosuid,nodev,noexec,relatime,nsdelegate,memory_recursiveprot,memory_hugetlb_accounting

0::/user.slice/user-1000.slice/user@1000.service/tmux-spawn-b49d0797-5bd1-4bb4-ae6d-5788ddab84c6.scope

0:: is the v2 signature — a single entry with no controller list, because there is only one hierarchy. The path is systemd's doing: user.slice for all human sessions, user-1000.slice for this user, and a scope per session (§9.6). Every process on a systemd machine is in a cgroup whether anyone asked for one or not.

Two files govern which controllers are available where:

cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.controllers
cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.subtree_control

cpuset cpu io memory hugetlb pids rdma misc dmem
cpuset cpu io memory pids

cgroup.controllers is what this cgroup has; cgroup.subtree_control is what it hands to its children. A controller must be enabled at every level from the root down to be usable at a leaf — and note that hugetlb, rdma, misc and dmem are available at the root but not passed down, so they are simply not options for anything below.

That leads to the rule that surprises everyone once:

The no-internal-processes rule. A cgroup may contain processes, or it may enable controllers for its children — not both. The root cgroup is exempt.

The reason is that a controller has to arbitrate between siblings, and a process sitting in an interior node has no sibling relationship to the child cgroups it would compete with. So a resource-limited layout is always: interior nodes hold structure and controllers, leaf nodes hold processes. Trying to write a PID into an interior node's cgroup.procs gives EBUSY, and the fix is never to force it — it is to add a leaf.

Delegation is how an unprivileged process gets to manage its own sub-tree. Give a user write permission on a cgroup directory and on its cgroup.procs and cgroup.subtree_control, and they can build and manage children inside it while remaining unable to escape it — the nsdelegate mount option in the output above makes a cgroup namespace boundary also a delegation boundary. This is what systemd does for your user session, and it is why systemd-run --user in the next subsection can impose limits without any privilege at all.

15.8 Limits That Actually Bite

Reading about memory.max teaches nothing. Watching a process die at a number you chose teaches it in one go.

systemd-run --user --scope puts a command in a fresh leaf cgroup with properties you set. Ask for a 64 MB ceiling and no swap, then allocate 8 MB at a time and print progress:

systemd-run --user --scope -q -p MemoryMax=64M -p MemorySwapMax=0 python3 -c "
b=[]
for i in range(40):
    b.append(bytearray(8*1024*1024))
    print(i, flush=True)
"
echo "exit=$?"

0
1
2
3
4
5
6
exit=137

Seven successful allocations — 56 MB of buffers, plus the interpreter's own footprint — and then the process is gone. Exit status 137 is 128 + 9: killed by signal 9, SIGKILL, delivered by the kernel's cgroup OOM killer. Nothing was denied and no exception was raised; Python never got the chance to see a MemoryError, because SIGKILL cannot be caught, blocked or ignored (§7.4).

That is the cgroup failure signature. If a service dies with 137 and no traceback, stop reading the application log and read memory.events:

systemd-run --user --scope -q -p MemoryMax=64M \
  sh -c "cat \"/sys/fs/cgroup\$(cut -d: -f3 /proc/self/cgroup)/memory.max\""

67108864

The main knobs, and the difference between them, which is where most misconfiguration lives:

memory.max
Hard limit. Exceed it and the kernel reclaims; if it cannot reclaim, it kills something in the cgroup.
memory.high
Soft limit. Above it the process is throttled — put to sleep to force reclaim — but not killed. The right knob for "slow down rather than die".
memory.low
Reclaim protection. Memory below this is reclaimed only as a last resort. Protects a cache from a noisy neighbour.
cpu.max
QUOTA PERIOD in microseconds. 50000 100000 is half a CPU; max 100000 is unlimited. A hard ceiling, enforced by throttling at period boundaries.
cpu.weight
Proportional share (default 100). Only matters under contention — a weight of 200 gets twice the CPU of a weight of 100 when both want more than exists, and nothing at all when the machine is idle.
pids.max
Maximum number of processes. pids.max is the cheapest defence against a fork bomb, and the reason a runaway loop takes down one container instead of the host — the failure is a clear Cannot fork rather than an unresponsive machine.
io.max
Per-device bandwidth and IOPS ceilings, keyed by major:minor device number (§8.5). io.max takes a line per device — 8:0 rbps=1048576 wiops=120 — and io.weight is the proportional alternative, with the same idle-capacity argument as cpu.weight.

The distinction between cpu.max and cpu.weight is the one worth internalising: a quota wastes idle capacity, a weight does not. A latency-sensitive service that is given cpu.max will be throttled at period boundaries even on a completely idle machine, producing tail latency out of nowhere; the same service given a high cpu.weight runs at full speed until something else competes. Reach for weights first and quotas only when you are billing, capacity-planning, or enforcing a contract.

Finally, cgroup v2 exposes pressure stall information — the fraction of time work was stalled waiting for a resource:

cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cpu.pressure

PSI answers a question that utilisation cannot. A CPU at 100% might be perfectly healthy (the work fits) or catastrophically overloaded (everything is queued); some avg10= in the pressure file tells you how much time was actually lost to waiting. It is the metric to alert on, and the one to reach for when a dashboard shows everything green and users say the machine is slow.

15.9 Capabilities: Root, Divided

Traditional Unix has one bit of privilege: UID 0 bypasses essentially every kernel check. Capabilities split that bit into individually grantable pieces, so ping can open a raw socket without being able to read /etc/shadow.

The split is live on this machine. ping is not setuid — its mode is rwxr-xr-x — and it works anyway:

ls -l /usr/bin/ping
getcap /usr/bin/ping

-rwxr-xr-x+ 1 root root 159552 Oct 31  2025 /usr/bin/ping
/usr/bin/ping cap_net_raw=ep

That is a file capability: an extended attribute (security.capability, §6.7) on the binary, granting a specific power at execve time. A file capability is strictly better than setuid for a program that needs one thing, and setcap cap_net_bind_service=+ep /usr/local/bin/myserver is how you grant one. cap_net_raw is the right to open raw and packet sockets; =ep means the capability is effective and permitted for the process. Compare that to the setuid approach, still used where a program genuinely needs several powers:

find /usr/bin -perm -4000 -type f | head -12

/usr/bin/fusermount3
/usr/bin/chsh
/usr/bin/sudo.ws
/usr/bin/newgrp
/usr/bin/ntfs-3g
/usr/bin/chfn
/usr/bin/mount
/usr/bin/su
/usr/bin/gpasswd
/usr/bin/passwd
/usr/bin/umount

Every one of those runs as full root when you execute it, and every one of them is therefore a place where a bug is a total compromise. A file capability grants one power; setuid grants all of them. Prefer the former whenever the program needs exactly one thing — a service that binds port 443 wants CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE, not root.

A process carries five capability sets, and the interaction is where the subtlety lives:

Permitted
The superset the process may draw on. It can never be raised beyond what it already has plus what an execve grants.
Effective
What the kernel checks right now. A careful program keeps this empty and raises a capability only around the syscall that needs it.
Inheritable
Preserved across execve — but only for a file that also marks the capability inheritable. Mostly a legacy mechanism.
Bounding
A ceiling on everything above. Dropping a capability from the bounding set is irreversible for that process and every descendant.
Ambient
Added in Linux 4.3 to fix the useless-inheritable problem: an ambient capability survives execve of an unprivileged file, so a shell script wrapper can keep it. An ambient capability must also be present in both the permitted and inheritable sets, and is dropped automatically the moment the process changes UID — which is what makes it safe to hand one to a service with AmbientCapabilities=.

capsh --print shows all of them:

capsh --print

Current: =
Bounding set =cap_chown,cap_dac_override,cap_dac_read_search,cap_fowner,cap_fsetid,cap_kill,cap_setgid,cap_setuid,cap_setpcap,cap_linux_immutable,cap_net_bind_service,cap_net_broadcast,cap_net_admin,cap_net_raw,cap_ipc_lock,cap_ipc_owner,cap_sys_module,cap_sys_rawio,cap_sys_chroot,cap_sys_ptrace,cap_sys_pacct,cap_sys_admin,cap_sys_boot,cap_sys_nice,cap_sys_resource,cap_sys_time,cap_sys_tty_config,cap_mknod,cap_lease,cap_audit_write,cap_audit_control,cap_setfcap,cap_mac_override,cap_mac_admin,cap_syslog,cap_wake_alarm,cap_block_suspend,cap_audit_read,cap_perfmon,cap_bpf,cap_checkpoint_restore
Ambient set =
Current IAB: 
Securebits: 00/0x0/1'b0 (no-new-privs=0)

Current: = is an empty effective and permitted set — an ordinary process. The bounding set is nearly complete, which is the potential privilege a setuid binary could confer, and dropping entries from it is one of the cheapest hardening measures available.

Three capabilities are worth knowing by name because they are effectively root:

  • CAP_SYS_ADMIN — the junk drawer. Mount, pivot_root, setns, keyring manipulation, and dozens more. Granting it is granting root; it exists because nobody split it up in time.
  • CAP_SYS_PTRACE — attach to any process. That means reading its memory, which means its credentials and secrets.
  • CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE — skip all file permission checks. Every file on the machine becomes readable and writable.

And one non-capability that behaves like one: membership of the docker group. The Docker daemon runs as root and its socket accepts "run this image with the host root filesystem bind-mounted". Anyone who can talk to it is root, without any capability appearing anywhere. The Building with Claude course makes the same point about allowUnixSockets and /var/run/docker.sock in §5.7 — a socket that reaches a privileged daemon is the privilege, and no sandbox setting downstream of it matters.

15.10 seccomp: Filtering the Syscall Interface

Capabilities gate privileged operations. seccomp gates the interface itself: it installs a BPF program that inspects the syscall number and arguments before the call executes, and returns a verdict — allow, error, trap, kill.

A seccomp syscall filter is a BPF program attached to the process, evaluated on every syscall entry, and inherited by every child. The point is attack surface. A web server needs perhaps 60 of the kernel's ~400 syscalls. The other 340 are code it will never legitimately call, and every one of them is a place where a kernel bug becomes a privilege escalation. Filtering them costs nothing and removes the reachable surface.

systemd exposes this as SystemCallFilter=, and we can watch it work:

systemd-run --user --pty -q -p SystemCallFilter="@system-service" -p SystemCallFilter="~@clock" \
  /bin/sh -c 'grep -E "^(Seccomp|NoNewPrivs)" /proc/self/status; date -s "2020-01-01"'

NoNewPrivs:	1
Seccomp:	2
Seccomp_filters:	3
Bad system call (core dumped)

Three things in four lines of output. Seccomp: 2 is filter mode (SECCOMP_MODE_FILTER); Seccomp_filters: 3 counts the stacked filters, because filters compose and never loosen. NoNewPrivs: 1 is mandatory — an unprivileged process may only install a seccomp filter after setting no_new_privs, which permanently prevents it and its children from gaining privilege through execve. Without that rule, a filter could be used to lie to a setuid binary about which syscalls succeeded.

And date -s died with "Bad system call" — SIGSYS. Note how different that is from a permission error: settimeofday was not attempted and refused, it was never allowed to reach the kernel, and the process was killed rather than told. Code cannot handle SIGSYS sensibly, which is deliberate: a program calling a syscall its filter forbids is a program doing something its author did not intend.

systemd groups syscalls into sets so you never write a syscall filter by number. @system-service is a curated allow-list suitable for ordinary daemons; the ~ prefix subtracts a set, so ~@clock removes clock-setting on top of it. The useful ones to subtract, in rough order of value: @clock, @module (loading kernel modules), @raw-io, @reboot, @swap, @debug, @mount, @obsolete and @privileged.

seccomp's limits are worth stating plainly. It filters syscall numbers and register arguments, and it cannot dereference pointers — so it can say "no open" but not "no open of /etc/shadow", because the path is behind a pointer that could be changed after the check. Path-based policy is the LSM's job, which is the next subsection. And a filter is inherited and can never be relaxed, so a wrong filter is fixed by restarting the process, not by adjusting it.

15.11 AppArmor, SELinux and the LSM Framework

The Linux Security Module framework is a set of hooks inside the kernel at every security-relevant decision point. A module answers yes or no at each hook, in addition to the ordinary discretionary checks — an LSM can only ever deny something the standard permission model already allowed, never grant.

Two mainstream modules, with genuinely different philosophies:

AppArmor (Ubuntu, SUSE) is path-based. A profile names a program by its executable path and lists the file paths it may read, write and execute. Profiles are readable by a person on their first day, and the failure mode is that two paths to the same inode are two different rules.
SELinux (Fedora, RHEL) is label-based. Every file, process and socket carries a security context stored in an extended attribute, and policy is written between labels. Strictly more powerful, immune to the path-aliasing problem, and considerably harder to author.

aa-status reports what is loaded and enforcing on an AppArmor system. Both modules distinguish enforce mode from complain (AppArmor) or permissive (SELinux) mode, which logs what would have been denied — the only sane way to develop a profile, because a policy written from imagination is always wrong on first contact with the program.

Two habits that save hours. First: when a program fails inexplicably with EACCES on a file whose permissions are obviously correct, suspect the LSM before you suspect yourself, and go and read the audit log — an AppArmor denial appears in the journal as apparmor="DENIED" with the operation, the profile and the path. Second: relabelling matters on SELinux systems. A file moved with mv keeps its old label while a file copied with cp gets a fresh one from the destination directory, which is why "I moved the web content into place and Apache can't read it" is one of the most common SELinux tickets in existence.

15.12 What a Container Actually Is

Assemble the pieces and the definition falls out. A container is a process — an ordinary one, in the host's process table, visible in ps on the host — that has been started with:

Namespaces
Its own mount, PID, network, UTS, IPC and cgroup namespaces — and, for a rootless runtime, a user namespace to obtain them.
A root filesystem
An overlayfs stack of image layers, entered with pivot_root, with a private /proc and /sys mounted inside.
A cgroup
A leaf under the runtime's slice, with memory.max, cpu.max or cpu.weight, and pids.max set.
A reduced capability set
Docker's default keeps fourteen capabilities and drops the rest — CAP_SYS_ADMIN among them.
A seccomp profile
A built-in filter, installed before the container's first instruction runs.
An LSM profile
docker-default under AppArmor, or a targeted SELinux type.

Those are claims that can be checked rather than believed. Run a container and read its own status file:

docker run --rm node:24-slim sh -c 'grep -E "^(CapEff|CapBnd|Seccomp|NoNewPrivs)" /proc/self/status'

CapEff:	00000000a80425fb
CapBnd:	00000000a80425fb
NoNewPrivs:	0
Seccomp:	2
Seccomp_filters:	1

capsh --decode=00000000a80425fb

0x00000000a80425fb=cap_chown,cap_dac_override,cap_fowner,cap_fsetid,cap_kill,cap_setgid,cap_setuid,cap_setpcap,cap_net_bind_service,cap_net_raw,cap_sys_chroot,cap_mknod,cap_audit_write,cap_setfcap

Fourteen capabilities, one seccomp filter — and NoNewPrivs: 0, which is worth pausing on. The default container is not running with no_new_privs, so a setuid binary inside the image can still raise privilege up to that fourteen-capability bounding set. docker run --security-opt no-new-privileges closes that, and it is off by default because turning it on breaks images that legitimately use sudo or setuid helpers. Note also that cap_net_raw is present, which is why ping works in a container with no configuration and why a container can forge ARP replies on its network unless you drop it.

Rootless containers are the variant that closes the UID gap: the runtime creates a user namespace first, so the container's root maps to your unprivileged UID on the host, and a rootless container that is broken out of yields an ordinary user rather than root. Podman defaults to rootless; Docker supports it in a separate mode. The cost is that a rootless runtime cannot do things that genuinely need host privilege — binding a low port without help, or using a network driver that requires CAP_NET_ADMIN on the host.

Nothing else. Which is why "container escape" is never one thing: it is a namespace not created, a capability not dropped, a socket bind-mounted in, or a kernel bug reachable through a syscall the filter allowed. The kernel is shared — that is the trade against a virtual machine, which pays for a second kernel and gets a much smaller escape surface in return.

Two ways to apply all of this without writing a runtime.

bubblewrap takes the pieces as flags, which is why it is used by Flatpak, by Steam, and by Claude Code's sandbox:

bwrap --ro-bind /usr /usr --ro-bind /lib /lib --ro-bind /lib64 /lib64 \
      --proc /proc --dev /dev --tmpfs /tmp \
      --unshare-pid --unshare-net --unshare-uts --hostname sandbox \
      --die-with-parent \
      /bin/sh

Every flag maps to something in this section: read-only bind mounts build a mount namespace containing only what was named, --proc gives the PID namespace a matching view, --unshare-net removes the network entirely, and --die-with-parent ensures the sandbox cannot outlive the supervisor. Note what is absent: there is no allow-list of files, because a bind-mount sandbox is deny-by-default — a path not bound is not there.

systemd unit hardening applies the same mechanisms declaratively to a service, and systemd-analyze security scores the result:

systemd-analyze security systemd-resolved.service

✓ SystemCallFilter=~@swap                     System call allow list defined for service, and @swap is not included
✗ SystemCallFilter=~@resources                System call allow list defined for service, and @resources is included (e.g. ioprio_set is allowed)
✓ SystemCallFilter=~@reboot                   System call allow list defined for service, and @reboot is not included
✓ User=/DynamicUser=                          Service runs under a static non-root user identity
✓ RestrictRealtime=                           Service realtime scheduling access is restricted
✓ CapabilityBoundingSet=~CAP_SYS_TIME         Service processes cannot change the system clock

→ Overall exposure level for systemd-resolved.service: 2.2 OK 🙂

Compare against the machine's worst offenders and the tool's value becomes obvious:

systemd-analyze security

UNIT                                  EXPOSURE PREDICATE HAPPY
ModemManager.service                       6.3 MEDIUM    😐
chrony.service                             3.7 OK        🙂
containerd.service                         9.6 UNSAFE    😨
cron.service                               9.6 UNSAFE    😨
dbus.service                               9.2 UNSAFE    😨

cron.service at 9.6 is not a scandal — cron's job is to run arbitrary commands as arbitrary users, so it cannot be confined without ceasing to be cron. That is the right way to read the score: it measures what a compromise of that service would reach, not how badly written it is. The number is a prompt to ask whether the service really needs what it has. For something you wrote yourself, the highest-value directives, roughly in order of benefit per line, are NoNewPrivileges=yes, PrivateTmp=yes, ProtectSystem=strict, ProtectHome=yes, CapabilityBoundingSet=, SystemCallFilter=@system-service, RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_INET AF_INET6 AF_UNIX, and DynamicUser=yes.

Those directives are namespaces (PrivateTmp, ProtectHome), capabilities (CapabilityBoundingSet), seccomp (SystemCallFilter) and no_new_privs, spelled as configuration. You have already met all of them in this section; systemd is just a very good front end.

Exercises

Exercise 15.1 — Read the failure. Four processes fail in four ways. For each, name the mechanism responsible and the single command you would run first.

  1. A service exits with status 137 and its log ends mid-request.
  2. A program prints Bad system call and dumps core.
  3. ip addr inside a shell shows only lo, and curl reports "Network is unreachable".
  4. A daemon fails with Operation not permitted on bind() to port 443, running as a non-root user.
Solution

1 — cgroup, memory. 137 is 128 + 9: SIGKILL. The application did not choose to exit and could not have logged its own death, which is why the log stops mid-request rather than at a clean boundary. First command:

systemctl show -p MemoryMax -p MemoryHigh <unit>

then read memory.events in the unit's cgroup for the oom_kill counter. If MemoryMax is infinity the kill came from the system OOM killer instead, and the journal will carry the kernel's Out of memory: Killed process line with the scoring.

2 — seccomp. Bad system call is the shell reporting SIGSYS. First command:

grep -E '^(Seccomp|NoNewPrivs)' /proc/<pid>/status

on a surviving process of the same service, or systemctl show -p SystemCallFilter <unit>. A Seccomp: value of 2 confirms a filter is installed. The usual cause is a library update that started using a newer syscall — statx in place of stat, or clone3 in place of clone — against a filter written years earlier.

3 — namespace. Nothing was denied; there is no network in this network namespace. curl's "Network is unreachable" is a routing failure, not a policy failure. First command:

lsns -t net

and compare the namespace inode against readlink /proc/1/ns/net. If they differ, you are inside a container or an unshared shell, and the fix is a veth pair or a proxy, not a firewall rule.

4 — capability. Ports below 1024 require CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE. EPERM from bind() is the kernel checking and refusing — the syscall ran. First command:

getcap /path/to/binary

The fixes, best first: AmbientCapabilities=CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE in the unit file; or a setcap on the binary; or net.ipv4.ip_unprivileged_port_start; or put a reverse proxy in front. Running the daemon as root to fix a one-capability problem is the option that trades a small failure for a large one.

Exercise 15.2 — The half-built sandbox. A colleague isolates a build step like this and reports that it "works, but the build can still see every process on the machine":

bwrap --ro-bind / / --unshare-pid --unshare-uts --hostname build /bin/sh -c 'make install'

Explain precisely why, and give the corrected command. Then say what is still wrong with it as a sandbox even after your fix.

Solution

Why: the PID namespace was created, but /proc was not. --ro-bind / / binds the host's entire root — including the host's procfs mount — into the sandbox, and procfs renders processes relative to the PID namespace of the mount, not of the reader. The build genuinely is in a new PID namespace (its shell is PID 2) and genuinely can read the host's process list, exactly as §15.3 demonstrated. The namespace changed the numbering; the mount decided the view.

The fix is one flag:

bwrap --ro-bind / / --unshare-pid --unshare-uts --hostname build \
      --proc /proc /bin/sh -c 'make install'

What is still wrong, in order of severity:

  • --ro-bind / / binds the whole filesystem. Read-only, but that includes ~/.ssh, ~/.aws, every environment secret on disk, and the source of every other project on the machine. A sandbox that a build step can read secrets out of is a sandbox against accidents, not against a malicious dependency — and make install runs arbitrary code from the project. Bind only what the build needs.
  • The network is untouched. make install can reach anything the host can. Add --unshare-net, or accept that dependency fetching means the sandbox has an egress path and treat that path as the security boundary.
  • It writes to the host. install writes outside the source tree by definition, and nothing here made the destination read-only or redirected it. --tmpfs /usr/local or a proper --bind of a staging directory would.
  • No --die-with-parent. If the supervisor is killed, the build keeps running unattached.
  • No IPC namespace and no cgroup limits. A runaway build competes with everything on the machine for memory and CPU.

The general lesson: a sandbox is a list of things you removed. Auditing one means asking what is still reachable, never what was blocked.

Exercise 15.3 — Choose the knob. A latency-sensitive API and a nightly batch job share one 8-core machine. The batch job must never starve the API, but the machine must not sit idle when the API is quiet. Write the cgroup configuration, and justify each choice against the alternative you rejected.

Solution 
# api.service
[Service]
CPUWeight=1000
MemoryHigh=6G
MemoryMax=8G
IOWeight=1000

# batch.service
[Service]
CPUWeight=10
MemoryHigh=2G
MemoryMax=4G
IOWeight=10
Nice=10

CPUWeight, not CPUQuota. A quota (cpu.max) throttles at every period boundary whether or not anything else wants the CPU, so a batch job capped at 2 cores leaves 6 idle at 3 a.m. and the batch still takes all night. Weights only take effect under contention: with 1000 against 10, the API gets roughly 99% of contended CPU, and the batch job gets the entire machine whenever the API is quiet. That is exactly the stated requirement, and a quota cannot express it.

MemoryHigh below MemoryMax on both. MemoryHigh throttles and forces reclaim; MemoryMax kills. Setting only MemoryMax means the first symptom is a dead process. With MemoryHigh at 6G, the API is slowed and reclaimed against at 6G and only killed if it reaches 8G, which converts a hard failure into a visible degradation you can alert on.

IOWeight matters more than people expect. A batch job reading a large dataset will saturate the device queue and add tens of milliseconds to every API disk read, and no amount of CPU weighting touches that. Note the honest caveat: io.weight needs a controller that can arbitrate — it is fully effective on bfq and on NVMe with io.cost configured, and weaker elsewhere. Verify with cat /sys/fs/cgroup/io.pressure under load rather than assuming.

Nice=10 as well as the weight, because niceness affects scheduling within a cgroup and costs nothing.

What is deliberately absent: CPUAffinity pinning. Reserving cores for the API would help worst-case latency and would break the "machine must not sit idle" requirement in the other direction — the API's reserved cores would idle whenever it was quiet. Pinning is for when jitter matters more than throughput, which is a different requirement from this one.

Exercise 15.4 — Prove the boundary. You are told a service runs "in a container, so it's isolated". Write the commands, runnable from the host, that determine (a) whether it is in a separate network namespace, (b) which capabilities it actually holds, (c) whether a seccomp filter is installed, and (d) what its memory ceiling is. Then say which single answer would worry you most.

Solution

With the container's host-side PID in $PID:

readlink /proc/"$PID"/ns/net
readlink /proc/1/ns/net
grep -E '^(CapEff|CapBnd|NoNewPrivs|Seccomp)' /proc/"$PID"/status
capsh --decode="$(grep CapEff /proc/"$PID"/status | cut -f2)"
cat /sys/fs/cgroup"$(cut -d: -f3 /proc/"$PID"/cgroup)"/memory.max

(a) The two readlink outputs differ if and only if the process is in its own network namespace. Comparing against PID 1 is the reliable form, because PID 1 is by definition in the initial namespaces.

(b) CapEff is a hex bitmask; capsh --decode= turns it into names. CapBnd matters just as much — a small effective set with a full bounding set means privilege can still be regained through a setuid binary unless NoNewPrivs is 1.

(c) Seccomp: 2 means a filter is installed; 0 means none. Seccomp_filters counts them.

(d) memory.max reading max means no limit — the container can take the whole machine.

The answer that should worry you most is CapEff containing cap_sys_admin. A missing memory limit is a reliability problem and a missing seccomp filter widens the attack surface, but CAP_SYS_ADMIN includes mount, setns and pivot_root — the operations that construct and enter namespaces. A process holding it can, in the general case, unmake its own isolation, at which point every other answer stops being a guarantee. The second-worst answer is the network namespace matching PID 1's: it means "container" was doing much less work in that sentence than whoever said it believed.

SECTION 16

Capstone: Harden and Sandbox a Real Service

Everything in this course converges on one recurring task: you have a program, and it must run on a machine as a supervised, logged, resource-limited, confined service that a colleague can operate without asking you. This section does that end to end, on a real service, on this machine — and it goes wrong twice on the way, because both failures are ones you will meet and neither is documented anywhere you would think to look.

The finding the whole section builds to is worth stating up front, because it changes how you should treat every hardening guide you will ever read: systemd-analyze security scores your configuration, not its effect. A unit can go from 9.8 UNSAFE to 1.8 OK while several of its protections are being silently ignored by the kernel. The last third of this section is how to tell the difference.

16.1 The Brief

A small internal HTTP service — call it statusd — must run on a shared Linux host. The requirements are the ordinary ones:

Supervised
Starts at boot, restarts on crash, does not restart forever if it is genuinely broken (§9.7).
Logged
Output in the journal, filterable by unit, with no log file to rotate (§11.4).
Bounded
Cannot take the machine's memory or fork bomb it (§15.8).
Confined
A compromise of it should not reach the rest of the filesystem, the kernel, or the network beyond what it needs (§15).
Operable
Someone else can find out whether it is healthy, why it failed, and what it is allowed to do — from the machine, with no documentation.

The service itself is deliberately unremarkable: it binds 127.0.0.1:8099, answers GET / with JSON, and writes its access log to standard error.

import http.server, json, os, socketserver, sys

class H(http.server.BaseHTTPRequestHandler):
    def do_GET(self):
        body = json.dumps({"ok": True, "pid": os.getpid(), "uid": os.getuid()}).encode()
        self.send_response(200)
        self.send_header("Content-Type", "application/json")
        self.send_header("Content-Length", str(len(body)))
        self.end_headers()
        self.wfile.write(body)

    def log_message(self, fmt, *args):
        sys.stderr.write("%s - %s\n" % (self.address_string(), fmt % args))

with socketserver.TCPServer(("127.0.0.1", 8099), H) as s:
    sys.stderr.write("statusd listening on 127.0.0.1:8099\n")
    sys.stderr.flush()
    s.serve_forever()

Two decisions in that file are the ones that matter operationally, and both come from earlier sections. It binds loopback, so it is unreachable from the network regardless of any firewall (§13.4). And it logs to stderr rather than to a file, so journald captures it with the unit name attached and there is nothing to rotate (§11.6).

16.2 The Naive Unit, and What It Scores

[Unit]
Description=Status API

[Service]
ExecStart=/usr/bin/python3 /opt/statusd/statusd.py
Restart=on-failure

That is what most services ship with, and it works:

systemctl --user daemon-reload
systemctl --user start statusd
systemctl --user is-active statusd
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:8099/

active
{"ok": true, "pid": 3402, "uid": 1000}

Now score it (§15.12):

systemd-analyze --user security statusd.service

✗ UMask=                     Files created by service are world-readable by default        0.1

→ Overall exposure level for statusd.service: 9.8 UNSAFE 😨

9.8 out of 10. Nothing about the service is unusual; it is simply that a unit with no hardening directives inherits every power the manager has, and the tool is reporting what a compromise of it would reach. That is the right way to read the number (§15.12) — it measures blast radius, not code quality.

16.3 Hardening It

The directives, grouped by what each one actually removes. This is the set worth keeping as a starting template for any service you write:

[Service]
ExecStart=/usr/bin/python3 /opt/statusd/statusd.py
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5
StartLimitIntervalSec=60
StartLimitBurst=3

# Identity — no privilege, and no way to gain any
DynamicUser=yes
NoNewPrivileges=yes
CapabilityBoundingSet=
UMask=0077

# Filesystem — a private /tmp, an unreachable /home, a read-only /
PrivateTmp=yes
PrivateDevices=yes
ProtectSystem=strict
ProtectHome=yes
ProtectProc=invisible
ReadOnlyPaths=/
InaccessiblePaths=/boot

# Kernel — nothing that touches the machine's own configuration
ProtectKernelTunables=yes
ProtectKernelModules=yes
ProtectKernelLogs=yes
ProtectControlGroups=yes
ProtectClock=yes
ProtectHostname=yes
RestrictNamespaces=yes
RestrictRealtime=yes
RestrictSUIDSGID=yes
LockPersonality=yes
MemoryDenyWriteExecute=yes

# Syscalls and sockets — the smallest interface the service can work through
SystemCallArchitectures=native
SystemCallFilter=@system-service
SystemCallFilter=~@privileged @resources
RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_INET AF_INET6

# Resources — bounded, so a bug is a service incident and not a host incident
MemoryMax=256M
CPUWeight=100
TasksMax=32

systemd-analyze --user security statusd.service

✓ UMask=                     Files created by service are accessible only by service's own user by default

→ Overall exposure level for statusd.service: 1.8 OK 🙂

9.8 to 1.8. Every line is doing something nameable: ProtectSystem=strict mounts the entire filesystem read-only except /dev, /proc and /sys; RestrictAddressFamilies means a compromised process cannot open a raw socket or a Unix socket; SystemCallFilter installs the seccomp-bpf filter of §15.10; MemoryDenyWriteExecute blocks the write-then-execute pattern most exploit payloads need; and DynamicUser=yes allocates a UID for the service's lifetime and discards it afterwards (§6.9).

At this point most hardening guides stop. Two things are about to go wrong.

16.4 Failure One: the Service Stops Starting

systemctl --user restart statusd
systemctl --user is-active statusd

activating

Not active — activating, forever, because it is crash-looping. The journal says exactly why:

journalctl --user -u statusd -n 6 --no-pager -o cat

statusd.service: ProtectHostname=yes is configured, but UTS namespace setup is prohibited (container manager?), ignoring namespace setup.
statusd.service: Failed to drop capabilities: Operation not permitted
statusd.service: Failed at step CAPABILITIES spawning /usr/bin/python3: Operation not permitted
statusd.service: Main process exited, code=exited, status=218/CAPABILITIES
statusd.service: Failed with result 'exit-code'.

status=218/CAPABILITIES is systemd telling you which setup step failed before the program ever ran. The numbered exit statuses above 200 are all of this kind, and they are the most useful diagnostic systemd produces: the failure is in the unit, not in the code.

The cause is specific and worth knowing. A --user manager runs as your unprivileged user, and dropping the capability bounding set requires CAP_SETPCAP, which it does not have. So CapabilityBoundingSet= fails outright — and so does anything that implies a bounding-set change. PrivateDevices=yes implies removing CAP_MKNOD; ProtectKernelModules=yes implies removing CAP_SYS_MODULE; ProtectClock=yes implies removing CAP_SYS_TIME. Each of them fails the same way, and the error names none of them.

Bisecting by removing directives one at a time — which is the only method that works here — leaves a unit that starts:

systemctl --user restart statusd
systemctl --user is-active statusd
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:8099/

active
{"ok": true, "pid": 3566, "uid": 1000}

In a system unit, run by PID 1, none of this arises — the system manager has every capability and can drop them all. This whole failure is a property of user units, and the reason to meet it here is that "test it as a user unit first" is common advice and this is what it costs.

16.5 Failure Two: the Protections That Do Nothing

The unit now starts and scores well. Verify that the protections are real, rather than trusting the score.

The decisive test for ProtectHome=yes is to try writing somewhere in your own home directory — a path ordinary permissions allow, so any refusal must come from the sandbox:

systemd-run --user -q --pty /bin/sh -c "touch ~/.cache/probe.txt && echo WROTE ok"
systemd-run --user -q --pty -p ProtectHome=yes /bin/sh -c "touch ~/.cache/probe.txt && echo WROTE ok"

WROTE ok
WROTE ok

Identical. ProtectHome=yes did nothing. And it did nothing silently — unlike ProtectHostname, which at least logged "ignoring namespace setup", this produced no warning in the journal at all.

The reason is one command away:

systemd-run --user -q --pty -p ProtectHome=yes /bin/sh -c 'readlink /proc/self/ns/mnt'
readlink /proc/self/ns/mnt

mnt:[4026531832]
mnt:[4026531832]

The same mount namespace as the host (§15.2). Every filesystem-protection directive — ProtectHome, ProtectSystem, PrivateTmp, ReadOnlyPaths, InaccessiblePaths — is implemented by building a private mount namespace and mounting things differently inside it. No mount namespace, no protection.

And the reason there is no mount namespace is §15.5: this is Ubuntu with kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns=1, so an unprivileged process — which is what a --user manager is — cannot create the user namespace it would need to create a mount namespace. The restriction that makes the machine safer makes this hardening inert.

This is the lesson the capstone exists for. The score went from 9.8 to 1.8 on the strength of directives that were, in this context, decorative. A tool that reads configuration cannot tell you what the kernel did with it, and neither can a code review. Only a probe can.

16.6 What Did Take Effect

The honest way to finish is to establish which protections are real here, one by one, with a test that would fail if they were not.

Seccomp — real:

systemd-run --user -q --pty -p SystemCallFilter=@system-service -p SystemCallFilter='~@clock' \
  /bin/sh -c 'date -s "2020-01-01"'

Bad system call (core dumped)

SIGSYS, exactly as in §15.10. Installing a seccomp filter needs only no_new_privs, which any process may set for itself, so it works unprivileged.

Task limits — real:

systemd-run --user -q --pty -p TasksMax=5 /bin/sh -c 'for i in 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8; do sleep 5 & done; wait'

/bin/sh: 0: Cannot fork

The pids controller refused the sixth process. cgroup limits are enforced by a delegated sub-tree the user manager already owns (§15.7), so they need no privilege either.

Memory limits — real, and visible in the live unit:

systemctl --user show statusd -p MemoryMax -p TasksMax -p MemoryCurrent

MemoryCurrent=9707520
MemoryMax=268435456
TasksMax=32

Seccomp and no-new-privileges on the running service — real:

grep -E '^(NoNewPrivs|Seccomp|Seccomp_filters)' /proc/"$MAINPID"/status

NoNewPrivs:	1
Seccomp:	2
Seccomp_filters:	4

So the split is clean, and it generalises:

Works unprivileged — seccomp filters, no_new_privs, and every cgroup limit (MemoryMax, TasksMax, CPUWeight, IOWeight). These need only powers a process already has over itself or over its delegated cgroup sub-tree.
Needs a system unit — everything built on namespaces (ProtectHome, ProtectSystem, PrivateTmp, ReadOnlyPaths, ProtectProc) and everything built on capabilities (CapabilityBoundingSet, PrivateDevices, ProtectClock, ProtectKernelModules). Some fail loudly, some silently.

16.7 The Same Confinement, Built by Hand

There is one more way to get the filesystem isolation on this machine without being root, and it is the argument of §15.5 made concrete. bubblewrap ships an AppArmor profile that permits it to create user namespaces, so it can do unprivileged what the user manager cannot:

bwrap --ro-bind /usr /usr --ro-bind /lib /lib --ro-bind /lib64 /lib64 \
      --ro-bind /etc/ld.so.cache /etc/ld.so.cache \
      --ro-bind /opt/statusd/statusd.py /app/statusd.py \
      --proc /proc --dev /dev --tmpfs /tmp \
      --unshare-pid --unshare-uts --unshare-ipc --hostname statusd --die-with-parent \
      /usr/bin/python3 -c 'import os; print("pid", os.getpid()); print("hostname", os.uname().nodename); print("home visible:", os.path.exists(os.path.expanduser("~"))); print("procs:", len([d for d in os.listdir("/proc") if d.isdigit()]))'

pid 2
hostname statusd
home visible: False
procs: 2

Four claims, all verified in one run and all of them things the "1.8 OK" unit did not actually achieve. PID 2 in its own PID namespace. Its own hostname. $HOME genuinely not present — not merely unwritable, absent, because nothing bound it in. And two processes visible in /proc, because --proc mounted a fresh procfs to match the PID namespace (§15.3).

Note what the command is: a list of things that were let in. Nothing was denied; a bind-mount sandbox is deny-by-default, and everything reachable is named on the command line. That property is why auditing one means reading a single line.

16.8 The Runbook

The last requirement was operable, and it is the one most often skipped. Everything below is answerable from the machine with no documentation, which is the standard to aim for.

Is it healthy?
systemctl status statusd — state, uptime, main PID, and the cgroup tree listing every process the unit owns (§9.8).
Why did it fail?
journalctl -u statusd -n 50; -b -1 for the previous boot. A status=2xx/NAME line means the unit, not the code (§16.4).
Was it killed?
Exit 137 is SIGKILL — read memory.events for oom_kill (§15.8). Exit 143 is SIGTERM, which is a normal stop.
What is it allowed to do?
systemd-analyze security statusd.service for the configuration, and the probes of §16.5–16.6 for the effect.
What is it using?
systemctl show statusd -p MemoryCurrent -p TasksCurrent, and cat /sys/fs/cgroup/<path>/memory.pressure for stall time (§15.8).
Is it reachable?
ss -tlnp | grep 8099 — and the bind address is the security answer, not the firewall (§13.4).
What does it have open?
ls -l /proc/$MAINPID/fd, counted against /proc/$MAINPID/limits (§8.2).
What is it waiting on?
ps -o stat,wchan -p $MAINPID, then strace -p $MAINPID (§7.3, §8.10).

And the deployment checklist, in the order the failures actually happen:

  1. systemd-analyze verify the unit file before installing it — it catches unknown directives and unresolvable dependencies without running anything.
  2. Start it and confirm active, not activating. A crash loop looks like a slow start for the first minute.
  3. journalctl -u immediately, even on success. Warnings about ignored directives appear here and nowhere else.
  4. Probe each protection you are relying on. Not the score — the effect.
  5. Confirm the limits are set, with systemctl show, and that the values are what you wrote (a typo in MemoryMax=256 without a unit means 256 bytes).
  6. Test the restart path: systemctl kill -s SIGKILL statusd and check it comes back, then check StartLimitBurst stops it if it cannot.
  7. Test the stop path: time a systemctl stop. Exactly TimeoutStopSec means the service is ignoring SIGTERM (§7.11).

Exercises

Exercise 16.1 — Audit a hardened unit. A colleague submits this unit and cites a systemd-analyze security score of 2.1. What do you check before approving it, and what would you expect to find?

[Service]
User=appsvc
ExecStart=/opt/app/bin/server --config /etc/app/config.yml
Restart=always
ProtectSystem=strict
ProtectHome=yes
PrivateTmp=yes
NoNewPrivileges=yes
SystemCallFilter=@system-service
CapabilityBoundingSet=CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE
AmbientCapabilities=CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE
ReadWritePaths=/var/lib/app
MemoryMax=2G
Solution

The score is not the review. Six things to check, roughly in order of how likely each is to be wrong:

1 — Does it start, and does it stay up? ProtectSystem=strict makes the whole filesystem read-only apart from /dev, /proc, /sys and whatever ReadWritePaths names. If the application writes anywhere else — a cache in /var/cache, a PID file in /run, a socket, a temporary file outside /tmp — it fails at runtime, often only under load. RuntimeDirectory=app and StateDirectory=app are the systemd-native way to get those written for you with the right ownership.

2 — Do the protections take effect? This is §16.5. As a system unit they will, since PID 1 can create namespaces — but verify rather than assume: readlink /proc/$MAINPID/ns/mnt against readlink /proc/1/ns/mnt, and grep Seccomp /proc/$MAINPID/status.

3 — Restart=always with no start limit. A permanently broken service restarts forever, filling the journal and hiding the original error. Add RestartSec=, StartLimitIntervalSec= and StartLimitBurst= (§9.7).

4 — Is CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE needed at all? It is only for binding below port 1024. If the service listens on 8080 behind a proxy, both capability lines should go and the bounding set should be empty. If it does bind 443 directly, this is exactly right — and better than running as root (§15.9).

5 — ReadWritePaths=/var/lib/app and ownership. The directory must exist and be owned by appsvc before first start. StateDirectory=app creates it with the correct ownership on every start and is the more robust form.

6 — Missing pieces. No RestrictAddressFamilies= (so raw and Unix sockets are still available), no MemoryDenyWriteExecute=, no LockPersonality=, no TasksMax= (so a fork bomb is still a host incident), no UMask=0077, and no SystemCallFilter=~@privileged. Each is one line.

And the question that outranks all of it: does the service need what it has? User=appsvc with a shell of /usr/sbin/nologin and ownership of nothing but /var/lib/app limits the damage far more than any of the directives above (§6.9).

Exercise 16.2 — The silent no-op. Design a general procedure for verifying that a sandboxing directive is doing something. Apply it to PrivateTmp=yes, ProtectProc=invisible and RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_INET.

Solution

The procedure: for each directive, find an operation that (a) succeeds without it and (b) must fail with it, where the only thing that could deny it is the directive — never something ordinary permissions would refuse anyway. Then run both. That last condition is the one that makes the test valid; §16.5 shows why testing ProtectSystem by writing to /etc as a non-root user proves nothing.

PrivateTmp=yes. Write a file to /tmp from outside, then look for it from inside:

touch /tmp/outside-probe
systemd-run --user -q --pty -p PrivateTmp=yes /bin/sh -c 'ls /tmp/outside-probe'

Effective → "No such file or directory", because the unit has its own /tmp. Ineffective → the file is listed. Cross-check with readlink /proc/self/ns/mnt against the host's; the same inode means no mount namespace and therefore no private anything.

ProtectProc=invisible. Count other users' processes from inside:

systemd-run --user -q --pty -p ProtectProc=invisible /bin/sh -c 'ls /proc | grep -c "^[0-9]"'

Effective → only the unit's own processes. Ineffective → the host's full count. This is exactly the experiment of §15.3, and the same caveat applies: it depends on a fresh /proc in a mount namespace, so it fails in the same conditions PrivateTmp does.

RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_INET. Attempt a socket in an excluded family:

systemd-run --user -q --pty -p RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_INET \
  /usr/bin/python3 -c 'import socket; socket.socket(socket.AF_UNIX, socket.SOCK_STREAM); print("UNIX socket allowed")'

Effective → the call fails with EAFNOSUPPORT. Ineffective → it prints. This one is implemented with seccomp rather than namespaces, so on this machine it works even in a user unit — which is itself the point: the directives fail in groups by mechanism, not individually, and knowing which mechanism each uses tells you which probe to write and which context it needs.

Generalise: namespace-based directives are verified by comparing /proc/PID/ns/* and by looking for something that should be absent. Seccomp-based ones are verified by making the forbidden call and expecting SIGSYS or an errno. Capability-based ones are verified with capsh --decode on CapEff from /proc/PID/status. Three probes cover every directive there is.

Exercise 16.3 — From unit to container. The service must now run in a container. Which of §16.3's directives have container equivalents, which are supplied by the runtime already, and which are lost? Write the docker run line that comes closest.

Solution 
docker run -d --name statusd \
  --read-only --tmpfs /tmp:rw,noexec,nosuid,size=64m \
  --cap-drop ALL \
  --security-opt no-new-privileges \
  --memory 256m --pids-limit 32 --cpu-shares 1024 \
  --user 65534:65534 \
  --network none \
  statusd:latest

Direct equivalents:

ProtectSystem=strict
--read-only
PrivateTmp=yes

--tmpfs /tmp (and add noexec,nosuid, §10.7)

CapabilityBoundingSet=

--cap-drop ALL

NoNewPrivileges=yes

--security-opt no-new-privileges — not the default (§15.12)

MemoryMax=, TasksMax=

--memory, --pids-limit

DynamicUser=yes

--user 65534:65534, or a USER line in the Dockerfile

SystemCallFilter=

--security-opt seccomp=profile.json — a default profile is already applied

Supplied by the runtime already: ProtectHome, ProtectProc, ProtectKernelLogs and ProtectControlGroups are all implied by the container having its own mount and PID namespaces with its own root filesystem — there is no /home to protect and no host /proc to hide. ProtectHostname comes from the UTS namespace.

Lost or weakened:

  • ProtectKernelTunables and ProtectKernelModules — /proc/sys is already read-only in a default container, but the guarantee is the runtime's rather than yours, and it changes with --privileged or a -v /proc:/proc mount that someone adds later.
  • RestrictAddressFamilies has no direct flag; it must go in a custom seccomp profile. --network none is the blunter and stronger version when the service does not need the network at all.
  • MemoryDenyWriteExecute and LockPersonality need a custom seccomp profile too.
  • Journald integration. Container logs go to the runtime's logging driver, so journalctl -u no longer applies and the retention and rotation story is a different one.
  • Restart=on-failure with a start limit becomes --restart on-failure:3, which is close but does not have the interval semantics.

The one genuine loss is the supervision contract. systemd knows when a service is ready (Type=notify, §9.7); a container is "started" when the process starts. Health checks (--health-cmd, or a readiness probe in Kubernetes) are the replacement, and they are polling rather than notification — which is why a container orchestrator sends traffic to a not-yet-ready instance in a way systemd does not.

Exercise 16.4 — The incident. At 03:14 an alert fires: statusd is not responding. You have SSH access and nothing else. Write the sequence of commands, and say what each possible result would mean.

Solution 
systemctl status statusd
journalctl -u statusd -n 100 --no-pager
ss -tlnp | grep 8099
curl -sv --max-time 5 http://127.0.0.1:8099/

Those four in that order, because each eliminates a layer, and then the branches:

inactive (dead) — it stopped. The journal says whether it exited cleanly (someone or something stopped it) or failed. systemctl start restores service; find out who stopped it from the journal before assuming it was a crash.

failed, exit 137 — SIGKILL. Check the memory limit and the cgroup's oom_kill counter (§15.8). If MemoryMax is set and the service grew into it, the limit did its job and the leak is the incident. If the system OOM killer fired instead, journalctl -k -p err has the kernel's scoring and something else on the machine is the cause.

failed, status=2xx/SOMETHING — a unit-setup failure, not a code failure (§16.4). Almost always follows a change to the unit or to the filesystem it expects. systemd-analyze verify and the journal name the step.

activating with a rising restart counter — a crash loop. StartLimitBurst should have stopped it; if it did not, that is the second finding. Read the first failure in the journal, not the latest — they are all the same and the first has the context.

active (running) but curl times out — the process is alive and not serving. Now go to §7: ps -o stat,wchan -p $MAINPID for state D (blocked on I/O, §7.3), ls /proc/$MAINPID/fd | wc -l against its limit for a descriptor leak, ss -tn state established for connections it never closed, and strace -p $MAINPID for what it is doing right now. A Recv-Q that stays non-zero on the listening socket means it is not calling accept() (§13.4).

active and curl works from the host — the service is fine and the alert came from elsewhere. Check what the alert actually probes: the network path, DNS, a proxy, or the monitoring host's own clock (§11, Exercise 11.2).

Before restarting anything, capture state: systemctl status, the last 200 journal lines, ss -tanp, and /proc/$MAINPID/status. A restart destroys every piece of evidence, and "it came back after a restart" is where most incident investigations stop being possible.

You started this course able to use a Linux machine. You can now open it up: name the mechanism behind each behaviour, pick the one tool that observes it, and tell the difference between a thing that is forbidden, a thing that is absent, and a thing there was not enough of. That is a different skill from knowing commands, and it is the one that holds when the command you know does not work.

What you can now do

  1. Reason about the kernel boundary. What a syscall costs, why buffering exists and how it produces an "empty" log file, why the syscall list is the attack surface, and how strace -f -e trace=%file turns a useless error message into a path and an errno.
  2. Read the file abstraction exactly. Inodes against names, so hard links, unlink, deleted-but-open files, mv's asymmetry across filesystems and /proc's zero-byte files all stop being separate facts.
  3. Quote correctly, and know why. Eight expansion stages in a fixed order, word splitting after parameter expansion, and printf '[%s]\n' $var as the way to check how many arguments a command actually received.
  4. Compose streams. Three descriptors, left-to-right redirection, 2>&1 as a copy of a current destination, pipefail and PIPESTATUS, and the backup script that reported success for a year.
  5. Process text without writing a program. sort | uniq -c | sort -rn as the shape of most log questions, BRE against ERE and why grep 'a|b' silently finds nothing, sed addresses, and awk's associative arrays as a one-line GROUP BY.
  6. Predict a permission decision. First-match not best-match across the three triads, x as traverse on a directory, umask as a mask, setgid directories for shared work, and the group list that is fixed at login.
  7. Diagnose a process. STAT before anything else, D as the state that ignores SIGKILL, zombies as a parent's bug, SIGTERM against SIGKILL as the whole of graceful shutdown, and 137 as the exit status that means the kernel did it.
  8. Read the machine's own state as files. /proc/PID/fd for a descriptor leak, MemAvailable rather than MemFree, /sys for device attributes, and the major:minor numbers that a cgroup I/O limit is written against.
  9. Explain a boot. Firmware to bootloader to initramfs to PID 1, Wants= against After= as the difference between what and when, Type=notify as the only honest readiness signal, and critical-chain rather than blame.
  10. Operate storage. The stack from block device to mount point, GPT against MBR, ext4 against XFS and which one cannot shrink, and the three different conditions that all say "no space left on device".
  11. Find out what a machine is doing. journald's filters, the difference between a rate and a total, %util as a metric that lies on NVMe, and pressure stall information as the thing to alert on.
  12. Reason about networking from the host's side. Longest-prefix routing, ip route get as the command that ends arguments, the bind address as the security answer, and the fact that dig and getent hosts can legitimately disagree.
  13. Confine a workload deliberately. Eight namespaces, cgroup v2 controllers and the no-internal-processes rule, five capability sets, seccomp's SIGSYS, and the reason bwrap --unshare-pid alone leaves the host's whole process list visible.

Three things worth carrying

A namespace is not a permission, and neither is a limit. The three axes of §15 — what it can see, how much it can use, what it may do — fail differently and are fixed differently. A missing file is a namespace question; EPERM is a capability question; SIGKILL with no traceback is a cgroup question; SIGSYS is a seccomp question. Reading the failure shape first is worth more than knowing any individual flag, and it is the fastest way to stop debugging the wrong layer.

Utilisation is not saturation. MemFree, %util, %idle and "disk 52% full" are all numbers whose bad value is routinely healthy and whose healthy value is routinely bad. What users feel is waiting — queue depth, await, pressure stall time, r and b in vmstat. Every performance investigation in this course turned on that distinction, and the dashboards that show the first set are the ones that let an incident run for an hour before anyone believes it is real.

Verify the effect, not the configuration. The capstone's hardened unit scored 1.8 out of 10 while several of its protections were being silently ignored by the kernel — no error, no warning, a good score, and no isolation. Configuration files, review checklists and scoring tools all read what you asked for. Only a probe reads what you got. That habit generalises well past Linux: the question is never "did I set it", it is "what happens if I try".

Where to go next

The obvious continuations, and what each adds:

  • Advanced Shell Scripting takes §2 and §4 much further — arrays, traps, getopts, strict mode, and ShellCheck as a gate rather than a suggestion.
  • Git is the other tool you will use every day on every machine, and its object model is a filesystem argument you are now equipped to read.
  • DevOps & Deployment builds on §9, §11 and §15: pipelines, images, orchestration, and observability at fleet scale.
  • Web Security takes §6 and §15's confinement thinking up to the application.

And the habit that matters more than any of them: when something on a machine surprises you, find the mechanism rather than the workaround. There is one, it is documented, and — because of the file abstraction — you can almost always cat it.
